#6852. 9 Interior Trends Designers Are Officially Ditching in 2027

Some of this year’s biggest design moments may not age well.

The end of the year is a great time to look back and reflect. When it comes to home design, there’s a lot to cover, from new trends to furniture collaboration drops to specific internet-breaking interiors. One of our favorite things to do around this time is figure out what’s coming next in terms of interiors.

We love predicting what’s going to be hot in home design for the upcoming seasons; however, you can’t talk about what’s going to be “in” without also touching on what’s going to be “out.” We asked a handful of designers to share what design choices and interior trends they saw a lot of in 2026 that they’re more than happy to leave behind in 2027. Keep reading to find out the nine things they said.

We spend so much time making our interiors warm and reflective of our personalities, it would be a shame to ruin the look of these spaces by opting for one-dimensional lighting.

“It's time to move beyond relying on a single overhead fixture or wall-to-wall recessed lighting to illuminate an entire room,” designer Kristen Mendoza of Four Brothers Design + Build says. “Flat, overly bright lighting can make even the most beautifully designed spaces feel sterile and uninviting.”

Instead, incorporating layers of lighting to add dimension and create a warm atmosphere is much better for your home. Sconces, picture lights, table lamps, and thoughtfully placed accent lighting at various heights throughout the room, Mendoza adds, give a space a more intimate touch.

“I also recommend using warm 2700K light sources on dimmers to flatter people, highlight architectural details, and bring out the natural beauty of materials,” she explains. “Lighting should be considered early in the design and construction process, not treated as an afterthought.”

Part of the appeal of “grandma chic” decor is that it’s been collected over time. The pieces show their age, the interior is imperfect, and it’s very clearly reflective of the homeowner’s personality and life. However, “there is a difference between a room that feels ‘collected over time’ and one that feels inherited all at once,” designer Stephanie Kraus explains, and she’s tired of seeing this false presentation on display in people’s homes.

“Heavy Roman shades with lots of fabric, chintz on top of chintz on top of chintz, fringe on every edge, etc.,” she adds. “One of these details in a room can be charming, but if you use them all together, the room can start to feel like a period movie set.”

It’s often clear when this eccentric aesthetic is manufactured rather than effortless. Instead of trying to make the “ideal” interior in the span of a month, be patient and work on your home over a greater span of time. That way, the end product will look authentic.

Though curved sofas are massively popular right now and look gorgeous in a photograph, Kraus is ready to leave them in 2026. “A curved sofa rarely seats more than two people comfortably,” she says. “Once guests settle in, everyone ends up sitting awkwardly, and conversation feels harder than it should.”

She’s all for a sofa or couch being something that invites people in to linger over conversations and stay late into the night, and a kidney-shaped seat isn’t going to do that.

While designer Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios loved seeing the return of decorative, playful details like ruffles and scalloped edges, she thinks we’ve gone a bit overboard in 2026. These touches are being applied to just about everything: upholstery, pillows, lampshades, bedding, furniture—the list could go on.

“These details can be incredibly charming, but when they're repeated throughout an entire room, they begin to lose the very character that makes them special,” she says.

Rather than treat every piece of a room with one of these details, Vollrath suggests opting for a scalloped lampshade or a beautifully tailored ruffled pillow instead to still capture that charm without drowning the space in them.

“Limiting these details to one or two thoughtful applications allows them to feel intentional rather than overly thematic,” she adds. “Sometimes, the most interesting interiors are as much about what you choose to leave out as what you include.”

“I'd love to see us move away from selecting materials simply because they imitate the appearance of something more expensive,” Vollrath explains. This goes beyond marble-printed contact paper and burl wood veneer; the designer means on a larger scale, like mass-produced tile that’s made to replicate the imperfections of handmade zellige.

“Part of what makes authentic zellige so beautiful is the craftsmanship and natural variation that comes from its production,” she says. “When those qualities are artificially reproduced, I think we sometimes lose the very thing that made the original material special.”

She would rather see a greater appreciation for beautiful, honest materials at every price point, and if the exact thing a homeowner wants is out of budget, then her solution would be to pivot the idea altogether rather than find something cheaply made that looks like what they desire.

“A classic Carrara marble paired with beautifully considered cabinetry can create an incredibly sophisticated interior, even when something as dramatic as Calacatta Viola isn't within the budget,” Vollrath explains.

We could probably say this every year, but it bears repeating: Don’t solely follow trends! “Just because something is trending doesn't mean it's right for your home,” designer Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox says. “Polka dots are having a moment right now, but they don't make sense in many homes, or dare I say, most homes.”

He suggests thinking of home trends as prompts that should get you to brainstorm new ideas for your space that actually fit your style. Take polka dots again. If you want to introduce a new pattern to your home, but you don’t particularly mesh with polka dots, try a stripe instead.

“If you try to force something you don't love into your home just because it's trending, you're going to drive yourself crazy,” he adds. Let’s leave this line of thinking back in 2026 once and for all.

While we love a good built-in bookshelf and wooden ceiling beam, those types of architectural details don’t always make sense in every single home. In 2026, as personality-filled interiors became more popular for everyone, a lot of homeowners looked into adding this type of millwork to their spaces as a way to add more visual interest and texture.

“Decorative beams, arches, and elaborate millwork can feel forced when they don't make sense with the architecture of the home,” designer Dani Flax of Pearson Design Group argues. “I think there's a tendency to add these elements to make a space feel more interesting, but the result can actually take away from the architecture itself.”

A home in a modern farmhouse style, for example, would look a bit odd with an intricate ceiling medallion and excessive picture frame molding. Design your interior around the existing architecture and the style of your home, don’t incorporate these elements simply because they’re everywhere right now.

The next year is not about playing it safe with your interiors—designers are tired of the stark white home. While ubiquitous gray and white color schemes had their time, they can often make a home feel generic and devoid of character.

“In their place, we're embracing more intentional, tonal palettes and color drenching,” Mendoza says.

You can still have a mostly white house, of course, but take a little risk and mix in some pops of color and pattern here and there, or choose a beautiful neutral wood tone for your cabinetry instead of a bright white paint.

“Taking a considered color, whether it's a deep green, warm clay, or earthy mushroom, and carrying it across walls, trim, and cabinetry creates a cohesive, enveloping effect,” Mendoza adds. “The goal isn't necessarily to introduce more color, but to use it with greater confidence and purpose.”

This is definitely a hot take, but designer John Maloney McNamara of Coosa Interiors is ready to leave velvet upholstery behind. “I’ve tried, but velvet almost always reads as either a 1990s prom limo or a hotel lobby trying very hard,” he explains. “The sheen shows every crush and bruise, and it ages into something that looks tired rather than loved.”

If you love the look of velvet, he suggests going with a mohair fabric instead, saying it has all of velvet’s depth and none of its shine. It also only gets better with age. And though he knows creamy bouclé has been overdone, Maloney McNamara thinks it deserves a reprise, but only in a rich color that can replace velvet still.

#6847. 6 Stylish Ways Designers Make Vintage Tile the Star of the Room

Here’s how to give this charming, old-school detail new life.

When we redesign, remodel, and renovate, it can be tempting to update, replace, and modernize every nook and cranny of your home. But the goal is rarely to chase newness. It’s easy to see vintage details, like quirky tile, as an obstacle standing between your home and a modern look, but tearing it out might make your heart ache later.

“I love the irregularities you find in older tiles, whether it's subtle variations in color, an imperfect glaze, or the undulation of a tile's surface. Those imperfections are what make them so beautiful, giving a room texture, character, and a sense of craftsmanship,” says Ali Burgoon Nolan, founder and principal designer at Studio Burgoon. Tile is one of the vintage details homeowners regret removing the most, as modern-day designs and materials lack the panache, durability, and craftsmanship of decades past. Not to mention, the removal and reinstallation will cost you a big chunk of your budget.

“Just like in other areas of the home, introducing a vintage element helps to create more interesting, storied spaces,” says Johanna Lyle, founder of Jo Lyle & Co. “When we mix elements from different eras, the home feels more unique and collected over time. If you've bought an old home and aren't sure you love the existing, perhaps historic, tile, try to change some things around it before you give up on it completely. You might not dig it just because you haven't seen it before, or it's not what is currently popular.”

If you’re lucky enough to have inherited vintage tile in your historic home, here are six ways designers have given it the main character energy it deserves.

Jennie Bishop preserved the charm of this century-old Illinois foyer by buffing the shine back into the home’s original tile. Her team then refinished the adjacent wood staircase and painted the walls a creamy white to brighten the room.

Designer Galena Younger knows potential when she sees it, and this historic cottage in San Antonio was no exception. The home’s original Mexican-style tiles surround the living room hearth, complemented by family heirlooms scattered around the room for an ultrapersonal, deliciously vintage aesthetic.

Interior and jewelry designer Trish Becker preserved this home’s original tile mantel, using it as the starting point for the room’s design. A corn-husk woven rug adds earthy warmth, while touches of green connect the room’s architectural details to its bold patterned slipper chairs.

With the goal of restoring her home’s “1931 glory,” designer Claire Thomas was ecstatic to find this bathroom’s original purple untouched, despite many renovations by previous owners. Playing on the hue, she brought in soft floral wallpaper and skirted purple stools for a feminine edge, finishing the room with a sage vanity for a pop of color and soft contrast.

Artist Dana Gibson and interior designer Sara Hillery brought a playful touch to this sunroom, keeping the home’s original tile floors as the foundation. They drew out the tiles’ blue tones in the plush textiles, then added woven furniture to complement its earthy browns.

It doesn’t get more classic than a black-and-white design scheme. In this Richmond bath, Gibson and Hillery leaned into the existing retro tiles, opting to carry the palette through the room’s entire design.

#6842. California Ease Meets New York Polish in This Jeremiah Brent-Designed Long Island Home

In this traditional Locust Valley house, hotel-inspired gathering spaces meet Art Deco furnishings.

Above: In the dining room of this Long Island home, a wraparound mural by Liz Allison carries a canopy of trees across the walls and ceiling. The dining table is from Petersen Antiques.

Up a long driveway, past sweeping lawns, a sprawling traditional house sits so far back from the road that not a neighbor is in sight. Built in 1971, the 12,840-square-foot home in Locust Valley, New York, reminds ELLE Decor A-List designer Jeremiah Brent of the property in Nancy Meyers’s 1991 comedy Father of the Bride: “It’s super beautiful and kind of bucolic,” he says.

For the house’s new owners, a family of five returning to the East Coast after a decade in Los Angeles, the move was a homecoming: The wife grew up in this Long Island town. They enlisted Brent to design the house for the next phase of their lives, as their three children finished high school and began leaving for college. They hoped their new home would blend the ease they’d grown accustomed to in California with a little of the New York City they’d be living just outside, without a design that felt too “new.” They also knew that they wanted to entertain.

Brent, who has offices on both coasts, often works with soft palettes, natural materials, and vintage furniture. The house’s traditional architecture gave him a unique starting point, and the couple was eager to see what he could do with the generously scaled rooms. A large parlor and dining room flanking the front hall offered ample space for entertaining—and for mixing styles and eras. “Let’s blend different design styles. Let’s play with different eras,” Brent recalls them saying in early conversations. Their enthusiasm was infectious. “There’s certain clients that you meet and you know intrinsically within the first five minutes that you’re just going to have the best time,” he says.

As part of her vision to entertain, the wife wanted the dining room to feel like a lounge. Brent put a large antique table in the center, then borrowed a detail from restaurants: banquettes with little tables and individual lights. Built into the corners, they give guests somewhere to move for a drink without leaving the room. “It becomes this area where, when you’re entertaining, you don’t leave,” he says. Overhead, muralist Liz Allison’s painted branches spread from the walls across the ceiling. “I wanted you to feel like you’re under a canopy of trees,” Brent says.

Across the hall, the parlor takes its cue from a hotel lobby, a reference the homeowner brought to Brent. He arranged the furniture in small groups so guests could break off into separate conversations. Within the neutral palette, he concentrated on texture and the way the room would reflect light. Art Deco pieces and a lacquered ceiling lend it polish. The light fixtures feature 3-D flowers that Brent sketched by hand and transformed into raised plaster details. “The florals are from the property’s garden, a particular part that the client loves,” he says.

The kitchen is as much a place to entertain as the formal rooms, but it’s also where the family gravitates when the children are home. It had to be a focal point. “I want a wow kitchen,” the wife told Brent. Boldly veined marble gives that drama, with contrasting stones on the island and the counter behind it lending depth. A glass-fronted pantry keeps the everyday supplies in view, from jars of spices to baguettes and fruit.

But not all design elements were new—Brent maintained much of the house’s traditional architecture, adapting it to the way the family wanted to live. He kept the stair spindles in the front hall, giving them a lacquered finish above a new soapstone floor. Under the stairs, wood trim and grasscloth gave an existing plain powder room more definition. Its bespoke stone sink, which Brent sketched himself, has a fluted base. “The client wanted a very tailored, edited home, but I didn’t want it to feel like it was new,” he says.

The primary wing is where Brent leaned further into the Art Deco inspiration. He reconfigured the layout, building a dressing room from scratch with silk moiré inset into the panels and a limestone pendant from the 1930s. He added parchment cabinets from the ’40s and designed the primary bath with the clean Deco lines in mind. In the bedroom, he kept the palette pale and played up the room’s height. “She wanted to feel like she was in the clouds.”

As their children move into adulthood, the couple will soon have more time to themselves—and more occasions to invite people in. “We really built a space that was geared towards the next chapter of their life, as empty nesters,” Brent says, “and a place where people could really come together.”

Julia Cancilla is the social media & news editor at ELLE Decor, where she oversees the brand's socials and covers design, pop culture, and emerging trends. She also authors the monthly ELLE Decoroscope column. Her work has appeared in Inked magazine, House Beautiful, Marie Claire, and more.

#6841. 26 Serena & Lily Black Friday Deals That Make It Easy to Refresh Your Home for Way Less

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

This is the sale we wait for all year long.

If you love to shop online as much as we do, the most wonderful time of the year is finally here: Black Friday. For the next few days, every retailer you can think of is having a big sale, which makes knowing where to start your shopping spree feel overwhelming. Our advice: Start somewhere splurge-worthy like Serena & Lily, which is offering up to 50 percent off everything.

Think of the retailer as the coastal grandmother and grandmillennial trends personified: The brand’s cornucopia of woven wares, bistro sets, and blue-and-white everything brings an easy, breezy spirit to virtually any room of the house. Serena & Lily's products might be desirable, but they’re not cheap, which makes its Black Friday sale a Cyber Week highlight.

Now through December 2, you can save up to half off furniture, tabletop essentials, bedding, and so much more. You could arguably spend all week browsing Serena & Lily’s discounted stuff, but remember: You have a lot of shopping to do. So let’s cut to the chase, shall we? Keep scrolling to see the deals worth adding to your cart.

It’s really not up for debate: New sheets will make the time you spend in bed dreamier. Not only will this sateen set’s dainty embroidery elevate your personal space, but its 50 percent discount will make your wallet happy.

Now is the time to decorate for the winter holidays, and not just because they're near. The Cyber Monday sales on all things festive, including fresh, live wreaths, are almost too good to pass up. This one, dotted with real white berries, comes in three sizes, ranging from 24 to 36 inches in diameter.

They come in three different sizes, so you'll find the right size for your home, whether you want to store a few strewn-about toys or all your extra pillows and blankets.

Lighting can completely change the look and feel of a room, so consider a small, sleek table lamp like this your mini powerhouse. Choose from white linen or woven raffia for your shades, but either way, they'll diffuse light for a soft, warm glow.

This side table can serve as a nightstand, with extra storage on the bottom for anything you need to hide or charge. Or use it as a living room table, with the upper tier for snacks or drinks and the lower tier for extra reading material.

We can't get enough of these dip-dyed stools, and the set of two is under $100. They're great for everything from bathtub side storage to stylish plant stands. They're carved from solid teak, which is one of the best materials for water resistance.

A subtly textured rug can make any room feel that much more comfortable. This one evokes Turkish patterns and is finished with fringe. We’re partial to the runner size placed alongside a bed, or in a hallway connecting living spaces.

Is there anything quite as dramatic as a canopy bed? Whether you drape princess-style curtains over these posts or not, it makes the bedroom feel taller and grander. You deserve to sleep like royalty.

#6838. Everything but the Plane: Here’s How One Brand Is Bringing Glamour Back to Travel

PS’s private terminals are the epitome of real luxury.

Imagine a trip to the airport with no lines. No lines to check in, to drop your bag, to be told your first-class ticket is the wrong first-class ticket for the first-class lounge. Now add to that no interpersonal friction. There are no snappy desk workers, no turbo TSA employees, and no graveyard-shift customs agents that give you grief.

Airlines can solve only so many issues. These are terminal problems. PS, a Los Angeles–based company operating its own luxury private terminals, is solving them. The goal is to get you as close to the charter experience as possible, all while still flying commercial. If you ask around or sit in the common areas and eavesdrop long enough, you’ll likely hear something along the lines of “I usually fly private, but when I don’t, this is what I use.”

The company has locations in Atlanta, Dallas Fort Worth, Miami, and Los Angeles, and the next order of business is expansion to the airports its clients use most—business hubs, mostly. But it started at LAX in 2017, and that’s the perfect airport for something like this. The horseshoe design and the traffic it creates, the small terminals, and the paparazzi culture do not cater to the A-list and executive frequent fliers that use it as a home airport. PS offers a way around that with on-tarmac connection from your flight to its terminal and vice versa. A chauffeur pulls you straight off the jet bridge and into a luxury fleet vehicle; that partnership is currently with BMW. If you don’t have any checked luggage, that 7 Series can (for an additional price) drive you straight to your destination within 35 miles of the airport. So if you are sitting in row 1 of first class, that’s about 50 steps between you and the i7 that takes you to your hotel. Or it can take you back to the PS terminal, which is not a lounge but acts as an über-private lounge experience.

It ain’t cheap: $1,300 per head one-way for the Salon (a shared bar area), $4,950 for a private suite (essentially an airport hotel room with butler and spa service; this price is all-inclusive), and $1,650 for that PS Direct drive-you-straight-home service. There are discounts and perks if you opt for yearly membership, but this is all on top of your ticket, folks.

And it can lean comically luxurious, with Champagne and caviar offered every time you enter—like before my 8:00 a.m. departure, to which I’m not opposed but which caught me a little off guard. There’s the jet-set marketing—tagline: The “back door” to the airport—and meant-for-the-timeline branded cocktail napkins: “You’ve arrived.”

But once you accept that PS could not afford to excuse itself from the flex-driven airport exclusivity economy, you can really admire its hospitality-inspired finishes. Its raison d’être is to get you through the airport with as little friction as possible, but it stands out in its commitment to hospitality within those confines.

For example, interior design and artwork are curated. The centerpiece artworks of each location are done in collaboration with local artists, made to fit the location they are in. The crown jewel thus far is in Miami, where PS took over the old Pan Am headquarters and flight-training academy. The mid-century building is a Miami landmark, with geometric thermal walls acting as the facades and this pinkish sandy and limestone-heavy concrete throughout. Argentinian-born ceramicist Nina Surel matched this with tile-work water features and a ceramic mural at the center of the property. Design like this, small and mostly unnoticed in the grand scheme of things, is what turns your mind back to a golden age of travel. It’s not credit card points and moneymaking schemes—though, actually, you can buy any of the non-centerpiece artworks—just beauty for the sake of beauty.

On top of that, the food and beverage program is considered and regional. The blue-corn cornbread with honey and a ricotta spread that chef Kelsey Kirwin (chefs’ names are on the menus, an indication of the atmosphere) is making at the DFW location overtook my mother’s as my all-time favorite. I would consider the signature PS martini—with (guess what) caviar-stuffed olives—worthy of a place on any hot big-city cocktail menu. And all the coffees, snacks, and water options are head and shoulders above those of both the general-population terminals and the litany of airport lounges within them.

As far as service, yes, an agent handles your party’s check-in and keeps you abreast of delays so you don’t have to open an app—but that’s to be expected. It’s more in the interim. Servers don’t hover, they listen, and they’re backed by an extensive behind-the-scenes operation: Mention that you prefer coffee, not bubbles, before a morning flight? A note is made on your profile for the next time you visit. Only ever ask for sparkling water when you fly out of LAX? They’ll know that in Atlanta. The entire operation seems to run on Guidara’s Unreasonable Hospitality, the same way all modern restaurants do. If you’re leaving Miami and mention to a business partner that you forgot to try a Cubano (on menu) and a cafecito (off menu), both will likely be delivered to your table, compliments of the house.

But every time I start to get wowed by the service details, I remember the price point and the level of clientele. That should be expected.

Standard terminals are air-side, post-security, where everything (even a fancy and expensive airline lounge) is working within those confines. Since PS operates land-side, with security the last stage of the terminal experience, it has the flexibility to be more hospitable to guests. For example, if you book a private suite, you can fly in, use it for a business meeting, and fly out. Or your parents can accompany the family to departure, hang out in the suite, and squeeze every last second with the grandkids out of your visit. It is an airport experience actually designed around the ways we interact and what matters to us when we travel. That, more than the Champagne and caviar, is the definition of luxury.

#6829. 50 White Elephant Gifts Under $50 That Are Practically Guaranteed to Be Stolen

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Beware, these picks might start some fights.

I’ve been writing gift guides as a shopping editor for years. Usually, I’m very sentimental, wanting to recommend thoughtful gifts to make your loved ones smile. But all bets are off when it comes to White Elephant gifts. I want to cause chaos at your family gift swap. Your gift should be the one everyone fights over.

If you're not sure what a White Elephant exchange is or how it works, just know this: it's really fun, and it's one of the only times when stealing is not only totally acceptable, but encouraged. Basically, it's a blind gift exchange that, when the recipient reveals the gift they chose, they can keep it or put it back and steal someone else's gift. It's a cheeky little game that is a great way to get into the holiday spirit.

It’s also a really affordable way to exchange gifts as a big group. No worries about finding the perfect thing for someone specific. Just bring something cool enough for anybody to enjoy, and there’s usually a budget cap. So for this guide, I provided the best white elephant gifts under $50. They’re so budget-friendly and unique that you might want to do all of your gift shopping below, or buy some items of your own. Worry not; we won’t judge.

#6826. 50 Percent of People In Relationships Would Take a Trip Together to Solve Their Problems

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

A “baecation” often entails a romantic getaway, passionate activities, and a lot of vacation sex. But what if traveling with your significant other meant a lot of difficult conversations, a heated back-and-forth, and working to fix your relationship. I know: this sounds nothing like the dreamy R&R you pictured—but for some couples, it’s exactly what they planned for.

According to Booking.com’s 2027 Travel Predictions, 58 percent of U.S. travelers said they would consider taking a trip to resolve conflicts or arguments.

Now, traveling itself already can cause real strain and arguments on relationships on its own. In the same data set, more than one-third of respondents said they found out they were incompatible with their companion after a trip. Nearly three in 10 people have considered ending a relationship or friendship over a trip, while more than one in five have actually cut a vacation short because of a bad travel companion.

But if you’re already having relationship issues with your partner, is a trip a good idea? People still seem to think so. About 81 percent said that taking part in at least one vacation activity with another person helps them have honest or difficult conversations.

So should you be planning a trip the next time you and your partner start arguing?

A change of scenery might be exactly what you and your partner need to see eye-to-eye again.

“Getting away from work, chores, kids, in-laws, or house and childcare stuff can lower defenses,” couples therapist Justena Kavanagh tells Cosmopolitan.

She explains that when you’re home, you’re essentially having every argument on the same “battleground.”

“Somewhere new often brings back the honeymoon period, when you could really see each other without life’s stresses in the way,” she says. And if both you and your partner have busy lives and packed schedules, a vacation may really be the only time you can focus on the issues in your relationship without distraction.

Plus, vacations encourage you to relax—which can go a long when in an argument. “When we’re rested, we’re more regulated, and when we’re regulated, we can listen instead of defending,” Kavanagh says.

She explains that it’s important to establish why exactly you’re going on the vacation in the first place. Are you getting away to talk about an issue? Or simple to get away from it and reconnect? Agreeing on the purpose for the trip before you start packing your bikini will help you and your partner get on the same page—so you don’t end up just arguing more later.

“Trips suit conversations about connection, like feeling distant, intimacy, or where the relationship is going,” she says. Other things like finances or in-law conflicts may need a follow-up at home.

If you decide that you’re going to talk your issues out, remember that you are still on vacation. That doesn’t mean everything has to be wonderful, of course. But Kavanagh encourages couples to treat the trip like a summit. This means talking early in the day (after a good night’s rest) and working on things while taking breaks.

The trip should essentially be your opportunity to discuss what’s currently happening in the relationship, where you want the relationship to go, and how to shift priorities to get there.

Sure, a beautiful view and hotel sex will definitely lighten the mood and probably even make you more agreeable—but it isn’t a magical bandaid for your relationship issues.

“If couples don’t have relational skills at home, they won’t miraculously have them on vacation,” Kavanagh warns.

So if you’re going to go on a vacation to work on things with your partner, you’re going to want to put a plan into place. The best method? Talking about the pattern behind the argument, rather than hyperfocusing on the latest incident.

And even if you do come to a resolution on vacation, make sure you’re setting a plan in place for the positive energy to flow into your everyday lives.

“Be realistic about home, where you’re more stressed and old patterns return fast,” Kavanagh says. “Plan follow-up support, a regular check-in, and what you’ll do when things go off track, because they will.”

Okay, so not all of us can afford to book a flight every time we get into an argument with our partner—but you don’t have to. Out of all the activities that people do on vacation, respondents said that it was mostly hikes or walking that was the most effective at getting them to open up and have the difficult conversations.

Lucky for you, taking a hike is totally free—and can do wonders for whatever issue you may be facing with your partner.

Kavanagh explains that walking and hiking take pressure off of an argument.

“Face to face can feel like a confrontation; side by side, you’re facing the same direction and don’t have to hold eye contact in the hardest moments,” she explains. This is something we see in psychotherapy: think about someone lying on a couch, looking at the ceiling. This helps physiologically and emotionally. Plus the actual physical activity helps, too.

“Walking also discharges stress and is a natural form of bilateral stimulation, the left-right rhythm used in therapies like EMDR,” she says.

When your partner is irritating you, it’s likely that the last thing you want to do is travel with them. But if you’re determined to move past whatever may be causing a rift, that might be exactly what you need.

Honeymoons and other happy occasions shouldn’t be the only time we travel with our partners with the purpose of growing closer, reconnecting, and strengthening the relationship. Sometimes, even in hardship, a little getaway could be the jumpstart your relationship needs to get back on track. Just remember that after the vacation ends, you do have to go back to reality.

“The good feelings are real, but the test is whether they last back home, where life is harder,” Kavanagh says.

#6824. Inside a Layered Ski-in, Ski-out Retreat in the Mountains of Utah

An affectionate nickname inspired much of the design details.

Designers learn a lot of things about their clients when building or renovating their homes—it’s how they ensure the spaces actually feel like the homeowners. Their hobbies shape the purposes of distinct rooms, their tastes affect the color palette, and their experiences help to build out the decor. So, when designer McKenzie Matthews of Coven Haus (accidentally) learned that her client affectionately calls her husband The Wolf, she simply had to weave that part of their story into the design of their Park City, Utah, winter retreat.

“What started as a playful detail became a recurring theme, from a custom wolf head silhouette repeated along the secondary staircase to wolf hooks in the mudroom lockers,” Matthews says. The designer, who was part of the six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new-build from the start, wanted every aspect of the interior to feel like a luxurious and elevated boutique ski resort but with more personalized charm. “We carried the wolf motif into decorative pillows, artwork, and accessories, creating little moments of personality and meaning throughout the home.”

Outside of these smaller details, the rest of the ski-in, ski-out style home is layered with natural elements, like stone, reclaimed wood, and plaster, to complement the views outside of the Wasatch Mountain Range. The front door opens to a wood-clad foyer, topped with a vaulted ceiling accented with reclaimed wooden beams, which continues into the great room. Flanked by two double-sided stone fireplaces and looking out beyond the second floor of the deck into the woods, this gathering area invites any guest to escape the cold of the slopes and stay as long as they like.

Handmade and vintage pieces fill in any gaps, giving each room a collected feel. “One of my favorite examples is the primary bedroom bookcase, filled with unique objects and accessories we’ve gathered over the years,” Matthews says. “It’s those little details that give a home so much character.”

Spanning 11,758 square feet, it can be hard to make a house of this size truly feel homey, but Matthews’ dedication to the details ensured it was. “Every room has something special and unexpected, yet the entire home still feels cohesive,” she says. “I love that there's always something new to discover as you move through each space.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: McKenzie Matthews of Coven HausArchitect: Otto Walker Stylist: Lauren GastelumLocation: Park City, UtahThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom ski-in, ski-out new build across 11,758 square feet.

The entry sets the tone for the rest of the home, with the wood-paneled walls, vaulted ceiling, limestone floor tile, and reclaimed beams serving as a warm, inviting escape from the cold outside. The variety of textures brings character and, along with the sculptural entry table, helps to soften the grand architectural details.

No detail is too small in this ski room. Marble knobs dot the top lockers, while a Calacatta Viola marble door jamb makes the mundane feel special. “With our clients being from Argentina, we wanted to do something a little unexpected and playful, starting with the textural floor tile,” Matthews says.

The fun continues to the ceiling, which is coated in the same red that lines the interior of the oak lockers, just in a matte finish. “We loved the idea of it feeling like a jewelry box,” the designer says of the painted locker interiors.

Fabric panels on the upper lockers soften the millwork and break up the oak, as does the painted boot wall which opens to the ski patio. “There are so many little details in this room, and I love every one of them!” Matthews adds.

Laying out the great room was a bit of a challenge. Flanked by two double-sided stone fireplaces, Matthews wanted to take advantage of the hearths without blocking or sacrificing the incredible view. “We opted for movable seating that could easily shift depending on how the space is used, while keeping the view as the star of the room,” she explains. The tumbleweed chandelier was the cherry on top, creating a bit of unexpected fun while bringing in an organic element.

To the right of the living room, beyond the fireplace, is a gaming corner, slightly blocked away from the main area in case any matches get a bit rowdy.

Downstairs in the living room, Matthews wanted plenty of seating for family and friends to gather, so they ordered a custom sectional with extra-deep seat cushions that comfortably seats at least eight people, if not more. “The reclaimed wood ceiling and exposed beams add so much warmth and character, while the soft furnishings keep the space feeling relaxed and lived in,” she adds.

If the family still has energy after a day skiing, they can always bond over a game of poker. Rich dark cocoa millwork and light-blocking drapery close off this space from the rest of the house, while the wall of books gives it character and ensures it still feels homey. If poker isn’t the game of choice, the wood top is removable so it doubles as a regular gaming or puzzle table.

“This might be my favorite room in the house,” Matthews says. “The ideas just kept flowing, and we reworked the bar layout a handful of times before landing here.” While the entire setup would make the most seasoned bartender jealous, it’s the details—like the patinated brass island, the tiled ceiling, the double bullnose countertop edge, and necklace-like sculptural chandelier—that makes the space a moment. “A little five-star resort experience without ever leaving home.”

Because Matthews’s clients wanted a dirty kitchen and a main one for entertaining, the designer could be a bit more creative here, choosing the finishes and features more for the way they look rather than how well they function. A calming blue and brown color palette makes it the most enticing spot for a quiet morning coffee or for setting out a grand buffet for party guests.

Since this is where all the cooking happens, functionality was Matthews’s biggest priority, from the layout to the actual appliances and finishes. “The kitchen layout changed several times throughout the process, and it certainly helped that our builder, Don Jones [of Craig Construction], was once a chef!” she explains. “We really prioritized flow and functionality first, then let the design follow.”

Blue cabinetry ties in with the stove while bronze hardware does the same with the custom patinated brass hood, which warms everything up and will only get better-looking with age, as will the quartz countertops. The vintage plates on the wall add personality and style to this workhorse space.

In the wine cellar and tasting room, the architectural elements were quite linear, so Matthews opted for an arched doorway to create some movement. “We loved the idea of the temperature controlled wine cellar having a cavernous feel, which is why we chose to bring natural stone into the interior,” she explains.

Another favorite room of Matthews’, she especially loves the bookcase since it gives the cozy room an almost historic, collected feel. To add height and balance the scale of the bookshelves, she hung a tapestry rug above the bed, creating a layered, timeless escape for the homeowners.

The wallpaper, paired with the ruched velvet bed frame, adds romantic appeal to this guest bedroom. Vintage artworks and furniture, like the trunk, lend a collected feel, bringing the space to life. “The vintage sketches were carefully framed and hung from brass chains, adding a nod to the past and giving the room a sense of history,” the designer explains.

With over a decade in the design industry, McKenzie Matthews firmly believes good design should feel effortless and evolve over time. Matthews is the founder and principal designer of Coven Haus, a boutique interior design studio based in Utah and serving clients nationwide. The studio’s mission is to bring its clients’ visions of home to life through thoughtful design, collaboration, and authentic representations.

#6821. How to Look Great at the Airport

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Take inspiration from these celebrity travel outfits

Let’s face it—all too often, travel is not so glamorous. With flight cancellations, long TSA lines, and schlepping luggage, you may not feel chic throughout it all, but your travel look can still be. Peak holiday season is here, and some of the busiest travel days are still ahead; we drew inspiration from a few of our favorite celebrities to bring you the best travel outfits for women, making your next excursion all the more stylish.

Comfort is often top of mind, but that doesn’t mean you have to settle for just leggings. Katie Holmes showed how to wear a simple white button-down shirt and baggy patchwork jeans on a flight, adding comfortable soft loafers and a stylish houndstooth jacket—a cool spin on a relaxed travel outfit that still feels put together. Kendall Jenner went for something polished, wearing a silky cream matching set, while Naomi Watts sported a leather bomber jacket, T-shirt, wide-leg sweatpants, and bright-red sneakers, showing the many different directions you can take to fit your own personal style.

Ahead, shop the 9 best travel outfits for women that will ensure your journey is just as good as the looks you packed.

As Katie Holmes proves, you don’t always have to rely on leggings for the airport; instead, elevate your look with a white button-down shirt and baggy patchwork jeans. Styling those with a houndstooth jacket, gray cashmere knit, and soft leather loafers, you’ll look incredibly polished throughout your travel journey.

Guest in Residence Shrunken Cashmere Sweater

Everyone knows matching sets are the easiest wardrobe choice when traveling, but take yours a step further with a silky cream version, as Kendall Jenner did. A sleeveless top and wide-leg pants instantly look stylish in the luxe fabric. Add classic accessories like a suede duffel bag, the Row’s leather flats, and a watch as small but impactful touches. For a little extra warmth, throw on your favorite wrap, cardigan, or coat.

Sometimes your tried-and-true capsule wardrobe essentials are all you need. Naomi Watts paired a leather bomber jacket with a gray T-shirt and coordinating wide-leg sweatpants for a relaxed take. Bright-red sneakers add a playful touch, balancing the neutrals. From curbside to the arrivals gate, you’ll be comfortable throughout the duration of your trip, even after a red-eye.

The Canadian tuxedo works just as well on the street as it does in the air. Take inspiration from Isabelle Huppert’s chic attire, styling a denim button-down shirt with coordinating bootcut jeans (and a tonal handbag). The actress broke up all the blue with a khaki car coat and white sneakers—clean finishes with plenty of jet-set appeal.

As a seasoned globe-trotter, Julianne Moore is constantly moving from one city to another and certainly knows how to look pulled together while doing so. Take notes from her minimalist look with a French twist. Layer a black suit with a striped Breton top, both easy foundation pieces you’re sure to wear throughout any journey. From there, keep accessories classic with optic white shoes and an oversize shopper.

When Alexa Chung steps out, we take notice. Here, she puts her signature spin on all the travel classics while traveling. A khaki trench coat, a lace-trimmed camisole, and straight-leg white jeans are all you need. By adding oversize black sunnies, leather slip-on shoes, and the iconic Boat and Tote bag by L.L.Bean, she brings a laid-back, cool sensibility to airport fashion.

Intimissimi Pretty Flowers Tank Top With Lace

Why make things overly complicated when travel already is? Priyanka Chopra shows how simplicity and monochrome dressing can be a winning outfit formula. Her straightforward tank top and loose wide-leg trousers demonstrate how comfort can be a priority without sacrificing style. Don’t forget to bring a long cashmere cardigan to ward off in-flight chills and an oversize travel tote bag to complete the look.

Reformation Katerina Lug Sole Chelsea Boots

When you’re looking to travel with a little more style, look to Mikey Madison for inspiration. By using a trench coat as her focal piece, she creates an effortless travel look by layering a sweater underneath and pairing it with slinky black pants. Suede flat slides, a bright handbag, and a trustworthy carry-on create the perfect combination for your next getaway.

Burberry Long Kensington Heritage Trench Coat

Banana Republic Lightweight Cashmere Crewneck Sweater

Nothing beats a white T-shirt and blue jeans, the American style staples that just keep giving. Channel the ease of Margot Robbie’s airport outfit with this similar combination, accessorizing with comfortable ballet flats and a stylish flap bag for added flair. A clean leather belt and tortoise sunglasses are all you need to ace this look.

#6817. 42 Designer-Approved Playroom Ideas That Prove Kids’ Spaces Can Still Look Stylish

These ideas make room for mess, imagination, and parent-approved design.

As much as you love your children, you may not love living among their toys and furniture. People spend a lot of time, effort, and money making their homes look beautiful, but not every design choice is kid-friendly—or built to withstand little ones. If you have room for a dedicated play space, outside of your children’s bedrooms, it could provide the perfect solution to let them be as messy and imaginative as possible. And a playroom can still be aesthetically pleasing, too. Take a look at these designer spaces for inspiration.

Whether you lean toward the “beige mom” style or prefer a more maximalist interior, these playroom ideas offer something special for you and your children.

Kids are always changing their minds about what they want, so keep things a bit more fluid in their playroom with a modular sofa that they can constantly rearrange. In this kids’ space, designer Kate Haynes of Noble Studio chose to put this sofa underneath the climbing wall for a soft place to land.

In this calming playroom, Mandy Cheng added wraparound cabinetry to store all the toys and crafts her clients’ children have accumulated over the years. It not only gives the room a clear purpose, but the closed cabinet doors make the space feel so much cleaner.

An unused corner in designer Ali Budd’s home became the ultimate play area for her kids. “It has a play structure, cushions, curtains, and Gucci wallpaper,” Budd says. “Somehow this tiny kids’ space ended up being one of the chicest spaces in the house.”

Just because the playroom is for the kids, doesn’t mean their walls should be bare. In this room designed by Katie Davis, she went with cute Goldfish cracker-themed 3D artwork, alluding to the children’s favorite snack.

The furniture in this playroom, designed by Hilary Colia and Jennifer Kostohryz of Fort Design Studio, actually came from the old family room. It gives the space a more elevated and timeless feel, even when it’s purposely designed for the kids, and it gives the parents a comfortable place to sit.

Designer Emory McKim knew her kids would want to be anywhere she and her husband were, so she designed their playroom to be connected to the den via a cased opening. “It has worked seamlessly for us,” McKim says. “We can be in the den enjoying a show, while also watching the kids.”

In this older playroom, designer Alden Fletcher picked out a dark green wallpaper to top the knotty pine wainscoting, then went with other deep earthy tones to make up the space. It’s cozy and inviting, and the ski-themed pattern on the wallcovering keeps it feeling youthful, but not immature.

If your kids have (reasonable!) design requests for their playroom, making them come true will give them the space of their dreams. In this animal-accented playroom, designer Ariel Okin says that the kids “really wanted a swinging chair,” so she added one.

Add playfulness to the room by choosing a fun, patterned floor. The team at Becca Interiors chose this white, black, and burnt orange carpet for the playroom in this Connecticut home, giving the space a bit more life and vibrancy.

A Dutch door is a playroom idea that bridges the gap between whimsical and practical. It encloses the space while letting you quickly peek in if needed. The team at Curated Nest chose to do so in this upstate New York home, and it matches the rest of the aesthetic perfectly.

In the same home as above, Curated Nest installed this custom climbing wall in the basement. “The kids are very active and wanted a space for friends to enjoy, so we created this custom climbing wall with a hand-painted mural,” Lina Galvão from Curated Nest explains.

Yes, the playroom in your home should appeal to the little ones, but that doesn't mean you have to sacrifice your entire aesthetic. Designer Annie Kern did not shy away from the rich tones and textures that make up the rest of the house in this playroom idea.

Combine a playroom and bunk room for the ultimate sleepover space. Designer Becca Casey added built-in shelves and a window seat to this moody space. Plus, there are three separate beds so the kids can have plenty of overnight playdates.

A hand-painted mural is the stuff of playroom dreams—it will encourage imaginative play and give your children the fondest memories of growing up in such a beautiful space. In this home, designer Tina Ramchandani used a ceiling mural to add color without feeling chaotic.

As your kids get older and their interests change, their personal space should evolve too. What was once a playroom covered in stuffed animals may become a pint-sized workspace for arts and crafts. Designer Marika Meyer says that this colorful, inspiring craft room might just be her favorite in the entire project.

Decorate the kids-only space with art that they've done themselves. Do what designer Ashley Gilbreath did in this playroom idea and showcase their artwork in custom frames—it'll make your children feel so special to see their work on display.

As designer Emily Henderson points out, kids outgrow things quickly—what they love today, they could be bored of tomorrow. “Keep large pieces neutral,” she says. “It's easier and more affordable to layer on pops of color and your kids' favorite characters or animals than it is to implement those motifs into the furniture and rugs.”

Implement traditional patterns and elegant materials when figuring out your playroom ideas—it doesn't have to be all neon and bright colors. Designer Becca Casey added a beautiful toile print wallpaper and rattan accents to this room that will only get better with time.

Related Story: Toile de Jouy: The Famous French Pattern’s History and Enduring Appeal

If your home has a small, awkwardly sized space, put the playroom in there. This reading nook in a boy's room by Benni Amadi was designed to feel like an indoor tree fort. Utilizing as much square footage in the house as possible and having fun with it was key.

Related Story: 12 Closet Remodeling Projects That Will Transform Your Space

Teach your kids early on about the importance of separating work and home life by designing a little study area for them in their playroom. As they grow older and start working on homework after school, their desk space can mature with them, like in this space by Marika Meyer.

#6815. 32 Best White-Elephant Gifts Under $50 That’ll Actually Get Stolen Twice

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Budget-friendly bangers and cheap crowd-pleasers for every chaotic holiday swap.

White-elephant gift exchanges are fun, but first you have to come up with the perfect gift everyone will want—within the established budget. Need a refresher on the game? Here’s how the chaos unfolds: Everyone brings a present, draws a number, and then, going in order based on which numbers everyone drew, either unwraps a mystery gift or steals one that’s already been revealed.

Some common caveats are that there’s a cap on steals per item (usually two or three), you can’t immediately steal back what was just swiped, and your turn ends when you open your gift (or exchange it for an already-opened one). The final player gets the power position—and sometimes a last swap—depending on house rules. You might also hear the game called Dirty Santa: same energy, same drama, whether it’s with coworkers or cousins.

What to bring under, say, $50? (That’s a pretty nice price ceiling.) Go funny but functional, like the stocking stuffers people will actually use. Think cozy comforts, clever kitchen gadgets, mini games, candles that actually smell good, and little indulgences that feel pricier than they are. Skip anything weirdly personal—you want universal hits that sisters, brothers, moms, dads, friends, and coworkers can all fight over with a smile. Bonus points if it’s something you’d happily steal back yourself.

That’s the general idea. It’s like Secret Santa but evil. The best part of all is that cheap is the way to go. A budget of $50 is more than enough, and there’s a lot of shit out there. So we sorted through it all and found the best white-elephant gifts for your 2026 festivities.

Since the Owala is the best water bottle of all time, we wouldn’t blame you if you ended up stealing it back for yourself.

Snoop writes a hell of a cookbook, whether you’re stoned or totally sober.

Read the room here, but these are our suggestions of who to put on these custom socks: the beloved family pet, your boss, the office coffee machine, yourself.

Dysons are nice, sure, but this tiny vacuum is well within any budget. It’s great for cleaning desk spaces, computer keyboards, and more.

Bean snobs swear this is the best way to make your cuppa.

The weiner-dog ice-cube tray is absolutely causing some fights this year.

Look at these cute little Yeti espresso cups! Who wouldn’t be psyched to unwrap these?

A little twist on a classic novel but focusing on cocktails. What more can we ask for?

Is a Pura smart fragrance diffuser the perfect white-elephant gift? I think so. By connecting to the Pura app, this clever little device lets you personalize your home scenting experience. Whoever gets this should host next time, because their place will smell sooo good.

This mini Zen garden will bring balance and serenity to their desk, either at home or at work.

This fun deck of cards will test out their card-playing skills as well as their music knowledge.

This notepad has little sayings like “I’m not even trying anymore” for the optimist in your friend group.

These winter months can be cold. I imagine everyone could use some hand cream right now, especially some really nice hand cream from Salt & Stone.

If they have a penchant for great breakfast foods, they’ll be all about this mini waffle maker.

Movie nights are getting an upgrade, and we are here for it.

Two Esquire editors have this slim toaster from Bella, which is easy to store, especially in a small apartment. It works like a charm and is so cute. And at $25, you might as well get one for yourself too.

On an earnest note, wisdom from Mr. Rogers will never go amiss, no matter your age.

I’d steal this from someone even if it wasn’t a white-elephant party.

In a similar vein, this leakproof mug keeps hot beverages hot and cold beverages cold, so whoever gets it can enjoy their tea or coffee at the perfect temperature.

#6813. 10 “Timeless” Design Choices Designers Say Will Always Be in Style

Step away from the trends and invest in these instead.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: A refreshing sunroom with built-in bookshelves by Doniphan Moore.

While embracing home design trends can be fun for a while, it can get really old really quickly. Of course, if you really like something, then by all means, decorate your home with it. However, if you’re simply reaching for the things that are popular at a current moment in time before throwing them out after you realize you don’t actually like them, then it might be time to invest in some more timeless decor.

“Ultimately, timeless design is all about creating spaces that are beautiful, functional, and meaningful,” says Abby Pendergrast, an interior stylist and set designer. “That’s why people are drawn to these classics—they make a house feel like a home, and that never goes out of style.”

Ahead, we asked a handful of designers to share the pieces and choices they deem timeless. From gilded mirrors to striped textiles, keep reading to see the 10 things that designers promise you’ll love for years to come.

Antique chests and dressers will never go out of style because they’re well made piece that only get better with time.

“They were meant to last for many years, acting as heirloom pieces for generations to come,” says Debbie Mathews LeRoy of Debbie Mathews Antiques & Designs. They’re multifunctional to use as storage or a spare surface, and they can easily move from room to room and house to house.

“An antique chest brings history and warmth to any space,” designer Emily Jiles of Maggie Griffin Design explains. “Mixing in an antique chest gives the room a collected feel and adds character that new furniture often can't replicate.”

Golden mirrors add a touch of elegance to any room, and they’re shockingly versatile. They can bring more luxury to a traditional style home, or they can add a historic air to a more modern space. Mathews LeRoy, however, specifically adores Louis Philippe giltwood mirrors.

“It can live happily in a traditional interior or fit within a transitional interior thanks to its simple arched shape with minimal decoration,” she says. “They boast understated elegance and can be beautiful in an entrance hall or foyer, dining room, living room, powder room, or bedroom!”

Like any classic choice, bookshelves will grow with you as your sensibility evolves and changes. They can be endlessly re-styled and adjusted to hold whatever pieces you acquire along the way. Plus, they are a kind of diary of the books you are reading and the conversations you are having.

“Literature never goes out of style. A home without books isn’t a home in my opinion. And a bookshelf acts as a blank canvas to explore your innate curatorial abilities,” says Alex Bass, founder and CEO of Art Advisory & Interior Design Studio Salon 21. “It is also something that grows with you and that you can constantly reorganize. So investing in a good one with timeless appeal is key.”

High-quality natural materials such as wood or stone just get better with time. “Using natural materials in your home is always a good idea,” says Pendergrast. “They bring warmth, texture, and that feeling of authenticity we all crave.”

Wood, whether it’s flooring, furniture, or even ceiling beams, adds a cozy, lived-in vibe, while stone is durable and elegant. “Wood and stone age gracefully, developing character as the years go by. They’re versatile enough to fit into any style, from modern to rustic, which means you won’t have to worry about them ever going out of fashion,” she says.

While we're on the topic of natural materials, let's not forget this inimitable combination. Marble and wood both have a knack for adding character and aging beautifully, and they're not just for kitchens. Think: Wooden chairs pulled up to a marble-topped dining table or a wood coffee table or console with a marble top. The combination is “both timeless, modern, and neutral while bringing a luxurious feel to your space,” says Bass.

Leather furniture carries memories—as a piece ages, its patina changes. And a leather chair can hold its own—the material has weight and personality that make it an excellent companion to a wide range of nearby colors, fabrics, and patterns. “It fits any type of design aesthetic, whether you’re going for something more mid-century or more traditional,” Bass says. “I love seeing the wear in a piece, the cracks and the wrinkles.”

Sticking with paint colors inspired by nature—such as soft blues, calming greens, whites, and warm tans—helps create a peaceful and balanced space. “These colors are easy on the eyes and make your home feel more like a retreat,” Pendergrast says. “Earthy, natural shades are always in style because they have this effortless way of grounding a space. They act like neutrals, so you can easily switch up your decor over the years without having to repaint the whole place.”

A wooden table, such as an antique farm table, is a timeless piece worth investing in. As demonstrated in this space designed by Clara Jung of Banner Day Interiors, it grounds the room, allowing other decor choices to be light and airy. Whether it’s a coffee table, side table, or made for a dining room, “You’ll always find use for it and it’s something sturdy that will never go out of style,” Bass says.

Stripes are a classic pattern that doesn’t ever feel too trendy. “You can throw them on curtains, rugs, or even wallpaper, and they’ll add a bit of structure without overpowering the room,” Pendergrast says. “Whether you go bold or keep it subtle, stripes always find a way to fit in.” The best part about stripes is how flexible they are. “They work with just about any style, and because they’re so simple, they won’t clash with other patterns or elements in your space,” she adds.

Built-in details such as crown molding or wainscoting give your space instant character. Your home may already have these features, but if it doesn’t, they are often simple additions you can implement. “These features add a little polish without feeling too stuffy, and they make the room feel more custom and unique,” Pendergrast says. “Crown molding and wainscoting have been around for ages, and they’re not going anywhere.” These architectural details are subtle ways to add depth and elegance to a room. Even better is that they can blend with any style, whether your vibe is traditional or modern.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6811. 16 Festive Holiday Date Ideas for the Most Wonderful Time of Year

Iam here to debunk a common dating myth: Despite what all the relationship gurus might suggest, dating doesn’t have to be confined to the summer. In fact, some of the best, most romantic dates can happen during the late fall and dead of winter. After all, every street and tree is covered in twinkling lights and the cold weather gives you an excuse (not that you need one) to cozy up next to your crush. Add the festivities of the holiday season on top of all of that and you literally have the makings of a dream date.

Sure, the urge to snuggle up with your coziest blanket and binge your favorite rom-coms is nagging you and your person—I get it. But this holiday season is the time to make your Hallmark movie dreams a reality! Whether you’re looking for an option for a third date with someone you just started seeing or trying to keep things fresh and fun in your long-term relationship, there’s something on this list for you.

Volunteering as a date night is a great way to see someone’s generous side. Find a local donation center, soup kitchen, or a cause that means a lot to you. You’ll be able to tell a lot about your date by their reaction to this idea and how they carry themselves throughout the volunteering.

This one will really test your teamwork. You can either grab a premade kit or go old-school and use a pint-sized milk carton and graham crackers as your base. Some of the premade are pretty simple, while others can be a bit more intricate. Either way, both will force you and your date to work together and problem solve. This will be a good way for those red flags or green flags to show themselves.

Because it’s time you get in on the whole matching pajamas and Santa hats. If you don’t want to put out money for a photographer, set up your phone’s camera with a timer and tripod—the photos can look just as amazing!

Elevate your hot chocolate game with fun flavors, like salted caramel, dark chocolate, or peppermint. You can buy premade variety packs or really chef it up with making your own. Whatever you choose, just be sure not to skim on the toppings. Grab marshmallows of all sizes, candy canes, whipped cream (I personally love the boozy vodka-infused peppermint one from Whipshots), and whatever else sounds enticing. You will be chocolate wasted and it will be fantastic.

Karaoke isn’t about who sounds the best but who has the best energy. Belt out your favorite holiday jams, from the classics to new renditions of the season’s most popular songs. It’s truly a perfect way to bond and see if your date can match your vibe.

This option is such a fun way to spend time together. Whether this is an early date with someone you recently started seeing or an activity you and your longtime love do, it’s great for talking, asking questions, and having fun. If you’re a part of the first group, this can be an opportunity to make a candle for each other—then you could exchange them as a low-stakes holiday gift (because getting a present for your situationship is always tricky).

If you or your date are people who appreciate a good aesthetic, finding a local light show could be the perfect night out. The low lighting and colorful moments can help you draw closer to your person, whether it’s in the car riding through or walking. Light shows leave room for more intimate conversations to take place, too, if you want that.

The best way to spread holiday cheer is by supporting local businesses. From small batch food to unique clothes and accessories, it’s where you and your partner can shop for each other or grab gifts for loved ones. Oftentimes, these events also have other activities available nearby, like ice skating or photo moments, making for the perfect two-in-one date.

Bring out the creative energy with a cutesy arts and crafts moment. Grab a few blank ornaments, brushes and bottles of paint, stickers, fabric, and whatever else strikes your fancy. Set it all up on a flat surface (like a dining table or floor), throw in a few Christmas songs and snacks, and you’ve got the makings of a simple and fun at-home holiday date night.

Speaking of not making date night complicated, a simple stroll through your neighborhood (or perhaps one that’s more festive than yours) can still be made special. Make some boozy hot chocolates or hotty toddies, strap in to all your cold-weather gear, and sidewalk hike through your community. Bonus points for lots of hand holding, hugging, kissing and gossiping about the neighbors inflatable holiday characters in their front yard.

“Do you want to build a snowman?” Anna from Frozen isn’t the only one who can enjoy the feeling of holiday magic. Similar to ornament decorating and gingerbread house making, building a snowman is the ultimate nostalgia cheat code. While he might not come to life like Olaf, dressing him up with the cutest scarf and hat will still be just as worthwhile.

The intentionality behind handwritten notes will forever show true thoughtfulness. This idea allows the two of you to really get creative with one another and reflect on who in your lives you want to wish a happy holiday season. Whether you’re sending our joint cards or individual ones, let your heartfelt emotions shine through.

Can you technically do this any time of the year? Yes. But walk with me: What makes sporting events special for the holiday season is the amount of on-the-ground activations and curated experiences that teams provide fans. I’m talking seasonal menus galore, holiday-themed lounges, and activations where you can put your partner’s competitive skills to the test. It’s almost like a bunch of dates in one!

Warm up from the brisk cool air outside by attending a wine tasting at your local winery or private holiday-themed events. Wines with notes of cocoa, hints of spice, and earthiness are especially great to lean into for rich flavor. They also pair well with heavier meals that people typically enjoy during the winter months.

Love the snow but hate the cold? Search for the nearest igloo dining restaurant near you for a winter lunch or dinner. Usually, the igloos are kind of snug (but not in an uncomfortable way), giving you at-home levels of cozy with a space heater or bonfire. They’re also super festive with the decor, so you and your date will feel all the holiday cheer.

#6807. 3 Unexpected Places to Paint Before the Holidays, According to Designers

These small paint projects can make a big impact before guests arrive.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Pictured above: A living room area designed by Kristen Thomas of Ink & Canvas Design

A fresh coat of paint is one of the easiest ways to give your home a little refresh before the holidays, but you don't necessarily have to take on an entire redesign project to make an impact.

Some of the most interesting places to introduce color are the details you might normally overlook. They're relatively small surfaces, but a little paint can make them feel like intentional design features, adding character and definition without the time, expense, or commitment of repainting every wall.

Whether you want to make a statement, add a little contrast, or create a more interesting backdrop, there are plenty of ways to make a big visual impact with a relatively small project. Ahead, designers share the unexpected spots worth painting before the holidays and how to choose colors that feel right at home.

Window casings are an easy detail to overlook, especially when they're painted the same white as the walls and trim throughout the house. But introducing a little color around the windows “can emphasize the proportions of a window and give the room more definition,” says designer Sarah Cole. Even if you're not ready to go bold, she adds that “a subtle contrast can make a difference.”

For a more traditional look, Kristen Thomas of Ink & Canvas Design recommends pulling from a historical or traditional palette, pointing out that “colored trim was once a sign of status in early American homes” because rich pigments were costly and difficult to come by.

When time is tight before the holidays, this simple painting project can be achieved in a single afternoon. Just don’t forget the painter’s tape.

Don't overlook the design impact of the interior doors in your home—a hallway lined with plain white doors can feel a little forgettable, but painting one or two in a contrasting shade can give the space a more intentional look. Paint it early enough, and you may be able to hang a wreath from it.

Massimo Buster Minale, founder and creative director of Buster + Punch, believes an accent like this “can completely shift the character of a room.” And you don't necessarily need to choose a bright or dramatic color to get that effect. Picking one you love, rather than going all-in on a trendy shade, is key.

“I recommend choosing a grounding color and adding some whimsy with the small touches,” says Lee Crowder, National Director of Design and Model Experience at Taylor Morrison. Before painting, take a look at the doors in adjoining rooms so you can decide whether a single standout door or a more consistent color story makes the most sense.

If you have built-in bookshelves or cabinetry, you already have a natural spot for a little color. Rather than repainting the entire unit, try painting just the back panels. This also gives you the opportunity to declutter what’s already there. Cole recommends this pre-holiday spot because “it adds depth and helps books and collections stand out.” Her advice for choosing a shade? “Pull a color from the room's upholstery, rug or artwork.”

Thomas also recommends painting built-in shelving and cabinetry as a way to create a special feature in the room. “Painting built-ins sets them apart as a special feature and creates a beautiful backdrop for your favorite objects and holiday decor,” she says.

Instead of going for a bold contrasting hue, Vivian Robins at Arketa Design Studio suggests using a color that already appears elsewhere in the space, explaining that this small paint update can work as a “punctuation mark” in the room.

“Sometimes the smallest design changes create the biggest impression within a room because you're drawing out a color that lives in the space,” Robins adds.
