#6806. How to Read ‘The Hunger Games’ Books in Order of Release

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the mood to read some Hunger Games books after seeing Sunrise on the Reaping in theaters? Maybe you never visited Panem on the page in the first place. Here’s your guide to how to read Suzanne Collins’s dystopian young adult book series in order. (Remember, even though there are six movies, there are only five books.)

Collins’s trilogy was published in a timely succession back in the day. Then a decade passed, and all of a sudden the author surprised us with a new installment. Are there more Hunger Games books on the horizon? We certainly hope so. But for now, let’s be grateful for what we have.

In the world of young adult fiction, “what if you had to fight to the death with the boy in your class who has a crush on you” is about as perfect as premises get. The emotional stakes are as high as the external stakes. Katniss Everdeen was a survivalist before she volunteered to replace her younger sister in her dystopian society’s televised child slaughter. She could hunt and forage better than most of the people in her Appalachian hometown. What she didn’t know how to do was smile for the cameras and perform for the crowd if that’s what it takes to survive.

Katniss returns to District 12 in a PR relationship with her fellow tribute, victor, and ally Peeta Mellark. They’re miserable on the victory tour, unable to process their feelings and seeing the consequences of their actions everywhere. Then, a year later, President Snow announces that the third Quarter Quell will be Hunger Games: All Stars. The tributes are all former winners and adults. Are Katniss and Peeta simply back where they started? Not really. This time they have allies, and plans to finally move against the Capitol.

In the final book of the original trilogy, Katniss is being dressed up and manipulated for the cameras yet again. But it’s not the Capitol doing it this time. It’s the rebellion, led by the secret District 13. It’s scary to think about how even if you believe in the cause, you may find yourself using the same methods as your enemy. War has broken out in Panem, and Katniss and Peeta are separated. So much happens in this book that they had to split it into two movies.

Ten years later, Collins returned with a fascinating subject for a new prequel: the 10th Hunger Games, during which a teenaged Coriolanus Snow mentored District 12’s lost victor, Lucy Gray Baird, and fell in love with her while brainstorming how to get the murder reality show better ratings with his classmates. Their romance is not really one that you can root for, because we know we can’t fix him. He had bad vibes all along. But it’s a fascinating, twisted tale of how the Hunger Games as we know it came to be.

Next, of course, Collins answered fandom prayers by writing a Haymitch Abernathy prequel. We now know the story of every single District 12 victor’s games. Background details link District 12 women like Lucy Gray and Maysilee Donner to Katniss Everdeen in fun ways. It also serves as a nice companion to Catching Fire, with many appearances from the victors Katniss and Peeta meet during their own Quarter Quell. But be careful what you wish for, because this is a brutal and bloody book about the many ways the Capitol can edit and control your story using propaganda.

The party line is that Suzanne Collins writes when she is inspired to write, and not a second before. As of the release of the film adaptation of Sunrise on the Reaping, there has not been a new book announcement. But that hasn’t stopped the theories.

In the fandom, the character who probably has the most support in this realm is Finnick Odair. He’s sexy, confident, and has a tragically beautiful love story with Annie Cresta, a fellow victor from his district. They were also Career tributes. The districts that chose to buy into the Hunger Games by training children to kill haven’t been explored in depth in the books. There’s definitely potential there for a socially relevant story. What is that process like from the inside? Do they have stage moms? I am all for this, but just have one note: let’s tell it from Annie’s POV.

But there are so many other avenues that a future Hunger Games book could take. Collins could write about Cinna and the world of the stylists. That could include an update on President Snow’s cousin Tigris. She could leave the games behind and write a book about District 13. (Alma Coin origin story, divas?) What about the unknown, presumably dead victor of the first Quarter Quell? There’s also the Dark Days and the first rebel uprising in Panem. I wouldn’t mind a Plutarch story, as well...but I really think we need to hear from a female character this time.

#6804. Blade Runner 2099 Is a Beautiful Slog Through a Grim Future

The new Prime Video series picks up way, way after Denis Villeneuve’s sequel. Not that it matters.

Crowded streets. Long, cool coats. Flying cars darting through smog and neon skyscrapers. An abundance of Japanese aesthetics that imply America’s lost superpower status. Ridley Scott’s seminal 1982 masterwork Blade Runner (itself adapted from Philip K. Dick’s Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep?) laid the foundation for almost all of cyberpunk science fiction, so much so that everything else feels undeservedly derivative. Even a new Blade Runner. The new series Blade Runner 2099 looks the part, but it lacks the soul of its predecessors, leaving its perspectives on what it means to be more human than human hollow, like a Replicant without memories of its own.

Streaming now on Prime Video (and just in time for a long holiday weekend) is Blade Runner 2099. A stand-alone continuation of both Ridley Scott’s towering original and Denis Villeneuve’s remarkable legacy sequel, the series takes place in 2099 (decades after Villeneuve’s Blade Runner 2049). An unseen revolution has elevated Replicants to the ruling class of Los Angeles, rendering flesh-and-bone meat-bag humans as the new discriminated minority.

Hunter Schafer flexes some of her budding leading-lady chops as Cora, a real human posing as a Replicant to smuggle herself and her brother out of Los Angeles. Her ruse leads her to Olwen (Oscar-winner Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant Blade Runner spending the final days of her artificial life span seeking out someone from her past. A humorless buddy-cop mystery, Blade Runner 2099 is mired in a plot-centric narrative that cares less about the why of all things—an underrated and beautiful feature of its predecessors—than the what and how.

In other words, it’s the first time a piece of Blade Runner media actually zeroes in on the plot, instead of anything else more interesting. Try not to think of this as an eight-hour Blade Runner series but rather an eight-hour series stretching at the seams to feel worthy of its branded title.

Is it because streaming TV in 2026 has become so plastic and hollow that even Blade Runner 2099 feels like an unengaging slog? Or were the direction of both Scott and Villeneuve so immaculate that we were distracted from how turgid they might have been all this time? Regardless, 2099’s lack of auteurship is its most deafening shortcoming. The series has been steered to the screen by executive producer Silka Luisa, whose warmer color palettes—based on her choice of more sunshine, which glistens off surfaces—lend 2099 some identity. Some.

But beyond even that minor distinction, Blade Runner 2099 is still just a plot-y crime drama that checks off boxes and goes through the motions of its genre, with nary a real thing to say. About us, about our fragile humanity, about the demise of all we’ve built through artificial intelligence. Blade Runner 2099 is a dystopian sci-fi in all the ways you expect it, but it’s hardly a good one. Sure, the original movies were oppressive, dreary, and morbid too. But they possessed a knowing glare into our fears about the future and our imminent collapse as a civilized species. The new series lacks this same insight, or indeed any of its own, all in the name of goosing up engagement numbers for the closest thing to a real-life Tyrell Corp. (Do shareholders dream of electric quarterly reports?)

Despite surface-level similarities to the movies, plus a moody noir-like motif with a sexy saxophone that, admittedly, completely and unapologetically rips, the plodding Blade Runner 2099 is a needless walkabout through a world that never begged for one. Needless is a good word, actually. The show’s strong visual execution is negated by its inability to rise to the occasion as a worthwhile or enlightening probe of the human experience, or even just as an entertaining cop show. It may be smart, but what good is intelligence if there isn’t an ounce of grace?

#6802. 7 Home Organizing Trends That Will Be EVERYWHERE in 2027, According to Pros

Your clutter-free future starts with what you already own.

Although trends prevail in the interior design world, they also can find their way into other areas beyond decor—such as home organization. Creating orderly spaces can make life a lot easier. While tried-and-true solutions are still around, there are always fresh ways to tackle organizing projects.

As we look to the year ahead, four professional organizers are here to share what they think will be the top organizing trends.

While AI has been all over the place, from work to our personal lives—it appears to be tapering off, according to Lenora O’Keith, decluttering and organizing expert and owner of Aronel Home.

“AI and smart home solutions were everywhere at the beginning of the year, but there’s definitely been a lot of AI fatigue and a push for ‘dumb tech’ lately,” she shares. “When working with clients, a lot of them are over AI being added into everything. They just want simple solutions that work.” This can include things like setting up a household command center and labeling storage containers.

Moving has traditionally been a stressful experience that you were expected to figure out on your own—but that may be changing. Allison Flinn, owner of Reclaim Professional Organizing, says that more people are taking advantage of organizing services when they move.

“You can pack and unpack, but you won't truly have a fully functional and an organized new home if you haven't decluttered prior to moving or aren't intentional during the unpacking process by implementing a system that truly works for your lifestyle,” she says.

Instead of doing what a lot of us do—devising storage systems for each individual space—2027 may welcome the idea that you should treat your home as an entire unit to be organized.

Michelle Urban, owner and founder of The Organized House, says that people are increasingly not treating each room as its own one-off organizing project. She says that instead, they’re looking at how belongings move from one space to another and where they need to be throughout the day. For instance, items like backpacks, shoes, and sports gear may land in the entry, get stored in the garage, and need to be back by the door the next morning.

“When those spaces are planned together, the system supports the family’s routine instead of just moving things from one pile to another,” Urban says.

Viewed more holistically, experts are also seeing that organizing isn’t just an occasional task, but something to stay on top of each week. “I think more homeowners will begin to view organization as ongoing home maintenance rather than a one-time project,” says Ashley Bailey, founder and professional organizer at Everything Orderly. “Just like other aspects of maintaining a home, organizing requires occasional attention as our lives and needs change.”

Flinn says that more and more people are outsourcing household tasks to a home manager or home concierge. In addition to handling things like doing laundry, returning packages, and making doctor’s appointments, these professionals can also help you organize your home in just a few hours a week.

Eco-friendly everything is still as popular as ever, and it even applies to how we organize our homes. While O’Keith says that sustainability never really goes out of style, homeowners are increasingly turning to eco-friendly organizing products—specifically, by avoiding plastic and embracing natural materials like bamboo, rattan, and sea grass for baskets and bins.

But she says that there is a exception for basements and attics. “I always recommend heavy-duty plastic storage [for these areas],” O’Keith says. “Since those spaces deal with dampness, temperature changes, and pests, other materials could get damaged easily.”

While it can be lovely to live in a well-designed, beautiful home, if there isn’t function, you might make your daily life more difficult. That’s why people are going beyond Instagram-ready interiors and are wrapping in plenty of function.

Urban believes that progressively more people are bringing functional storage into a room’s design, and it isn’t just an add-on.

“A credenza can hold games in the living room; a dining room buffet can store wrapping paper and party decor between celebrations,” Urban points out. “As rooms take on more jobs, people want storage that looks like it belongs and does a useful job.”

As we head into 2027, people are thinking about their budgets more and more, which is why using items you already have as storage solutions is cost-effective.

“I'm seeing more clients interested in making better use of what they already own rather than automatically purchasing new organizing products,” Bailey says. For instance, she recently took on a project that transformed a playroom, but used items the family already had.

“Sometimes the best solution isn't another organizing product. It's rethinking how you're using the space and what you already have,” she says. “I think we'll see more emphasis on thoughtful editing and repurposing before purchasing something new.”

#6796. Roborock’s Smartest Robot Vacuum and Mop Saved Me Hours of Cleaning—and It’s $650 Off Right Now

It self-empties, washes its mop pads, and dodges clutter while you’re out.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I pride myself on living a busy life, but that means I have to work really hard to reduce cognitive load. I love having a clean home, but between running to events and keeping up with work, it’s hard to remember to. I didn’t think a robot vacuum was the solution until I read my colleague’s review of the Roborock Saros 10R. Seeing the way it took care of her hardwood floors, I was convinced to give the vacuum brand a try. I decided to test Roborock’s newest model of 2026: the Qrevo Edge 3 Pro Robot Vacuum and Mop.

Though the Saros 10R has some powerful suction of its own, I chose the Qrevo Edge 3 Pro for two reasons: its ability to lower into tight spaces, and its more powerful mopping speed. After over two months of testing, I’m thrilled with how much time I’ve saved. This is like having a cleaning service come through before I host; someone’s at home vacuuming while I’m out picking up dinner party ingredients.

Best for: Cluttered family homes or a single person’s apartment.

Not ideal for: A budget-friendly first vacuum purchase or a home with lots of stairs.

Price at review: $849.99, discounted from $1,499.99.

Bottom Line: This robot vacuum and mop is absolutely worth the premium for the time and headache saved. It’s great for homes with lots of different floor types, from hardwood and tile to scattered rugs of different piles.

Half of the genius of the Qrevo Edge Pro 3 is in the dock, so it does take up some floor real estate. The vacuum itself is just over 13 inches wide and three inches tall, and the camera mounted on top retracts for low-clearance access under furniture or cabinets. The dock itself measures 17.72 in × 15.83 in x 19.45 in, so be sure you’ll have that amount of space for it to live, plus room for the vacuum to exit and park itself.

The dock holds the mop pads, clean and dirty water tanks, and the dustbin that the robot vacuum empties into. On the underside of the Qrevo Edge Pro 3, there are dual spinning pads for mopping, and they can extend to get to your most annoying edges and corners. There’s also a sweeping tool and tangle-resistant brush to vacuum. A sweeping arm reaches out to stir up any dirt and debris before powerful suction takes care of it.

The camera on top is what makes it a smart vacuum. It scans and learns your home with AI detection that’s pretty spot-on, but you can make adjustments as needed. You can have it clean custom zones or specific rooms, so if there’s just one spill in the kitchen, it won’t head for the bathroom.

Is it too cliché to say it works like a dream? I didn’t know how badly I needed this vacuum until I had it. Its vacuum function is ideal for regular maintenance and surface-level cleans, and so helpful for last-minute tidying before the guests come. And the mopping feature is super smart, especially for homes like mine that vary between rugs, hardwood, and tile. The Qrevo Edge 3 Pro vacuums the carpeted areas first so your rugs don’t get wet. And if it needs to travel over rugs to get to hardwood, it’ll actually lift the mop pads up or physically detach and leave the pads behind.

When you first set it up, it’ll travel your home, making a digital map of the floor material, surface area, furniture, and any potential obstacles, like a power strip with long wires. You really need to clear the floor beforehand to let it do its best work.

Sadly, the map isn’t easy to adjust, so if you’re someone constantly rearranging your furniture, you will also be resetting the map. But if you’re like me and stick to one layout once it’s set, it knows your home like the back of your hand. And then it works quickly and accurately. Remember to leave closet doors closed or open, depending on what you want it to clean. This makes it easy to adjust if you don’t want it to wander into a closet—it won’t push open a door.

As it travels my apartment floor, it reliably dodges charging cables, shoelaces, socks, and any other trip hazards. If anything, it’s a little too cautious, but once it finishes a pass, you can see in the app what the vacuum considered an obstacle, and correct it if need be.

I love being able to see the exact path it took, so I know if there are any corners it missed. I set a few shortcut cleaning modes with set routines, like Bathroom Deep Clean when I want it to scrub the floors of my walk-in shower with extra strength. The “Happy Working Song” routine triggers a deep clean of the whole place.

This is a robot vacuum that lives up to its hefty price tag. It’s the kind of automation I used to picture watching The Jetsons. For one thing, it’s voice-activated. I named mine Giselle after the movie Enchanted, but all models activate when you say “Hello, Rocky.” I can give it a command like “clean the bathroom,” and it’ll set off on its way. And once it’s done with its cleaning routine, it takes care of every bit of maintenance. It self-empties into a spacious, sealed dustbin. It drains dirty water, washes the mop pads in boiling water, and dries them with heated air. So there are no soggy bacterial centers for me to deal with. All I have to do is empty the dirty water down the drain.

There’s a remote control option too. You can either look through the camera of the Qrevo Edge 3 Pro to see where it’s going, or control it like an RC car toy. This is why it’s my best tool for hosting—I can run out and grab the groceries while tracking where it’s headed. And with the included microphone, I can “call” Giselle and my voice will speak through the vacuum cleaner. I could speak to someone while away from home or check on a pet. It’s like a nanny cam on wheels. It also comes with its own light for cleaning dim areas, but I find it does its best work in daylight.

A robot vacuum is only worth its salt if it’s more helpful than it is tedious to maintain. I’m happy to say that’s the case with Giselle. After mopping, there’s no excessive water left behind. It dries super quickly. And it takes not even 10 seconds to empty the dustbin, mess-free. Washing the mop takes five minutes; however, drying the mop takes two to three hours.

A few notes on cleaning: you will want to empty the dirty water tank every time you run the mop. Because the tank is out of view, it can get gross in there if you forget to wash it out. I learned that the hard way. That’s the only step the Roborock doesn’t do for you. But light scrubbing with some Dawn is all it needs. You don’t need to change the dust bag often (I haven’t needed to in over a month of testing).

I have to provide a few disclaimers on my use of the Qrevo Edge 3 Pro. I live alone, so I can’t speak to how it interacts with kids or other adults in the home. I tend to just stay on the couch and out of Giselle’s way while the vacuum is running. I have short hair and no pets, so I can’t really speak to tangle resistance, either. I also live in a single-floor home, so it’s important to note that if you have multiple floors, you still have to manually carry the robot up or down the stairs. However, the Qrevo Edge 3 Pro supports Multi-Level Mapping, so it’ll memorize and recognize which floor it’s on and start the cleaning cycle in seconds. No need to recalibrate.

As far as volume goes: when I’m in the same room, I find it too loud for a phone call without some kind of noise cancellation. But when it’s in a different room, I forget it’s running. The loudest feature is when it’s emptying into the dock, which is a small price to pay for automation.

Roborock’s newest robo-vacuum and mop is definitely one of the more premium options on the market, but it’s worth the investment. The smart obstacle avoidance and self-maintenance features take away the stress of cleaning.

The Roborock Qrevo Edge Pro 3 is such a huge help for anyone who wants a cleaner home but doesn’t have the time to put in the elbow grease to clean every room. I’m so much calmer knowing my home is being cleaned while I work or multitask. It knows my home so well, even with so many different floor materials to navigate. I love this so much that I’d run this while I sleep (if I didn’t think it’d bother my neighbors). And with the current Black Friday deal running on Amazon, it’s nearly half off, so now is the best time to get one!

#6793. All the Details on ‘Phony’ Season 2 for Everyone Obsessed

It’s been a moment since a show’s had me more locked in than Phony on Hulu, and with just one more episode left, inquiring minds (me) want to know: WHEN IS SEASON 2? And also important: is there even going to be a season 2?

Phony follows high schooler Sonny Cooke, who wakes up in the hospital after a car accident to find that his mom seems...off. For starters, her facial features are ever-so-slightly different—but there’s also the fact that she doesn’t remember their special way of saying “I love you” (three hand squeezes), and the fact that she’s just acting generally weird. Was she swapped out by an imposter? Does Sonny have Capgras syndrome? Is there some sort of alternate reality happening? Hopefully we’ll find out by the end of season 1, but then what? Which brings us to...

Not yet. But given how freely the cast and creators are talking about a second season (and the fact that the show is pretty popular), it seems likely to get a sophomore pickup.

Yes...but it’ll also end on a cliffhanger. Creator Nick Paley told Us Weekly “I’m really excited about the cathartic emotionality of the [season 1] cliffhanger. This thing kicks us into the next story in this world because I don’t like constantly depriving people of information. I want this to feel like it means a lot and it feels very important to the characters emotionally. So that cliffhanger does both. Every episode has very big turns at the end. When you’re telling a mystery, it’s really important to show it to people who don’t know.”

Paley told the Los Angeles Times that Phony Season 2 will feature “a whole new story” after the season 1 finale delivers “resolutions and answers.”

The majority of Phony Season 1 was filmed from March through May of 2026, and it premiered in October—a pretty short production time. So, if the show follows a similar schedule for season 2 and starts filming this spring, we could be looking at another October premiere.

Potentially a LOT. Connie Britton, who plays Ellen, told The Wrap, "I think Nick has this thing laid out through probably five or six seasons. He's so clear about what the story is, and so I would be very surprised if he has doesn't have Season 2 all ready to go."

The best designers know when and where to shop at most home decor stores. For example, IKEA suits tight budgets and DIY-friendly spaces, while Anthropologie is perfect for a whiff of whimsy. However, if you want to treat yourself to some sleek, splurge-worthy pieces, consider this your sign to mosey over to Design Within Reach, or DWR.

Since its founding in 1998, DWR has been the ultimate resource for buying midcentury and modern pieces. #6788. Omega and Swatch Just Launched a New MoonSwatch—and No Two Are the Same

The Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake, the 39th watch in the series, will have collectors keeping an eye on the Swiss weather forecast.

By MoonSwatch standards, it’s been a quiet year. Swatch has been otherwise occupied with the Audemars Piguet Royal Pop, the May release for which people lined up for six days in New York, police deployed tear gas in France, and which, according to Swatch Group, has generated more than 25 billion views on social media.

Not that interest in the MoonSwatch has gone away.

An estimated 4.5 million have been sold since the collaboration began in March 2022 (and that figure only takes us to the end of 2024). In July this year, some 1.2 million people applied to buy one of the 1,969 examples of its $570 Mission to the Moon 1969, a watch that incorporated 11 grams of 18-carat gold recycled from vintage Omega components.

The latest MoonSwatch—only the third new model this year, compared with 11 in 2023—is the Mission to Earthphase – Silver Snowflake.

An all-black counterpart to last winter’s all-white Cold Moon edition, it takes as its starting point the idea that no two snowflakes are alike.

Swatch has developed a digital process capable of generating millions of different snowflake patterns, one of which is laser-engraved onto the moonphase indicator of each watch. So, unusually for a mass-produced quartz watch, every example is unique.

It also features the complication Swatch introduced in November 2024 and which remains exclusive to the MoonSwatch: an earthphase indicator. Where a conventional moonphase shows the phases of the Moon as viewed from Earth, this does the reverse. The two displays run in opposite directions over the same 29.5-day cycle.

(How useful this is to those of us who live on Earth is debatable, but it’s one of the more original ideas in watchmaking in recent years.)

There are some nice details elsewhere on the 42mm Bioceramic watch, including Snoopy and Woodstock in the snow, a pair of miniature skis at 11 o’clock, and a tiny patch of white on the Earth representing a snow-covered Switzerland, which glows green under UV light.

As with last year’s winter edition, there’s a stipulation.

The watch goes on sale at selected Swatch stores worldwide on Black Friday, November 27. After that, until March 20, 2027, the last day of winter, you’ll only be able to buy one when it’s snowing in Switzerland.

Swatch will be providing weather updates on its website.

#6787. What Exactly Is an Aura Gap Relationship?

It’s all about matching and complementing energies, and it’s one of my non-negotiable.

When I first heard about the social media concepts of age, height, and swag gaps, I was confused as to how they could possibly threaten established relationships, or generally anyone who was into someone else. After all, these are generally known factors.

When you meet a new love interest, there will always be characteristics that reveal themselves over time—that's what dating is. Whether you meet someone in person or online, you generally know what you're getting into. Sure, you can lie on Hinge, but one IRL date should usually sets the facts straight.

Unless you're Pedro Pascal in Materialists or Demi Moore in The Substance, there's not much you can do about age and height gaps. Swag gaps, on the other hand, are a little more amenable. You can become a guy's stylist, educate him on the lingo, spots, and social niceties needed to play on the same aesthetically-pleasing field. It takes brute force, hard work, and someone willing to give you carte blanche with their time and dime, but it is possible to close that gap.

What is far more dangerous, explosive, and slow-killing is the aura gap. But what exactly is aura? It’s basically the vibe you give off, from your confidence, to your demeanor, to if you’re warm and personable or cold and short. It’s something you’re born with, not something you can buy. Sure, could you learn social skills and etiquette, of course. You could even pay someone to make you seem like you have good aura online, but the truth will eventually present itself in person. You can manufacture an image, but you can't concoct the energy you bring into a room.

When we consider aura-gap relationships and their longevity, I suspect the issue is less about understanding someone's faults and more fundamental to a partnership's long-term compatibility.

The best long-term example I can think of is my parents. My mom is larger-than-life, extroverted, crass, and will leave most people in ab-clenching laughter. My dad is brilliant, detail-oriented, and gets anything and everything done. While those attributes complement each other wonderfully, it's the indescribable warmth and ferocity the duo exude together that makes them such a good team. Their personalities are different, but their auras are entirely compatible.

And I think that's where the distinction lies. An aura gap isn't necessarily about one person being louder, funnier, or more charismatic than the other. It's about whether the energy you bring into a relationship is reciprocated, complemented, or quietly diminished.

Bad habits, political and religious differences, and financial compatibility are all big factors on marriage checklists, but aura, to me, is even bigger. You can agree on where to live, how many children to have, and what constitutes an appropriate amount of money to spend on dinner, but what good is all that if the person sitting across from you makes you feel like a less interesting version of yourself?

As we date, the question should be: does the person opposite me radiate a consistent energy that not only complements my own, but makes mine better? Aura is my non-negotiable, and frankly, it should be yours, too. I'd rather be with someone who makes me feel more myself than someone who makes me feel like I have to be enough for the both of us.

#6786. 5 Thanksgiving Hosting Tips That’ll Make Your Dinner Parties Feel More Special Any Time of Year

Experts say the secret is using what you love—and skipping what you don’t.

For many households, Thanksgiving is the main hosting event they look forward to all year. But what is it about this holiday that makes it such an annual highlight, and how can you emulate that same essence more than just once a year?

We reached out to entertaining experts to learn which Thanksgiving hosting tips you can apply to year-round gatherings. These are the lessons that will make even your most laid-back get-togethers all the more memorable.

According to Whitney Ray, owner of Wyeth Ray Interiors, one reason that hosting Thanksgiving feels so special is that it's the one time of year most hosts actually break out their nice tableware. However, she believes you should be using these beautiful and sentimental pieces more often.

"So many people wait to use their best serving and dinnerware pieces at holidays like Thanksgiving, but I say those beautiful dishes should be used all year long," she says. "A Tuesday night dinner with a few friends doesn’t need to be formal, but mixing your nice china with your everyday pieces elevates a normal night into something more."

Hovik Harutyunyan is an event designer, etiquette specialist, and the founder of Harutyunyan Events. He believes that one key logistical trick a host can apply to any dinner or party they host is curating a menu full of familiar foods that are easy to prepare ahead.

"Thanksgiving menus work so well because the recipes are familiar as well as tried-and-true," he says. "For example, let's talk about the pies. Thanksgiving dessert is so easy because most of us opt for pumpkin and pecan pie. We know the dessert, and it's extremely easy to make in advance."

He also notes that it's best not to try out complicated, brand-new recipes for a big hosting event. You should opt for an elevated menu that is easy to scale and distribute across a large number of guests.

Thanksgiving is one meal where guests love contributing by bringing a side dish or dessert. While not every gathering needs to have a potluck element, there are other ways you can lighten your cooking load.

Christina Samatas and Renee DiSanto are principals of Park & Oak, and DiSanto is also the owner of Marcel’s Culinary Experience, a culinary community and cooking school. Both of them fully endorse taking a few shortcuts when it comes to hosting any big dinner.

"Not every detail needs to be homemade to feel special," DiSanto says. "There are many ways to save you a few steps, such as using pre-cut veggies or incorporating a few prepared appetizers or sides. Spend more time on the details that matter the most to you, rather than the little things."

For any event you hold, it's pretty much inevitable that guests always end up in the kitchen. "Whether you invite guests in or not, Thanksgiving shows us the kitchen is where people naturally congregate before a meal, whether they are wanting to help or simply catch up before dinner is served," Ray says.

For this reason, she says you should always plan to entertain in the kitchen at any gathering. Consider adding small bites and a drinks station on your kitchen counter or island, and try to clean as you go while making the meal.

Thanksgiving is a celebration of gratitude, and Harutyunyan believes you should apply the same spirit to any party. Anytime you gather with loved ones is an opportunity to give them a special experience.

"Thanksgiving, and the holidays in general, is one of the most magical times of the year," he says. "As stressful and chaotic as they may be, we love to gather with our loved ones to enjoy each other's company. We share stories, memories, and gratitude. The holiday spirit takes over all of us, which is what drives us to create such fabulous dinner spreads, decorate the home, and create an intentional experience for those on the guest list."

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

Tessa Cooper is a freelance writer and photographer based in Springfield, Missouri specializing in interior design, housekeeping, and lifestyle content. With nearly a decade of experience writing lifestyle and commerce content, Tessa has developed quite a passion for old and restored homes. In addition to House Beautiful, Tessa has written for Better Homes & Gardens, The Spruce, and Apartment Therapy. Previously, she was a contributing editor for 417 Home, her region's premier interior design magazine. Tessa attended Missouri State University where she earned her bachelor’s degree in public relations with a minor in design. She and her husband love traveling and have an affinity for France and Italy, but their restored Victorian home in the middle of America will always be one of their favorite places in the world.

#6781. The Affordable Mattress Brand I’ve Been Sleeping on for 9 Years Has the Best Black Friday Deal Right Now

It’s hands-down the best I’ve ever tested.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I didn’t have the best sleep setups growing up: I slept on rock-hard mattresses as a kid, then on a super-soft twin with zero support in college, plus the occasional sofa when I crashed with friends. When I got into my twenties, my poor mattress habits caught up with me, and I found myself dealing with random (or not so random) pains in my back, hips, and neck.

I had to be more discerning about what I slept on, and at the time, I was spending a lot of time at my boyfriend’s place, and he had just purchased a Nectar. It changed my life. Nine years later, we’re married, and we’re still sleeping on a Nectar. The Classic memory foam is the best mattress I have ever tested, and the one I keep coming back to because it’s affordable, has soft but firm-like support, and gets better with age.

Most memory foam mattresses start to cave in, turn yellow, sag, pill, or fray, but not the Nectar. It becomes more comfortable and molds to your body over time. The Classic bed has six layers, each of which provides a different perk, from cooling to support to motion isolation. We have a queen-size mattress at home, and I can hardly feel my husband get up or move around in bed. Plus, changing the fitted sheet is a breeze because the mattress isn’t heavy to lift.

I have tested mattresses that cost thousands of dollars and slept on countless beds at five-star hotels, yet I keep coming back to the Nectar as my ultimate favorite. The queen is only $699 right now during Black Friday/Cyber Monday, which is an insane steal. Typically, it’s $1,567, which still isn’t a terrible price considering most luxury mattresses will cost over $3k.

Over the years, we’ve managed to convince a few people to get this mattress, too, especially if they spend a night or two on it. After my husband’s cousins crashed on our Nectar Classic mattress once, they immediately asked what brand it was and bought one for their home, too.

A couple of months ago, I ordered one for my in-laws’ guest room, where my husband and I stay while we’re in town, because I could not spend one more holiday season on their super-hard mattress. Their old mattress gave me back pain, and I would just deal with it for the week I was there, but then I realized, why not just buy them a Nectar in King? They have the space in Virginia, and I would get to replicate my dream setup from home. Suffice it to say, this Thanksgiving I am spending seven days with them, and I’m already sleeping so much better thanks to the new Nectar Classic in our room.

Funnily enough, my sister-in-law and her husband were also complaining about the mattress in their guest room, and now I’m thinking...should I just replace theirs too with a Nectar? I’m sure my in-laws won’t mind, right?

#6775. The 50 Best Sports Movies of All Time

We’re long overdue for a reassessment of the genre’s best of the best.

If Esquire’s list of the greatest sports movies of all time was accompanied by a training montage, it’d look something like this: the entertainment editors hunkered down at home, night after night, watching (and rewatching) our way through the great canon of this beloved genre. Caddyshack, still a blast. Talladega Nights, again? Twist my arm. A first watch of Breaking Away, a blind spot no more. Remember the Titans, for probably the seventeenth time.

Smash cut to us fighting about which films were truly worthy of our final top fifty. A long debate about whether or not The Color of Money is actually better than The Hustler. An editor’s brilliant suggestion that The Fast & the Furious: Tokyo Drift is actually a sports movie. A mutual decision to cut Friday Night Lights because, really, does it deserve to be here if the TV show is the one we remember most?

Final shot: the writing. Writing. Writing. Writing.

Now, this is the part where you take it all in, rewatch one (or four) of your favorites, decide what you agree with, what you simply cannot stand for, and let us know about it. Let’s walk through the tunnel together, shall we? These are the best fifty sports movies of all time.—Brady Langmann

Burt Reynolds stars in The Longest Yard as the incarcerated former quarterback, Paul "Wrecking" Crewe, who was kicked out of the NFL after admitting to point shaving. While in prison, he convinces the football-obsessed warden to let the inmates play a game of football against the guards, even though the seemingly harmless activity only exacerbates the power dynamic between them. But unlike most underdog sports stories, The Longest Yard’s setting pushes themes of reclaiming your self-worth in the face of a cruel, authoritarian system much more than its competition. —Josh Rosenberg

The Pride of the Yankees arguably did more for the legacy of its star, Gary Cooper, than its subject, baseball legend Lou Gehrig, but it was 1942 for goodness’ sake. The film was rushed into production and premiered in theaters just a little over a year after the New York Yankees player died from a rare disease that would later be named after him. And if it wasn’t for Cooper’s incredibly earnest portrayal, the conventional biopic likely wouldn’t have had the same effect—even with recency bias and Babe Ruth playing himself. But twenty bucks says you can’t watch Cooper give Gehrig’s real-life farewell speech at the end of the film without shedding a tear. —J.R.

The first time the Fast & Furious franchise swerved from street races and highway heists came in the standalone 2006 sequel, Tokyo Drift—the first helmed by Fast Saga mainstay Justin Lin. Set in the world of Japanese drift racing (originated by motorcyclist Kunimitsu Takahashi and mastered by “Drift King” Keiichi Tsuchiya), troubled American teenager Sean (Lucas Black) moves to Japan to live with his father, where he gets caught up in the motorsport’s resurgent scene. Basically The Karate Kid on wheels, The Fast & the Furious: Tokyo Drift is about the only time these movies proved it didn’t need Vin Diesel and barbecues to be a thrill. —Eric Francisco

We know what you’re thinking: Clint Eastwood’s Million Dollar Baby exists. But allow us to make a case for his 2009 drama Invictus. Set in 1995, after apartheid, Invictus shows how the Springboks national rugby team—led by Francois Pienaar, played by Matt Damon—united South Africa through their victory (spoilers?) at the 1995 Rugby World Cup. One of their biggest motivators was Nelson Mandela (Morgan Freeman), whose democratically elected presidency was equally symbolic of South Africa’s journey to reconciliation. Where Million Dollar Baby was a deeply personal (and harrowing) tale of one woman in the boxing ring, Invictus is about an entire nation in desperate need of something to believe in. —E.F.

The Big Lebowski is a bowling movie. You might not think about that right away, especially since the Joel and Ethan Coen comedy starring Jeff Bridges starts with a California slacker who’s simply on the search for his missing rug. But amid all the crime capers and accidental kidnapping plots, the heart of The Big Lebowski is a film about a group of guys—including John Goodman and Steve Buscemi, a.k.a. the dream blunt-rotation bowling team—who just want to take down rival bowler Jesus Quintana (John Turturro). If Hollywood ever makes a traditional bowling movie as good as The Big Lebowski, you can bet that it will still be compared to The Dude every step of the way. —J.R.

How many sports movies actually created a team? Only The Mighty Ducks. In the original that started it all, Emilio Estevez stars as a hotshot lawyer whose community service leads him to coach a ragtag youth hockey team. During his state-mandated service, he rediscovers his lost love for the ice. There have been sequels and even a two-season Disney+ revival series—and Disney’s brief ownership of a real franchise, the Anaheim Ducks, who won the Stanley Cup in 2007—but the first movie is still undefeated. Ducks Never Say Die! —E.F.

In 2011, the MMA business was booming. Tickets were through the roof, the UFC was finding its place on primetime, and new leagues like ONE Championship took off. So it was a good time for Gavin O’Connor to unleash his smashing drama, Warrior. Tom Hardy and Joel Edgerton play two estranged brothers whose entry in a mixed martial arts tournament forces them to confront their fractured relationship. Warrior hits hardest where it counts, and functions as an early showcase of Tom Hardy’s brawny charisma. —E.F.

I’m not one of those sickos who wishes that rollerball was a real sport, but that doesn’t mean I don’t enjoy watching it. Especially when, in the dystopian world of the fictitious sport—which combines roller derby, handball, biking, and outright bloodthirst—James Caan is the GOAT. The ‘70s cult classic earns its spot in our rankings not because of the rollerball action (which is thrillingly directed by Norman Jewison), or its retro-futuristic set design, but because of its prescient vision of a sports league that is shrouded in rich, manipulative C-suiters and a controlling body that, well, only wants to control. —B.L.

Nobody respects the referee. And why should they? They’re always slowing down the game and making terrible calls. I mean, are they blind, dumb, or both, you know what I’m saying? Well, when fanatical baseball fan (and umpire hater) Bill Johnson (William Bendix) is forced to go to umpire school after losing his job in this silly 1950s comedy, we all learn a thing or two about why the game actually needs an impartial bystander who keeps the game clean. Even if he can’t seem to see the damn ball. —J.R.

Is the John Huston movie where Sylvester Stallone and Michael Caine play POWs at a German camp who form a soccer team, play said Nazis in soccer, and escape at the end of the game extremely cheesy? Yes, it absolutely is. But is it ... also sort of amazing to watch Stallone earnestly play goalie, Caine direct his squad like a seasoned Premier League manager, and Pelé—yes, Pelé is in this film—deke up some Bambi-legged Nazis? Also yes. I won’t spoil the method in which Pelé scores a goal, but it’s so stupidly fun that it solidified Victory’s place on this list. —B.L.

Want a sports-movie take that has earned me some flak around the Esquire office? Of the two Ron Shelton-directed, Kevin Costner-starring sports movies—Tin Cup and Bull Durham, of course—I much prefer Tin Cup. The golf comedy has it all: a perfectly sleazy Costner performance, the sly intellectualism of Bull Durham, a pitch-perfect Tex-Mex backdrop, a Cheech Marin-played caddy, and, most importantly, a Jim Nantz cameo. Its premise, which sees a problematic ex-pro take another shot at the PGA Tour, has more influence than you’d think; look no further than Owen Wilson’s Apple TV comedy Stick, which plays like a loving homage to Tin Cup, right down to the shaggy Winnebago. —B.L.

Dodgeball is, without question, one of the stupidest movies ever made. Even among the Anchormans and American Pies of the late nineties/early 2000s, this sports comedy starring Vince Vaughn and Ben Stiller about a group of average Joes who compete in a dodgeball tournament to save their gym from being taken over by a fat-shaming, corporate fitness giant has no business being packed to the gills with so many memorably hilarious lines. I wish we had a hundred more movies where Stiller is the antagonist, because his fitness freak White Goodman is one of the funniest villains of all time. Dodgeball even inspired ESPN to actually air bizarre sports on its fictional spinoff channel ESPN 8 “The Ocho,” and I can’t say any other sports comedies ever had that much of an impact on the real world. —J.R.

When I saw The Way Back, which stars Ben Affleck as a struggling alcoholic who is asked to return to his high school to coach its basketball team, I immediately felt like it was one of the greatest sports movies ever made. That was at a press screening in March 2020—you can see where I’m going with this. The pandemic washed out what should’ve been a genuine moment for Affleck and the film, because The Way Back is a stunning character portrait with one of the boldest endings in all of sports cinema. I said this to its writer, Task creator Brad Ingelsby, around this time last year. “I think it's one of the best performances Ben's ever given,” Ingelsby told me. “I really believe that.” That makes two of us. —B.L.

Breaking Away might just be the most underappreciated film on this list. How underappreciated? Well, all three of us hadn’t seen it until longtime Esquire research editor Kevin McDonnell alerted us to its greatness. And damn, was he right. On the surface, Breaking Away is a classic midwestern coming-of-age dramedy: Four teenagers (played by Dennis Christopher, Dennis Quaid, Daniel Stern, and Jackie Earle Haley) clumsily figure out post-high-school life amidst the class wars of Bloomington, Indiana. But Breaking Away’s Oscar-winning screenplay—which layers a climactic cycling competition on top of it all—is so damn smart, funny, and full of teenage heartache that you’ll wish Hollywood made more like it nowadays. Or, at the very least, more high-stakes biking movies. —B.L.

After The Wolf of Wall Street and Suicide Squad, Margot Robbie became Hollywood’s new it girl. But her Oscar-nominated performance as controversial skater Tonya Harding in the 2017 crime biopic I, Tonya, cemented her prestige status. The movie is based on the life of Harding, her contentious relationship with her mother (played by Allison Janney), and her connections to the infamous 1994 assault on rival Nancy Kerrigan. Robbie pulls out all the stops for a 10/10/10 performance, with an extreme focus on ice skating as a tougher, dirtier sport than the bedazzled outfits imply. —E.F.

Cool Runnings isn’t just a joke. As funny as a Jamaican bobsleigh team is—and was when the real-life team debuted at the 1988 Winter Olympics—Canadian actor John Candy (Planes, Trains and Automobiles) wouldn’t star in a comedy film in the nineties coaching a ridiculous sports team unless it also had some heart. Luckily, Cool Runnings has loads of it. The good-natured comedy for the whole family was a smash crowd pleaser. It took the focus off the Olympics’ superhuman competitors and proved that anything was attainable with the right camaraderie. —J.R.

Mention the Von Erichs to anyone of a certain age from Texas, and they’ll tell you how important they were to the community—and how cursed they might have all been. Based on true events, Sean Durkin’s musclebound tragedy follows the rise and demise of the Texas wrestling dynasty, the Von Erichs. As the owners and stars of World Class Championship Wrestling, the Von Erichs were on their way to immortality until a series of events tore them apart. Though The Iron Claw is packed with body slams and sweat, its story about the strength of brotherhood in the face of tyrannical masculinity makes it a transcendent tear-jerker. —E.F.

It’s to be expected for sports movies to be about dirty, dehydrated athletes. But only Bring It On dared to fix its cameras on the cheerleaders. An early 2000s classic and a cable TV staple, the movie follows the rivalry between an affluent suburban cheerleading team (led by Kirsten Dunst) and an inner-city Los Angeles squad (led by Gabrielle Union). After Dunst’s character learns her team’s dynamite routine was stolen from the East Compton Clovers, she works to come up with a new idea from scratch before a national competition. Don’t let the high-pitched cheers and pearly white teeth fool you; Bring It On is an honest-to-goodness examination of cultural appropriation and the class and cultural divisions even amongst American youth. —E.F.

Timothée Chalamet, who has a debilitating fear of heights, stood atop the Las Vegas Sphere to promote Marty Supreme. While the man went home from the Oscars without a statue, he still left behind one of the best, unlikeliest sports movies in recent memory. Set in 1952, Marty Supreme stars Chalamet as an ambitious hustler with a silver tongue (and orange balls) who is dead set on serving as America’s emissary in the most exciting sport of the postwar era: ping pong. Essentially Uncut Gems in pleated pants, Marty Supreme isn’t actually about table tennis, but about the hustle, and whether it’s in your favor or not, you have to respect it. —E.F.

I’m a sucker for Rounders. The 1998 indie gem starring a cooler-than-all-of-us Matt Damon and a peaking Ed Norton as poker sharks is still a brilliant watch. And I’d be remiss if I didn’t mention the ace up Rounders’s sleeve: A terrifying villain in John Malkovich’s Teddy KGB (the name still gets me), who you genuinely believe would drain the life savings of a guy like Damon’s Mike McDermott and not give a single shit about it. Give Rounders a watch today, and it’s hard not to feel how visionary it was in showing how young men’s lives are hooked, warped, and ruined by the allure of gambling. Remake Rounders today and McDermott might just have DraftKings and FanDuel as the most-used apps on his phone. —B.L.

The Karate Kid is one of the main reasons why I took up martial arts as a child (along with Power Rangers), and I imagine it was much the same for most nineties kids. Not just because Daniel LaRusso (Ralph Macchio) and Rocky director John G. Avildsen replicated Stallone’s underdog magic for teenagers, but because the film helped create one of the best spiritual mentors of all time in the late Pat Morita’s Mr. Miyagi. There are few training methods as iconic as “wax on, wax off” and few villainous plans more menacing than “sweep the leg, Johnny.” But if you master the former, and avoid the latter, you just might learn what martial arts are really about. —J.R.

How Warner Bros. ever convinced basketball phenom Michael Jordan to star in this half-animated/half-live-action Looney Tunes basketball movie is beyond me. Because, if I’m Michael Jordan, you show me any storyboard frame from this film during pre-production and I’m bolting for the door faster than the Road Runner. But I’m beyond grateful that the basketball GOAT saw the vision behind Space Jam, because it’s unbelievable how this film wasn’t just an achievement in animation but also one of the greatest sports movies of all time. Really think about that for a second. One of the most celebrated, family-friendly sports movies of all time stars Michael Jordan and Bugs Bunny. Who could have seen that coming? —J.R.

Kumite! Kumite! Kumite! The movie that cemented Jean-Claude Van Damme’s status as the coolest ass-kicker of the 1980s, Bloodsport is still a good time even if it can’t shed its beefcake cheese. It is based on the very tall tales (read: bullshit) of Frank Dux, who claimed to be a U.S. Marine and ninjutsu master who won a secret underground tournament in Hong Kong. Whatever. Bloodsport still rocks as an exhibition of JCVD at his prime. It is The Karate Kid for dudes cracking open mini-fridge beers, and it’s awesome. Fun fact: The original Mortal Kombat started out as a pitch for a Van Damme arcade game due to the success of Bloodsport. —E.F.

There are plenty of movies like Slap Shot in the sports-movie oeuvre: a great athlete, nearing the end of his time in the game, undergoes an existential crisis that somehow turns into one last great winning run. It’s just that Paul Newman did it best in Slap Shot. The 1977 comedy, which follows a mess of a hockey team that turns to outright violence to win games and boost fan interest (however morbid) is still an absolute blast to watch, especially when those damn Hanson brothers drop their gloves. Heated Rivalry could never! —B.L.

The WNBA was in its infancy when Gina Prince-Bythewood’s Love & Basketball hit the court. Sanaa Lathan stars as Monica, a girl with hoop dreams who grows up next door to Quincy (Omar Epps), the son of an NBA baller. As they grow up as childhood best friends before becoming lovers in college, Monica and Quincy eventually learn to square their relationship with their professional aspirations. In most sports movies, romance is a subplot for the main character. Here, love is the very name of the game. And it’s a slam dunk. —E.F.

You can think of Major League as a funnier, better Ted Lasso set in the world of professional baseball (and the Hannah Waddingham analogue an actual villain). Tom Berenger, Charlie Sheen, Wesley Snipes, and more fill out the roster of the Cleveland Indians. After a vindictive new owner (Margaret Whitton) buys the team and prepares a move to Miami, the players spend their final season dramatically improving their game just out of spite. Everything comes down to a climactic division playoff game against the one and only New York Yankees. It’s a cliché to call Major League a “home run” or whatever, so instead I’ll just say it has nice velocity. —E.F.

In my book, The Hustler is the greater billiards film over its legacy sequel, The Color of Money. (Sadly, I was overruled.) Sure, the latter has Tom Cruise. But the original black-and-white Paul Newman film better captures the stress of betting on pool, swathed in the smoke of cigars and just the right blood-alcohol level to concentrate without second-guessing yourself. Throw in George C. Scott as a professional gambler and Jackie Gleason as pool shark Minnesota Fats, and you have one of the most star-studded and high-stakes films of the sixties—all over the act of poking some billiard balls into table pockets. —J.R.

Never before has tennis been so exhilarating—and, we’ll say it, titillating. Luca Guadagnino’s meme machine of 2024, Challengers, raised the bar as an erotic sports drama about two former best friends (Josh O’Connor and Mike Faist) torn apart over a beautiful tennis ace (Zendaya). During their tense reunion at a rinky-dink tournament, they compete to settle things once and for all. Between Guadagnino’s dizzying camera and the dynamic duo of Trent Reznor and Atticus Ross going beast mode on the soundtrack, Challengers had us all spending one summer bringing our situationships to the theater. It was a weird summer, admittedly. —E.F.

Who can forget the Beast, or Wendy Peffercorn, or those long summer nights playing ball under glittering fireworks? The Sandlot is the ultimate love letter to adolescent, Americana-flavored nostalgia, where boyhood friendships are forged in the cauldron of baseball. Set in 1962 in the San Fernando Valley, new kid Smalls (Tom Guiry) joins with other neighborhood kids who occupy an empty sandlot for their own baseball games. Over a long and fateful summer, Smalls makes memories that will last a lifetime. But he’ll always be a square—the kid’s a square! —E.F.

If the word “manifesting” could be defined by any movie, it’s Rudy. Sean Astin stars as real-life former Notre Dame Fighting Irish football player Daniel “Rudy” Ruettiger, a young man with learning disabilities whose dreams of playing football for the University of Notre Dame seem out of reach. Somehow, Rudy gets his opportunity—but only after incredible amounts of patience, perseverance, and resolve in the face of those who want to bring you down. Next to Rocky, Rudy is one of the greatest underdog sports dramas ever made, one that can inspire just about anyone to get off the couch and make their own dreams a reality. —E.F.

Chariots of Fire is the most serious capital-F film on this list. The British historical drama was nominated for seven awards at the 1982 Academy Awards, winning Best Picture, Best Screenplay, Best Costume Design, and Best Score for its portrayal of two real-life track stars, Harold Abrahams and Eric Liddell (one Jewish, the other a Scottish Christian), who overcame prejudice to compete for their country in the 1924 Olympic Games. Vangelis’s score, and the scene of the track team running on the beach in slow motion, remain Chariots’ lasting impact forty years later. But don’t let modern pastiches and parodies of the film sour the original’s legacy. Chariots of Fire is as much a fascinating picture of England in between the world wars as it is one of the most inspiring sports films of all time. —J.R.

W.C. Fields once said that his one piece of advice for show business was to “never work with children or animals.” Years later, Kelsey Grammer said a similar thing when he agreed to star in the Cheers spinoff, Frasier: “No kids, no dogs, and no wives.” But if Walter Matthau followed that rule, we would have never arrived at The Bad News Bears—a heartwarming family comedy about a beer-slugging Little League baseball coach and his ragtag group of kids who have no idea why their parents force them to play baseball over the summer. But just maybe, this oddball group of losers can help us all remember that everyone deserves to enjoy the great American game regardless of which team runs around the bases more than the other. —J.R.

If Talladega Nights isn’t the most quotable film of all time (“Dear 8 pounds 6 ounces newborn infant Jesus, don't even know a word yet...”) it’s at least the most quotable sports movie of all time. (One more: “Chip, I'm all jacked up on Mountain Dew!”) One of Will Ferrell and Adam McKay’s greatest collaborations during their undefeated run in the aughts, the epic NASCAR spoof—which pits drivers played by Ferrell, John C. Reilly, and Sacha Baron Cohen in a gasoline-slicked competitive love-hate triangle—is a riot twenty(!) years later. Why has Talladega Nights stood the test of time? You could say it’s all thanks to Ferrell and McKay’s sharply written, gag-stuffed script—complete with an iconic Applebee’s scene. Or even the racing scenes themselves, which are downright thrilling. (Watching Ricky and Cal pull the slingshot move still gets me.) But it’s the tour de force comedic turns by leading men Ferrell, Reilly, and Cohen that make Talladega Nights a film that’ll hold this spot for another two decades more. —B.L.

Here’s what Ford v Ferrari is about on the surface: Matt Damon plays real-life auto designer Carroll Shelby who, along with Christian Bale as English racer Ken Miles, is hired by Ford to beat the dominant Ferrari at the legendary 24 Hours of Le Mans in 1966. While Ford is in it to boost car sales, Shelby and Miles are in it to win it, to prove American muscle has what it takes to compete on the world stage. But here’s what James Mangold’s 2019 masterpiece is really about: Can art survive under commerce? Look under the hood, past the gears and the minutiae of automotive design, and what you have are artists trying to exercise brilliance under breakneck pressure. Between that and its abundance of menswear inspo, Ford v Ferrari isn’t just one of the best sports movies or racing movies—it is hands-down one of the best movies, period. —E.F.

There is no crying in baseball, but there’s so much more too. Primarily set during World War II, when professional baseball players served abroad, an all-women’s league begins to satisfy a baseball-starved public. The movie follows the Rockford Peaches—coached by drunk, acerbic Jimmy Dugan (Tom Hanks)—who become one of the most popular teams in the beautiful but short lifespan of the All-American Girls Professional Baseball League. Geena Davis stars as the Peaches’ best player, whose relationship with her sister (Lori Petty) serves as the movie’s main emotional engine. A wholesome ode to sisterhood, to baseball, and to knowing the good ol’ days when you’re in them, A League of Their Own is truly in a league of its own. The 2022 Prime Video revival series, which ran for a single season, is also worth checking out. —E.F.

Released just one year before Field of Dreams, Kevin Costner stars in Bull Durham as “Crash” Davis, a veteran baseball catcher brought in to coach a hotshot minor league pitching prospect (Tim Robbins) with a “million dollar arm, but a five cent head.” Unlike Field, Angels in the Outfield, or any of the God-meets-baseball sports movie canon, Bull Durham establishes right away that baseball might be like a religion, but it’s also like sex. So, as Costner works on the brash rookie in the dugout, baseball groupie Annie (Susan Sarandon) finds success taming the bull in the bedroom. “The stadiums are like cathedrals,” Costner tells the team. “The hotels all have room service, and the women all have long legs and brains.” It may not be a conventional baseball film, nor a traditional rom-com, but Bull Durham is all about the mind games that get in the way of great play. And, of course, Sarandon’s undeniable sex appeal. —J.R.

Happy Gilmore is a living, breathing embodiment of everything that makes Adam Sandler great. The golf comedy is packed with heart, still-funny one-liners (“The price is wrong, bitch!”), and slapstick shenanigans that only the Sandman could pull off. Add a wonderfully sincere Carl Weathers performance, a pitch-perfect cartoon-villain turn from Christopher McDonald, and a still-great Bob Barker cameo and you have a generational comedy. Plus, the golf is still so believably tense that you’ll still second-guess every one of Happy’s putts on a rewatch. And I dare you to listen to Lynyrd Skynyrd’s “Tuesday’s Gone” and not get the chills. —B.L.

No living director understands the anatomy of a sports movie better than Ryan Coogler. (So much so that, selfishly, I wish he would make more, but that would mean Black Panther and Sinners wouldn’t exist.) You can see it in every single minute of Creed, which smartly recasts Sylvester Stallone’s Rocky Balboa as the wizened, ailing mentor to an upstart boxer played by Michael B. Jordan. Creed immediately established three things: 1/ Coogler and Jordan are the Scorsese and De Niro of our generation, 2/ Coogler wasn’t content to simply run back Rocky and rake in a modest box-office return, and 3/ Good lord, can this man craft a training montage that will make you immediately sign up for boxing lessons. You might not find a more thrilling five minutes than the “Lord Knows/Fighting Stronger” sequence, which sees Jordan retrace Stallone’s steps while forging his own legacy, in all of sports cinema. —B.L.

There’s an entire subgenre of sports films that tie religion to baseball. Hell, there’s even another film higher up on this list that may deserve the title of best baseball movie ever over The Natural. But the Robert Redford-starring film about a kid from the Midwest who is seemingly chosen by God to inspire people with baseball after lightning strikes a tree on his family farm and he carves a bat from it by hand still lives up to its mythical story. Every impossible home run that Redford’s Roy Hobbs hits in The Natural would be the greatest thing you’ve ever seen happen at a baseball game, made even more iconic by Randy Newman’s Superman (1978)-esque score. —J.R.

Few comedies have remained as quotable as Caddyshack over the last four decades. Sure, it helps that the screwball golf film stars a murderers’ row of Chevy Chase, Rodney Dangerfield, Bill Murray, and director Harold Ramis. But even outside of the joy of Dangerfield pulling a boombox out of his golf club bag and exclaiming, “So what? So let's dance!”, or Bill Murray relentlessly playing whack-a-mole with gophers, Caddyshack is a celebration of the good ol’ times—when all a summer blockbuster had to do was make us laugh, and it could. —J.R.

I’ll say this as many times as Tom Cruise yells “Show me the money!” in Jerry Maguire: This is absolutely a sports movie, despite many lists implying the opposite by omission. Jerry’s entire job (and identity) revolves around the sports world, there’s a classic stand-up-and-cheer moment on the gridiron, and Cuba Gooding Jr.'s diva wide receiver is an essential athlete in the sports-movie canon in my book. Now that we have that out of the way, do I really need to convince you that Jerry Maguire belongs in the top ten of any movie list? (It holds the top spot in our Tom Cruise rankings, by the way.) If you disagree, I guess Jerry is right: "We live in a cynical world. A cynical world. And we work in a business of tough competitors.” —B.L.

Where to start? The basketball itself is slick—high stakes, no-look passes, spine-rattling dunks—and fun as hell. Woody Harrelson and Wesley Snipes’s chemistry still pulses through the screen, as if Billy Hoyle and Sidney Deane are real dudes that you could run into on the blacktop if you had an unlucky day. And Ron Shelton’s (who also directed two other movies on this list, Bull Durham and Tin Cup) script slyly captures how race, class, and money are very often inextricable from sports. Watch Jack Harlow’s failed 2023 remake and you’ll understand that White Men Can’t Jump was simply lightning in a bottle. —B.L.

When Americans needed a miracle, they found it on ice. The legendary “Miracle on Ice”—when the U.S. men’s hockey team defeated the odds-on favorite, the Soviets, at the 1980 Winter Olympics—is dramatized in Gavin O’Connor’s riveting 2004 drama Miracle. Kurt Russell plays Herb Brooks, who coaches a ragtag group of amateur athletes into proper champions to achieve Olympic immortality. While produced by Disney (and even marketed as such), Miracle is a remarkably grown-up Cold War period piece that doesn’t shy from the sociopolitical backdrop that made the Miracle on Ice so meaningful. —E.F.

Twenty-five years after The Hustler, Paul Newman reprised the role of “Fast Eddie” Felson for The Color of Money—this time, with Martin Scorsese and a young Tom Cruise in tow. And thank the sports-movie gods he did. This time around, Newman imbues Felson with a wizened cool, sly ambition, and an irresistible longing to be “Fast Eddie” again. Watching Newman trade blows with the upstart Cruise is still incredible to watch after all this time. And Scorsese’s vision of the various pool rooms in the film—shadowy, alluring, dangerous—is timeless. —B.L.

There are some states in America where having a major league sports franchise doesn’t mean anything to the people who call it home. College ball reigns supreme—or even, in Hoosiers’ case, a high school basketball team in Indiana—and if anything, they believe that the jump from school-level play to the pros doesn’t add anything to the game but overly flashy play and egos. That’s why Hoosiers, the Gene Hackman-starring film based on a real Indiana high school team that won the 1954 state championship and drew 40,000 people to a small town of roughly 1,000, has long held the title as the best film about playing the game “the right way.” —J.R.

Most sports movies are about what happens on the field. Rarely are they about what goes on in the back offices. Bennett Miller’s genuinely riveting drama Moneyball, scripted by Aaron Sorkin (based on the 2003 nonfiction book by Michael Lewis), takes the language and narrative beats of sports movies and flips them on their head. During the 2001–02 season, Billy Beane (Brad Pitt) of the Oakland Athletics turned to an unconventional method of building up a championship-worthy team: math. Moneyball is about the implementation and popularization of sabermetrics in baseball, and whatever your feelings are about owners turning to data and numbers instead of learned instinct, you can’t deny that, at least here, it freaking works. —E.F.

Even in the realm of sports movies, Martin Scorsese and Robert De Niro deliver a fine American classic. Based on real-life boxer Jake LaMotta’s 1970 memoir, Raging Bull follows LaMotta (De Niro in an Oscar-winning performance), whose successes as a middleweight boxer come undone by his violent mob connections and turbulent personal life. Where movies like Rocky celebrate underdog victories, the elegant yet gruesome Raging Bull—a staple in film schools everywhere—shows that even the highest highs come with terrible lows. Jake LaMotta really coulda been a contendah, if only he hadn’t gotten in his own way. —E.F.

Out of all the movies on this list, Remember the Titans is the one in which—if you encounter it in the wild, whether it’s a cable rerun or on the TV behind the bar on a sleepy Saturday afternoon—you’re absolutely watching it until “Na Na Hey Hey Kiss Him Goodbye” plays at the end. Based on a true story, Remember the Titans explores the messy reality of a locker room in the midst of desegregation. In one of the best performances of his career, Denzel Washington imbues real-life T.C. Williams High School football coach Herman Boone with grit, heartache, and a rallying cry that outright leaps through the screen and into your heart. —B.L.

Rocky is the ultimate underdog story. Not just because a struggling, working-class man goes the distance with world heavyweight champion Apollo Creed. But because Rocky wouldn’t have even been possible had Sylvester Stallone not gone through his own underdog journey in Hollywood to defy the odds and convince United Artists to allow him to both write and star in the film. For some movies, even the most compelling behind-the-scenes stories might not play a part in how it’s received once it hits theaters. Rocky is different. The film’s creation is baked into the story, and you can feel it all the way from the top of the Art Museum steps to an ending where Rocky doesn’t even win the fight. Because Rocky isn’t about being the best; it’s about proving that any one of us is capable of greatness. —J.R.

If you build it, he will come. Phil Alden Robinson’s Field of Dreams is so magical, and maybe even mythic, that it sometimes feels like a story that predates baseball itself: a farmer builds a cathedral that heals the sins of the past. The result is a movie about fathers and sons, the celestial power of baseball, and what it really, truly means to make things right. Yes, Field of Dreams will make you cry, every single time. Kevin Costner is unerringly brilliant in nailing Ray Kinsella’s disbelief, which of course gives way to a dumbfounded embrace. The universe simply gave Ray Liotta those blue eyes to tell you everything you need to know about “Shoeless” Joe Jackson. James Earl Jones, if you had any doubt, is ethereal. Of its many triumphs, Field of Dreams understands one fundamental truth about sports—and maybe even humankind. It’s this: there are few greater acts of love in this world than playing catch with dad. —B.L.

Save up to 70 percent on mattresses, holiday decor, and furniture.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Wayfair’s Black Friday sale is in full swing, and as a first-time homeowner, it’s one of the first places I’m looking to invest in my home. The retailer is known for affordable furniture and other big-ticket finds, but I also look for Wayfair’s Verified badge—the purple check mark that signals a product has been evaluated by the company’s in-house experts for quality, durability, assembly, and value, with customer feedback in mind. Wayfair has thousands of finds on sale, and to help narrow down the options, I found the only Black Friday deals worth considering.

Whether you’re getting your home ready for company or updating a room, shop Wayfair’s Black Friday deals ahead. Prices and availability can change, so if a deal catches your eye, it’s worth considering before it sells out.

It’s not too late to shop for holiday decor—in fact, Black Friday is a great time to find festive pieces for your home. Wayfair has deals of more than 50 percent off on everything from tall, realistic-looking trees to vintage-inspired tableware for Christmas dinner.

#6765. Behind the Scenes of Julianne Hough’s Sudden Breakup Just Hours Before the Live ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Finale

Her new relationship reportedly “ended abruptly” right before the finale.

Julianne Hough has truly had a week. On Monday (November 23), E! News revealed that the Dancing With the Stars co-host was dating Peloton instructor Adrian Williams after the pair had soft launched their relationship on Instagram. But then one day later—just hours before the Dancing With the Stars live finale—TMZ reported that the “quite serious” relationship had “ended abruptly.”

Despite all the drama, Julianne showed up to the Dancing With the Stars finale and didn’t miss a beat (shoutout to her semi-sheer revenge dress), and now a source is dishing on how she handled things behind the scenes.

Speaking to The Sun, the insider said that Julianne “seemed totally fine filming” and “wasn’t distracted or upset. She didn’t seem emotional or anything like that.”

“She was focused on the job but she did mention it was a ‘whirlwind’ of a week with the romance becoming public,” they added. “She didn’t get into the details about why things ended. The feeling I got is that it was extremely abrupt and it hadn’t even hit her yet that her relationship just crumbled because she was so focused on the finale and doing a good job.”

The source didn’t have details on why the relationship ended so suddenly or on who called time, but did say that “now that the season is over, maybe it will hit her harder.” They also pointed out that she’s a total pro, which, true!
