#6760. Before & After: This “Generic” New Build Now Looks Like a Century-Old Historic Home

Playing off the existing architecture, the formerly all-white space feels packed with personality.

The best thing about this Winnetka, Illinois, home is that it looks like it has been around for a century, with the kind of bespoke details and fine finishes that befit a bygone era. The irony? It’s barely a decade old. When designer Hayley Kauffmann of Hayley Maureen Interior Design was brought on to transform the 4,400-square-foot new build, she was tasked with a hefty assignment: Make a perfectly nice house look a little less new.

For six years, the homeowners had lived in the home with their two sons, filling the rooms with furniture and mementos from previous chapters in California and New York. But while their collections told the story of a life well lived, the house itself wasn’t helping carry the conversation. Generic finishes, mismatched architectural details, and an abundance of white paint left the interiors feeling more like a blank slate than a reflection of the vibrant family who called it home.

The solution wasn’t a gut renovation, but something more like reverse engineering. Kauffmann stripped away decorative fretwork, analyzed trim details, and replaced flooring that interrupted the home's flow, all in an effort to set an architectural foundation that felt more authentic. “While color, texture, lighting, and thoughtful furnishings are what ultimately bring a space to life, they can only be as successful as the architectural framework around them,” she explains.

With the bones sorted, Kauffmann turned her attention to the couple’s existing treasures, using their art collection as a jumping-off point for crafting a palette that feels equal parts scholarly and spirited. The once-white living room became a chocolate-drenched retreat, complete with a soapstone fireplace and a vintage game table from composer Stephen Sondheim’s estate. In the family room, emerald green built-ins and a geometric-clad sofa play off a beloved Broadway poster, while the dining room trades heavy trim for menswear-inspired pinstripe wallpaper.

The result suggests a certain sense of history, without pretending to belong to another era. “Without the before photos, you might actually think there was an element of restoration to this project,” Maureen says.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Hayley Kauffmann, Hayley Maureen Interior DesignBuilder: Ratia ConstructionLocation: Winnetka, ILThe Space: A six-bedroom, five-and-a-half-bathroom home across 4,400 square feet.

Though it exists in the same location as it did pre-renovation, pretty much nothing in the family room suggests its drab former life. In an effort to “introduce more depth and give the room a strong visual anchor,” Kauffmann opted for a custom CR Laine sofa, clad in an outdoor fabric from Marseca Textiles.

The vintage Roger & Hammerstein’s Carousel musical poster inspired Kauffmann’s palette for the space, which included bold shots of agave green and goldenrod.

“This room was already the family’s evening game room, but we wanted to make it feel more warm, and inviting,” says Kauffmann, who installed a custom rug from Stark that followed the shape of the room. “To create a moody, welcoming atmosphere perfect for Chicago winters, we color-drenched the room in rich burgundy-brown while keeping the existing ceiling coffers. We also simplified some of the architecture around the fireplace and replaced the existing glass tile surround with natural soapstone, giving the fireplace more prominence and adding warmth and timeless character to the space.”

The family’s game table, where they spend many evenings together, now gets pride of place by the expansive wall of windows.

“The dining room remained in its original location, but we refreshed the space by removing some of the heavy fretwork paneling and introducing a suiting-inspired pinstripe to visually elongate the room,” says Kauffmann, who also repurposed the family’s existing breakfast chairs, giving them new life with a plaid Brentano fabric.

With an original kitchen coated in all-over white, the breakfast nook needed a hefty dose of charm. “We layered in a natural woven rug, bistro chairs, and a colorful striped Roman shade that ties back to the family room’s palette,” says Kauffmann. “The whole space reflects the influence of the family’s California life, bringing a relaxed, casual warmth to the home.”

“The entry and hallways already had beautiful trim work, but the spaces lacked hierarchy and visual interest,” says Kauffmann. “We introduced natural woven grasscloth wallcovering and a Roger Oates runner to add texture, warmth, and sophistication, creating a more cohesive and intentional transition between the home’s various spaces.”

Though the Kravet wallpaper in the powder room is a showstopper, the real hero of the space is the marble mosaic floor tile, a spec that was worth the wait—and stress. “We all fell in love with the tile for the powder room floor but learned it had a questionable lead time,” explains Kauffmann. “We were working up against a deadline for a big party the homeowners were throwing. After reviewing alternates, we decided to stick with the original and even considered installing something temporarily if the tile didn’t arrive in time. We may have been grouting tile the day of the furniture installation, but we made it in time, and it was worth sweating it out!”

“The primary bedroom deserved the same level of detail and sophistication as the rest of the home, while also feeling intimate and quietly dramatic,” says Kauffmann. To achieve that, she created a two-tone color-drenched effect, using a deeper blue on the trim and ceiling and a slightly lighter, yet equally saturated, blue on the walls. The existing bed was reupholstered in an embroidered Patterson Flynn linen, while block-printed drapery envelops the room, adding softness and texture while creating a “rich, cocoon-like atmosphere.”

Hayley Kauffmann is the owner and lead designer at Hayley Maureen Interior Design, serving the Chicago and Milwaukee areas. The firm’s experience and expertise in navigating the process—from design concept to interior construction to home furnishings—leave clients feeling confident and seen.

#6757. How Does Owen Wilson Find His Zen?

“I just believe there’s something more going on that you can tap into,” the actor tells Esquire on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.”

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Owen Wilson is a spiritual guy—and long has been. That’s why it fits so naturally that he would star in a golf series for Apple TV, Stick, in which his character, Pryce Cahill, is often searching to find his perfect zen. And as Wilson jokes, he’s started calling himself “Johnny Flowstate” on the green as he talks to himself through shots.

“I just believe there’s something more going on that you can tap into,” he tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The fifty-five-year-old actor, best known for his roles in The Royal Tenenbaums, Wedding Crashers, and Midnight in Paris, even admits that he found himself reaching into the beyond for advice from his late father, TV executive Robert Wilson, in a moment of pure panic before hosting Saturday Night Live in 2021.

“He was already gone at that point, but the night before I was freaking out like, I don’t know if I can do this. I was so nervous, and I was thinking a lot about my dad,” Wilson says. The two were incredibly close, and he reveals that his father actually worked with him and his brothers, actors Luke Wilson and Andrew Wilson, on several of their films. One of which was Wes Anderson’s Rushmore, in which their father wrote Bill Murray’s now-iconic church address to the students: “Take dead aim on the rich boys."

So, as Wilson found himself full of doubt and fear the night before hosting SNL, he recalls looking up and “literally asking [his father] for help” before falling asleep. “I woke up, just incredibly calm, and I had this conviction of this joke my dad would say that needed to go in my monologue.”

What was the joke? “That he was the charter member of the 3970 club,” Wilson says, “which stood for ‘three sons, nine high schools, seven colleges, and zero degrees.’”

“That wasn’t something he was proud of,” Wilson continues. “That was his way to cope with disappointment, but you could almost say that that joke was worth us having all those struggles in school because that joke has lived longer than another diploma would have.” You can watch the episode above as well as listen on Spotify and Apple Podcasts.

Luckily, it all worked out for the Wilson brothers. Next up, Owen is set to appear in season 2 of Stick on Apple TV and the Meet the Parents legacy sequel, Focker-in-Law with Ben Stiller. He also takes a liking to Esquire’s suggestion of a Christmas movie with another one of his longtime collaborators, Wes Anderson, in the episode.

“It’s one of the biggest relationships of my life,” Wilson says of his friendship with the director. “Had I gotten into one of the colleges that I applied to, I would never have met Wes. And there’s something I always say to my boys—the idea that you can’t do it alone. If there’s something going wrong, I know I can always call and he’ll always be there for me.”

#6755. Are You Ready for the ’80s Fashion Revival?

Rejoice: shoulder pads and opulence are back.

It happened suddenly: golden shades, huge shoulders, stacks of oversize jewelry. At Anthony Vaccarello’s swan song for Saint Laurent, it was all golden with big jackets, safari suits, and joyfully ruffled blouses fit for a Joan Collins-era Dynasty, but built for 2026. For spring/summer 2027, the ’80s have been a big focus. Valentino showcased jewel-toned gowns with piled-on maximalist pearls, and Giorgio Armani’s strong shoulders were straight out of a fashion history book. Mugler and Calvin Klein did strong silhouettes that alluded to the era. And even Louis Vuitton couldn’t help but look back to New York in the 1980s for its recent cruise collection, presenting models in stiff jeans and Keith Haring tops.

And why not? The ’80s were a time of experimentalism and personality, making it ripe to be mined for fashion inspiration and also less referenced than the forever hall of famer ’90s and Y2K style that fashion can’t stop loving.

The obsession has already started (or, rather, it never really ended) in the vintage world. Haile Lidow, founder of Lidow Archive, has seen a demand recently for her vintage drop waists and ruffle-skirt dresses in wild patterns. “I have so many in my collection that have gone neglected for years and are finally being noticed,” she tells ELLE. “Also, the ’80s power suits! They are back! Sometimes I update the skirt length if it feels too dated. We call it ‘minify’ but regardless, the requests for these are pouring in.”

For some, it’s about nostalgia, but for others, it’s totally new. Violet Sky, a 26-year-old Gen Z singer first became inspired to dress totally ’80s after watching the film Girls Just Want to Have Fun while she was in high school. “It was all the bright colors and just how fun everything was and the big hair,” she tells ELLE. “I just felt really drawn to it. I didn’t really have my own personal style, and so at that moment, I was like, well, why don’t I just explore this?” She started by pulling pieces from her mother and grandmother’s old wardrobe in her attic, and now exclusively wears acid-wash denim, tops bedazzled with rhinestones, and Reebok Classics every day.

“I think fashion, and interiors, and so many other aesthetic-based industries has been stuck in a really boring rut that’s been disguised as minimalism,” adds Lidow. “It started in the late ’90s as ‘chic’ and developed into an excuse for society to try less. The ’80s were the opposite: one bangle was never enough, you had to have four. Eyelids were splashed in bright colors and patterns were clashing all over the place. I think we’ve finally grown bored of the boring. We want to have fun again.”

Even fashion historians can’t help but think of the ’80s as a sort of golden age of fashion, when Vivienne Westwood and Christian Lacroix were sending highly creative pieces down the runway. Valerie Steele, director and chief curator of The Museum at the Fashion Institute of Technology, even associates the era with a shoe renaissance, which we’ve also been experiencing in 2026 with the rise of It shoes. “I think what we’re seeing in part may be a hope that we can come back to a period when fashion really was fashionable, because now we’re seeing so much dissatisfaction with fashion, and with the costs of fashion, not only personally, but also to the environment, and to workers and so on,” she tells ELLE.

The familiar feeling of power dressing in the air (and on the runways) with these ’80s silhouettes is deeply tied to the female gaze. “What you see in the ’80s is that it’s coming out of a feminist movement that was growing in the ’70s, and then in the ’80s became a somewhat different feminist movement, so it was more kind of, girly, economic feminism rather than a sort of leftist, ideological feminism of the ’70s,” adds Steele. “So instead of working-class androgyny, you had suits like men had. You had powerful shoulders, you had a look of Amazonian femininity going down the runway, and that resonated with a kind of booming market, and a period when maybe, maybe feminism was becoming more commodified, or that was certainly a period when people realized that they could market specifically to women by having things that look both powerful and sexy.” Who can argue with that?

“I think that bright colors and loud fashion of the ’80s is something that’s so opposite than what’s kind of like going on today at least in everyone’s everyday life versus the runway,” adds Sky. “People are kind of intrigued about that. And they want to maybe try something different.” The ’80s revival may be nostalgic, but its appeal feels entirely of the moment: it’s instant personality in an era of sameness.

#6753. Denmark’s Queen Mary Delivers a Trifecta of Designer Looks During Her Historic State Visit to Colombia

From a D&G pencil skirt to a Saloni maxi dress

Denmark’s Queen Mary is looking posh and joyful on day two of her historic state visit to Colombia—a trip which makes her the first Danish queen to travel to the country.

Today, the monarch was given a tour of the Gold Museum in Bogotá, and later walked through the city’s main square, Plaza de Bolívar, with Mayor Carlos Fernando Galán.

The official visit runs through November 26, and the queen’s agenda includes learning about the country’s post-earthquake reconstruction efforts (three months after a 7.4-magnitude earthquake struck Colombia) and ongoing sustainability projects.

To arrive in the country, Mary went sharp in a black Forte Forte single-breasted blazer with a frilly sky-blue Stenströms Darya blouse, which she paired with black trousers, Malone Souliers Marion 70 pumps, and black Gucci glasses, plus some gold jewelry.

Then, for this afternoon’s engagements, she slipped into a sophisticated business look, the highlight of which was a black-and-white tweed pencil skirt from Dolce & Gabbana and a black collared top from Red Valentino. To round out the ensemble, she reached for sheer black tights, plus matching stilettos and a leather handbag.

The royal performed another quick change for her evening engagement: a reception in the Biodiversario greenhouse at Bogotá’s Botanical Garden. The look was a breezy wine midi dress with a fun dotted print by Saloni. In semi-sheer fabric, it featured a high neck, long sleeves, a cinched waist, and a long skirt that reached her ankles. Black-and-white heels added the perfect contrast to the colorful maxi.

#6752. Why The Rings of Power Will Never Be the Same After the Season 3 Finale

A post-finale interview with the showrunners of the ‘Lord of the Rings’ series reveals what may come.

When the creators and showrunners of The Lord of the Rings: The Rings of Power embarked on season 3, they knew the endgame from the start: The forging of the One Ring. After two seasons in the Second Age of Middle-earth, which saw almost all the other Rings of Power forged—three for Elves, seven for Dwarves, and nine for Men—it was time for Sauron to forge the one to rule them all.

“We wanted a tighter, tauter, more profound season,” says co-showrunner Patrick McKay, in an interview with Esquire. “We wanted to start with a bang and never let up. We wanted to deepen our characters psychologically, and challenge them in new ways. You’re seeing the end of one part of the story, and the beginning of another.”

“We had to have Sauron forge the One Ring,” adds J.D. Payne, who also created and serves as co-showrunner with McKay. “To do that, all the storylines in some way are connected to Sauron’s attempts at world domination. That was the one that so much of our attention and focus went into getting right, and it took all of our departments working together at the top of their games to create.”

On Wednesday, just in time for Thanksgiving, Prime Video gave Tolkien fans a feast with the final two episodes of The Rings of Power season 3: episode 7, “Orodruin,” and episode 8, “The Doom of Men.”

In the penultimate episode, Sauron (Charlie Vickers) learns from the Balrog—who talks, apparently!—how to use the fires of Mount Doom to forge the One Ring. Then, with the power of the One Ring literally in his hands, Sauron mobilizes his army for battle against Pharazôn (Trystan Gravelle), the king of Númenor. But instead of a big, violent bloodbath in the spirit of, say, Game of Thrones, Sauron simply... surrenders. Or does he? We’re talking about a master deceiver, after all.

In a spoiler-filled interview with Esquire, McKay and Payne unpack a few of our biggest questions about season 3, including how and why the Balrog speaks (with actor Simon Pegg lending his voice) to why Pharazôn is actually one of the most consequential figures in the series.

This interview has been edited for clarity.

PATRICK MCKAY: One of the things we're constantly trying to do is go deeper into Tolkien’s life's work. He did not just create The Lord of the Rings, he wrote all this other stuff. He envisioned an entire world. It's his legendarium. When you do the deep dive, you find there are surprising things if you only know the book and film trilogy. The Balrog Gandalf faces down in Moria is part of this level of “super being” that is equal to Gandalf and Sauron. They’re Maiar. In prior ages, Balrogs could cast spells and command troops. They’re intelligent. They can talk, you just don’t see that one talk in that circumstance.

The idea of walking out onto a diving board and doing something risky, to show a different side of that being, was something that we felt compelled to do this season. It’s certainly unexpected. We’re always looking to do that. We don’t want to be familiar. We want to show you things in this world that you haven’t seen before.

Talk to me about designing a new One Ring. Granted, it’s a gold ring; there’s not much you can really deviate. But in what ways did you want to create a One Ring that belongs to The Rings of Power?

MCKAY: The books are pretty specific about the way it looks. It’s a plain gold band. If you’re gonna do that, it’s going to look like the One Ring you’ve seen in other adaptations. We did make a few modifications, just for camera. We broadened it to give it more chunkiness. But it is what it says on the tin.

How is season 3 a culmination of everything that’s happened before, and how is it setting up the rest of the show?

J.D. PAYNE: It pulls together everything we’ve been doing with Sauron and Galadriel from the first two seasons. In season 1, he befriended her and deceived her in the guise of Halbrand. In season 3, you see Celebrimbor tormenting him: “You’re never gonna be able to make a ring capable of dominating all the other rings—or dominating her.” Part of Sauron’s ambition to forge the One Ring, as a character motivation, is being able to dominate the person who said “no” to him over the last few seasons. You understand how crazy that must have driven him.

MCKAY: The show’s called Rings of Power, because the creation is the throughline of this entire era of Tolkien’s mythology. At the end of season 3, you’ve now seen the creation of all of them. That’s now the world we live in. Everyone in Middle-earth is a nuclear power, so to speak. Now we’re setting up for the next part of the story, which is, “What are the consequences going to be?” One thing in season 3 we really invested in was Númenor to set Sauron up as the bowling ball crashing into those pins. That’s sort of the beginning of the end of the Second Age. It’s a tragic story. The Rings have an impact on Middle-earth that is felt for thousands of years. Now they are in this world.

The finale is literally titled “The Doom of Men,” and it ends with Sauron “surrendering” to Pharazôn. Or so that’s what he wants them to think. Can you break down this moment? How is this Sauron’s big chess move?

PAYNE: Everything builds up to that. You’ve seen Gandalf getting through to the Dark Wizard and take out a massive portion of Sauron’s army at the last moment. So the game changes right from underneath him, and he has to make a calculation. Is he going to put all his chips on the table and lose his army in a stalemate? Or is there another way? The great thing about Sauron is that he’s amazing at constructing scenarios that are lose-lose. If he can’t beat Pharazôn on the field of battle, he reasons maybe there’s another way. That’s why the final moment is filled with tension. It’s not simply a surrender. Our Elves, particularly Galadriel, know Sauron so well. She knows this is not a defeat for Sauron, and he’s going to find a way to make this into a play for exactly what he wants. Those who know the lore know what happens when Sauron is taken prisoner to Númenor. We wanted that feeling of dread, watching Charlie Vickers have a hint of a smile.

One of the most consequential characters this season has been Pharazôn. In what ways does Pharazôn represent the downfall of man?

MCKAY: This is a major character in the mythology. We introduced him in season 1, he’s been sort of a politician gathering power. In season 3, he’s been king for five days, and he’s in his heyday. He’s in his golden era. But he’s driven by insecurity. There was this blacksmith we empowered, who got away, who’s now marching around saying he’s “Lord.” No, I’m the guy! There’s professional jealousy there.

We talk sometimes about how he’s Stalin to Sauron’s Hitler. You’ve got two characters who are quite full of themselves, and have a high opinion of themselves, which puts them on a trajectory. Pharazôn’s goal is, “I want to take on Sauron.” That makes him a compelling character because you watch the way he greases the wheels and maneuvers to bring about that end. We give a lot of credit to our actor, Trystan Gravelle. He leaps off the screen in season 3.

Speaking of showdowns, one of the biggest confrontations you stage in season 3 is between Sauron and Gandalf, which Tolkien never wrote in the books. What did you hope to discover by pitting these characters together?

MCKAY: The journey of Gandalf and his involvement with Ciaran Hinds’ character, Pollando, has real deep roots in some Tolkien deep cuts where he talked about wizards who went east and that their activities probably prevented the east from joining Sauron. The story comes from that. We wanted to dramatize that through some of the most iconic characters in the legendarium coming face to face.

PAYNE: Seeing the contrast between them felt like too delicious an opportunity to pass up. Sauron is seeking power at all costs, and Gandalf has been told by the Secret Fire, “To access the greatest power in the universe, you have to give up all other power.” So when Gandalf is offered the world by Sauron, he turns it down. If you demand power, it’s going to destroy you. Ultimately, having the One Ring, and forging it and trying to keep power, sets up the weaknesses of the Third Age that the heroes exploit to finally destroy Sauron.

One of my favorite hobbies is Magic: The Gathering, which has had both Lord of the Rings and The Hobbit as crossover sets. Are there any possibilities for a Rings of Power collaboration?

MCKAY: [Laughs.] No. This is the first we’ve heard about it.

PAYNE: We actually wrote a draft of a Magic: The Gathering script in the before times. It was one of the last scripts we worked on before turning to Rings of Power. But it was right before the Disney-Fox merger, and it kinda got lost there. But we love that universe.

Here’s what happened after Jan was censored on the live DWTS finale….

Jan Ravnik went slightly off-script during Tuesday night’s Dancing With the Stars finale, by which I mean he gushed “she fucking killed it” about Tatyana Ali. On live TV. On Disney+.

Mickey Mouse has been in a state of shock ever since, but the moment was honestly very sweet and endearing…though, apparently Dancing With the Stars producers didn’t think so. The Sun reports that Jan was pulled into “closed door talks” after swearing, and everyone was “furious.”

It would be a nice gift to give this season.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The air gets very moist in my house during the summer, and extremely dry in the winter. When this happens, my wood dining table cracks, my skin feels dry, and I get nosebleeds—usually when I wake up in the morning. As I said, the air gets very dry. To combat this and revive my parched, eczema-prone skin, I’ve been using the Carepod One Plus, which has truly been a lifesaver.

Every time a friend or family member asks about it, I simply say: “Get it; it’s so easy to clean and functions perfectly.” In fact, this is what I told my sister-in-law, who was asking me about it this week because right now, Carepod is running an excellent Black Friday deal on all its humidifiers with up to 38 percent off. The only cons of this product are that the Carepod does take up a decent amount of surface space unless you get it on a stand, and it doesn’t come in any other color except white. With the Black Friday deal running right now, I plan to scoop up another Carepod or two to give as gifts. #6738. Forget Plymouth Rock This Thanksgiving. American History Is Better Reflected by a Different Stone.

Instead of staring at a boulder in a hole in the ground, drive 42 minutes west to the town of Berkley, where our country’s complicated past is plain to see.

Let me send out a little Thanksgiving advice to potential tourists planning to visit us in the Commonwealth (God save it!) in the future. If some local tells you to go to see Plymouth Rock, tell them you have tickets for a four-day Duck Tour of the Freedom Trail. I promise you: Seeing Paul Revere’s house four times is infinitely more interesting than seeing Plymouth Rock once.

First of all, Plymouth Rock is a boulder in a hole in the ground. Oh, there’s a classical templish thing above it, but it’s still a boulder in a hole in the ground. Second, there’s almost no historical evidence that any pilgrim feet ever trod upon the rock in question. None of the contemporaneous accounts mention it. It was a nonagenarian named Thomas Faunce who, in 1741, 121 years after the Mayflower arrived, decided that this was the rock on which all the Bradfords and their shipmates disembarked. It moved around Plymouth a lot in the early years. In 1774, it cracked in two, which some folks took as an ill omen for the burgeoning Revolution. (Pieces of it are in the Smithsonian) The year 1620 was carved into the boulder’s surface in 1880. In 1920, it found its permanent home. In 1990, writing in The New Yorker, John McPhee wrote of the rock:

Even Alexis de Tocqueville, America’s greatest tourist and, therefore, its primordial sucker, fell for the Rock’s charisma.

Damn, Alexis. Have some Madeira and chillez out, mon ami.

If you want to see a genuinely interesting boulder, drive 42 minutes west to the town of Berkley, where you will find Dighton Rock State Park. In another templish building, there is Dighton Rock. It is a six-sided, five-foot-high slab of sandstone. It is also adorned with ancient petroglyphic carvings of uncertain origin. There are depictions of animals and an assortment of lines and whorls and mysterious designs. In 1680, John Danforth got a look at the Dighton Rock and not even his recently bestowed Harvard degree could explain them. He made drawings of the carvings he could see. (At the time Danforth saw it, the rock was partially submerged in the Taunton River.) It also drew the interest of local celebrities like Cotton Mather and poet James Russell Lowell.

Danforth’s opinion was that the carvings were made by the local Native tribes, and he thought it depicted an ancient battle of some sort. (If there’s anything like a consensus among people who have studied the carvings, it’s Danforth’s.) However logical that might seem, it didn’t tamp down speculation as the years went by. Down through the centuries, the carvings have been attributed to Phoenicians, Carthaginians, Vikings, Portuguese adventurers, Chinese explorers, and Japanese mariners. And, of course, inevitably … aliens.

However, it was a professor of psychology from Brown University who had the right of it, I think. Edmund Delabarre was a well-respected psychological researcher in the first half of the 20th century. He also grew fascinated with the study of rock carvings in and around Narragansett Bay. Ultimately, his researches brought him to the shores of the Taunton River and to the Dighton Rock.

Ultimately, Delabarre dismissed all the other claims of origin and concluded that the carvings on the Dighton Rock were probably made by a crew sailing under the command of one Miguel Corte-Real, a Portuguese explorer who charted about 600 miles of coastline in and around the Canadian Maritimes. In 1502, Corte-Real set sail for the western Atlantic, seeking to discover what had become of his brother, Gaspar, who had not returned from a voyage to Newfoundland. Miguel vanished, too.

Delabarre’s contention was that Miguel had made it to New England, and he argued that the inscription was a form of abbreviated Latin that proclaimed Corte-Real to be “king of the Indians.” In his 2017 book The Place of Stone, Douglas Hunter pretty much demolished all of the outre interpretations of the carvings, arguing that the carvings were of Native origin, and that the other interpretations were elements in the erasure of Native history and the legacy of bloody colonization, of which, ironically, the settlement at Plymouth was Ground Zero. The people who didn’t step onshore on a rock in Plymouth launched one of the first campaigns of the greater American genocide. As Hunter wrote:

So there’s American history on the Thanksgiving of 2026, ground up between two stones, one a legend with a counterfeit past, and another a mystery with a stolen past. The latter is by far the more compelling. Its history encompasses all of the history, good and bad, of this appropriated continent. Edmund Delabarre was wrong about Miguel Corte-Real and the Portuguese, but he might have been more right than he knew when he wrote that Dighton Rock was a kind of test. In the ambiguity of its carvings at the time of Delabarre’s work, a person saw in them what he wanted to see. Not all of it was pretty, or ennobling, or triumphal. But it's all ours.

#6734. Which Glossier Perfume Is the Best? I Sniffed and Ranked All 5 in Honor of Their Black Friday Sale

Breaking down Glossier You, Doux, Rêve, Fleur, and Soie—and exactly how each one smells on skin.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Before Glossier You had its sisters (Doux, Rêve, Fleur, and Soie), it was the most popular perfume in the world. Truly, I couldn’t walk down my college campus without the familiar scent wafting up my nose. More than the inside of a Forever 21 and Svedka Strawberry Lemonade, it became a signature scent of the late 2010s and early 2020s; like the Abercrombie and Hollister of the early aughts. You rewrote what perfume could be: subdued and skin-like, with a smooth freshness that just smells like you, but better. Since then, it’s been named one of the best perfumes by Cosmo editors and a 2025 Readers’ Choice Award.

All this fame led to its 2024 iterations, Glossier You Doux and Glossier You Rêve, both of which received rave reviews online and in the Cosmo office when I waltzed around spraying it to get everyone's take. In 2025, the brand expanded with Glossier You Fleur, a salty and floral iteration of the original. Now, Glossier has debuted its 2026 flanker: Glossier You Soie, a beachy white floral. But how do they all compare? Which scent remains on top? Reviews, right this way. But first: FYI, Glossier is running its Black Friday sale right now, offering 25% off everything, including fragrance. If you ever wanted to try Glossier You or any of its flankers, now’s the time.

All Glossier You perfumes have the same essential DNA. With ambroxan and ambrette notes that smell musky, sweaty, sweet, and warm, they're meant to be an upgraded, more effervescent version of your skin. But that's where the similarities end with all of the version of You, as they really do have their own distinct scent family and vibe. Keep scrolling for our full reviews (and ranking) of each scent:

Key notes: Salt, cashmeran, apricot, ylang-ylang, osmanthus, ambroxan

I was fully prepared to hate this scent, considering I'm not a floral lover. But Fleur isn't your typical floral perfume. Sure, there's osmanthus, but it's heavy on the apricot and salt, giving the scent a way more beachy vibe than garden-y one IMO. I saw someone compare it to the vibes of Call Me By Your Name: juicy fruits, blooming flowers, ocean air, all whipping through your hair as you bike along the Italian countryside with a lover. I see the vision. It's the sweet smell of yearning, baby.

I wore this perfume on a press trip with a handful of beauty editors from other publications, and everyone kept stopping to ask me what fragrance I was wearing. "Wow?! Glossier?!" They couldn't believe their noses. Not because other Glossier perfumes aren't good, but because this is so unique that they thought it'd be from some $$$ niche fragrance house. Of every Glossier perfume, this stands out amongst the rest. The only con is that it might not be the best in the depths of fall and winter unless I want a little escapism. But for spring and summer, this will have a dedicated space on my vanity.

Key notes: Palo santo, sweet violet, frankincense, myrrh, ambroxan, ambrette

Glossier You Doux feels like the rich aunt of the family, but in a "quiet luxury," reserved kinda vibe. Like wrapping yourself in a $2,000 cream sweater that feels like butter against your skin. Or the inside of an ultra-chic hotel lounge you need a membership card to enter. It's for the people who loved the original You in high school or college and want to level up their signature scent to match their big adult salary (without, ya know, paying a fortune, ofc—these perfumes are all under $80 for a 1.7 oz).

Cosmo editors were addicted to this perfume since it's so subdued (naturally, "doux" means soft in French). Truly, I didn't find a single team member who didn't like it. Editor-in-Chief Willa Bennett immediately spritzed this on her skin (bold) and loved how light it was. I picked up on the palo santo right away, which reminds me a lot of Le Labo Santal 33 (which makes sense, considering Frank Voelkl, the perfumer of Santal 33, is also the nose behind these two fragrances). Assistant news editor Sam Olson agreed, noting that it smells like if you layered O.G. Glossier You with Santal 33—"just without all the gaslighting and trauma of the ex everyone has who wears the Le Labo scent," she says. Real!

Shopping editor Hannah Oh messaged me the night after the test to praise the longevity too. "I just smelled my wrist, and HOURS LATER, it smells so yummy," she wrote. Best part about this fragrance to me is how versatile it is. I could wear this for every single occasion, any time of year, with any outfit, and always feel like it's right.

Key notes: Rice milk, tiare water, ambroxan, evening jasmine

Of course, a Glossier beachy scent fits the whole “clean girl” thing. This isn’t tropical or sunscreen-y. Don’t expect a perfume akin to a piña colada. Instead, Soie (which means “silk” in French) is a solar-inspired white floral perfume with notes of rice, milk, and water. There’s a creamy, savory undertone that makes this smell clean, but not sharp. It’s like flowers soaked in milk. Baby-soft and a little warm, but with a burst of energy from an opening bergamot note. To me, this smells like plopping your butt in the sand and watching the waves crash. There’s minimal sweetness, rather relying on the lactonic notes to create a creamy throughline.

Sounds like it should be at the top of the list, right? Well, it just doesn’t last that long on my skin. It would be fantastic for someone who prefers a super subtle perfume. But I just wish it didn’t disappear after a few hours and need to be reapplied. The performance just isn’t up to par with Doux and Fleur, but its scent makes up for this compared to the other two from the line.

Key notes: Iris, pink pepper, ambroxan, ambrette

Hey, it's an O.G. for a reason. Glossier You completely changed the fragrance game when it launched in 2017. You know those pH-balanced blushes and lipsticks that are supposed to look different on everyone? You is that, but in fragrance. It works with your skin to essentially enhance your natural scent, which is why you'll rarely ever hear anyone say that it smells "bad" per se. The soft, creamy, musky scent started a chain reaction of other ambroxan-heavy fragrances meant to mimic the smell of skin, but You will forever be my favorite.

At this point, You doesn't feel as groundbreaking as it once did, but it acts as a fantastic layering fragrance to boost the musky notes of whatever you pair it with. I actually love mixing You with all of the other flankers to add some longevity. But it's great with my other favorites as well, like Parfums de Marly Valaya, Amouage Guidance, and The Maker Lover.

I own a lot of perfumes (ahem, upwards of 200+), and You is one of the longest-lasting scents in my collection. I swear, I can spritz this on a T-shirt and still smell it after it's been through multiple washes. It's like magic. And on my skin, it'll linger for days on my wrist, even after taking showers, washing my hands a gazillion times, layering on hand sanitizer, etc.

Key notes: Buttercream, almond, iris, sandalwood, plum, ambroxan, ambrette

Think of Rêve, which means dream in French, as the ~365 party girl~ perfume of the trio. It's out until 3 a.m. drinking espresso martinis, then swiping on YSL Touche Eclat to cover up the remnants of last night's eyeliner in the morning. It was developed to be the sexy, "brat" version to You, with a delicious buttercream and almond opening that swirls into a juicy, tart plum that finishes on the earthiness of iris and sandalwood.

Just because it has some sweetness, though, doesn't mean it's sticky or too cloying. It's not a gourmand perfume in that it smells like a birthday cake or like you bathed yourself in vanilla. Rather, it's edible and succulent—like you wanna take a bite out of it. Associate sex and relationships editor Kayla Kibbe loved this scent and even referred to this fragrance as "the slutty one." Personally, I find it subtly sweet, as its overtaken by the ripe plum butter notes. It's like cough syrup, but the Halls lozenge kind that taste shockingly super yummy. Assistant shopping editor Jasmine Hyman says it smells like a cherry Jolly Rancher—juicy, mouth-watering, and candied.

FWIW, though, there actually isn't a cherry note here, even though every single staffer clocked one. I believe this is coming from the plum and toasted almond, which create a tart sweetness that adds a depth and ~sensuality~ that reminded us of other cherry perfumes we love (think: Tom Ford Lost Cherry or Kayali Lovefest Burning Cherry 48).

Immediately out of the bottle, many editors picked up on the fruitiness (lifestyle editor Hannah Chubb said it smelled like a sweet gum from her childhood). But on skin, it was a different story, with the sugary buttercream notes front and center, says sex and relationships editor Veronica Lopez. For ~science~, I doused myself in this perfume so people could sniff it on my skin too to see how it interacts with skin chemistry as well. And many found it softened quite a bit, especially since the projection isn't very loud.

If we had to choose, Fleur would be our favorite for how fresh it feels, while Doux is a super close second, given its understated elegance. But Soie and Rêve are steep competition. And ofc, the original You will always be a winner around these parts. It deserves a whole lotta flowers for how it changed perfume as we know it.

All four fragrances are available now at Glossier.com and Sephora.com. Not into blind-buying? Don't worry, you can smell IRL at Sephora and Glossier stores too.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with more than seven years of experience researching, writing, and editing fragrance stories.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan, where she covers skincare, makeup, hair, nails, and more across digital and print. She can generally be found in bright eyeshadow furiously typing her latest feature or hemming and hawing about a new product you "have to try." Prior to Cosmopolitan, she wrote and edited beauty content as an Editor at The Everygirl for four years. Follow her on Instagram for makeup selfies and a new hair 'do every few months.

#6727. Wayfair’s Fairytale “Corner Cabin” Is an Instant Backyard Charm—and It’s $1,000 Off During Black Friday

This is a pretty and practical addition to your outdoor space.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Avid shoppers know Black Friday is packed with unbelievably good deals on mattresses, vacuums, and furniture, but if you want to make the most of today’s shopping extravaganza, don’t forget to pick up a shed. There are some great bargains on backyard builds, and Wayfair’s Outdoor Living Today’s Western Red Cedar Penthouse Corner Garden Shed is at the very top of our shopping list.

Sheds might usually be placed somewhere in your backyard, but that doesn’t mean they should skimp on style. Decked with a pair of French doors, two flower boxes, and a shingled roof, this red cedar setup delivers some storybook charm.

Of course, there’s more to this pick than good looks. The red cedar used on the shed—which one reviewer says “smells so good”—is naturally rot-, decay-, and insect-resistant, so it’ll hold up nicely throughout the ever-changing weather. Meanwhile, two screened, lockable aluminum windows will offer some extra light and ventilation, so you can use it as a home office, poolhouse, or kid’s zone. With 467 cubic feet at your disposal, there’s plenty of room to outfit the space with workout equipment, a makeshift bar, or patio furniture in the off-season.

And don’t be fooled: Outdoor Living Today’s shed might look round, but it’s actually a five-sided structure with an angular point in the back. Thanks to its clever design, you can stick this “corner cabin” in the very back of your yard for a stylish, space-efficient setup.

Well, space- and time-efficient, that is. The brand delivers this shed as pre-clad, panelized cedar wall sections with all hardware included to help simplify installation. While one reviewer says it takes some time to assemble, the shed “looks beautiful when finish[ed].” “Great material, very classy, very practical,” said another. A third happy customer called the corner cabin “well-constructed.”

Best of all? It’s on sale. Depending on where you shop, Outdoor Living Today’s corner garden shed can cost up to $9,299; however, Wayfair’s selling it for $1,000 less during Black Friday. Sure, nearly $8,300 is still a little pricey, but remember: An accessory dwelling unit, or ADU, can boost your home’s value by up to 34 percent, making this a worthwhile investment.

#6713. The Case for Ending a Relationship Even When Nothing Is Wrong With It

During my sophomore year of high school, in the throes of the COVID-19 pandemic, I started a relationship with my first love. While I am known to be a Type A planner, I tried to live in the moment, experiencing all the butterflies, first-times, and ups and downs of adolescent affection, just like my idols in the best coming-of-age movies.

It wasn’t until the start of the college application process, two years later, that my love-blinded bubble popped and I realized we simply wanted different things. But looking back, I wonder how much of my break up decision was about the relationship itself, and how much was about the life I was looking forward to in college and beyond.

I think that’s common for a lot of young people in relationships. Many of us break up at young ages not because there’s something wrong with the other or the dynamic—there's no cheating, no explosive fight, and no obvious villain—but because we feel like we should. I’ve witnessed friends who are perfectly happy together in their early 20s call it quits for the fear of missing out on the future experiences that are a fundamental part of being young.

If I’m being honest, I think it’s good to expand your dating portfolio before finding your forever love; there's great value in diversifying. Different relationships introduce us to different versions of ourselves, revealing what we need, what we're willing to compromise on, what we never want to experience again and simply what we do and don’t lie. And while I admire the people who are “one and done,” I can't help but wonder how you could know what you want forever if you've only ever known one version of someone—mainly yourself.

In relation to my first love, I knew I was ready to end things, but remained in limbo for months. Not only was that a waste of his time, but it was stupid to waste my own. Looking back, I don't regret the relationship, nor do I regret leaving it. Since then, I've loved and been loved by different people, for different reasons, and learned something new about myself each time. I'm not saying you have to date half of your city before settling down, or that those who stay with their first love are missing out. For some couples, growing up together is precisely what makes their relationship so special and I would never knock something like that. But I do believe there’s also value in choosing to leave a relationship even if there isn’t anything wrong with it—not for the reason that it’s all you’ve ever known, but because you realize you want to know more.

#6705. Julianne Hough Defends Her ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Season 35 Style

Julianne Hough had a lot of fun with her looks this season on Dancing With the Stars, and isn’t paying any attention to critics who have come for her style, tyvm.

“I really feel like I’m at this next inflection of my career,” the DWTS co-host recently told Glamour. “When you get into your late 30s, you just don’t give a fuck anymore. You’re like, ‘Who am I? What do I want to wear? What makes me feel incredible?’ It has more intention rather than trying to fit into what’s hot.”

“I feel like I’m in my leading lady era, and I mean that in the actual projects that I’m doing, but also me as a person,” she said. “I’ve gone a little bit more vixen-y this year than I have girl next door because we’re trying to find that character. It’s been very strategic.”

Julianne added that “apathy is the enemy,” and noted that “The reason things exist and have charge and stay visible [is] people either love it or hate it. They still want to talk about it.”

Julianne’s been working with stylist Brad Goreski, who said he has “an awareness” of what fans are saying online, “and I love it. Any conversation, good or bad, around fashion is great. I think people weighing in—they like the hair but not the dress; they thought it was a bad outfit; they think she should fire me—is totally fine, because we’re really excited by what we’re doing, and we put a lot of thought and energy into it.”

Brad also said that “Jules ultimately brings the looks to life, and I’m glad that there’s another part of the show that people talk about. Not every person’s going to love every look. I know that from being a stylist for 15 years. And that’s okay.”

#6704. The Berkshires Are Made for a Soft-Life Getaway

Two hours from New York City, this corner of Massachusetts pairs farm-fresh finds and open-air beauty with restorative retreats

Every Saturday from May to November, Great Barrington has a popular farmers’ market. Lines of local Berkshirites, visitors, little kids, and friendly dogs on leashes gather under packed canopies to buy a vibrant mix of fresh fruits, vegetables, flowers, and more. An older woman overheard me talking to my husband—in town for our end-of-summer vacation—about honeycrisps and insisted we go to the opposite end of the market to Maynard Farms’s booth. The family-owned orchard carries a diverse range of apples and “amazing stone fruits” she swore our excitable toddlers would love. She was right—the boys devoured nearly all of the produce before we left the market.

Last August, in the final warm days of summer, we drove winding highways through Hudson River mountains to the westernmost area of Massachusetts: a lush and culturally rich destination composed of 30 towns and two cities, collectively known as the Berkshires and located just two hours outside of New York City.

Lenox, the town we stayed in, is a quaint enclave that boasts multimillion-dollar Cape Cods, luxury day resorts, golfing, country clubs, and farm-to-table restaurants. It’s an ideal place for a weekend getaway with friends, a romantic escape, or a family trip with your littles to touch well-landscaped grass.

We stayed in a newly built rental home, directly across the street from the Boston Symphony Orchestra at Tanglewood. In the evenings, if you’re within three miles of the parking lot, you can hear concerts from world-renowned artists. “Whether you're sitting in the front row enjoying James Taylor in the Koussevitzky Music Shed or sharing a candlelit picnic under the stars on the lawn, there is something magical about being here,” says Amy Aldrich, senior director of patron experience at Tanglewood. “We take great pride in creating a welcoming atmosphere where world-class music and the beauty of nature come together to create something truly special.”

Well-to-do families have long escaped the hustle of metropolitan New England cities to decompress in the Berkshires because of its natural beauty. Author Edith Wharton, who chronicled the sordid lives of Gilded Age elites, built her 16,000-square-foot mansion and expansive garden estate in Lenox in 1902.

Back in the day, when affluent housewives needed a respite, they’d go to the “spa.” I never fully understood the older meaning of the term until I visited Miraval Berkshires, a 380-acre resort that boasts ancient Eastern treatments, healing equine therapy sessions, meditation classes, macronutrient dining, sound baths, astrological readings, and much more. It’s an adults-only, device-free space, so disconnecting from stress is mandatory.

Equestrian activities were a common theme for us during the trip. HomeFarm at Undermountain, run by executive director Lori Pestana, is a gem of a property. The animal sanctuary is dedicated to caring for and maintaining the health of horses—but also houses goats, sheep, and donkeys. “It’s a place where life slows down, where people reconnect with nature, with animals, and often with themselves,” Pestana says. “Whether visitors come to ride, explore, learn, or simply experience the peaceful rhythm of farm life, they leave with something they didn’t necessarily know they were looking for—a sense of belonging, joy, and connection.”

Pestana’s small but dedicated staff offers riding lessons and a holistic view of what restoration can look like with a lot of care.

For dining, expect most restaurants to serve farm-to-table dishes that are sourced from the seasonal produce and livestock being raised in the area. You couldn’t possibly get a closer farm-to-table experience than Highlawn Farms, which has delicious cheeses, ice cream, and milk made from the same cows grazing its property. It’s a general rule of thumb in my family that we never pass up the opportunity to have a wood-fired pizza—Pizzeria Boema has a chic Scandinavian interior and New England quaint courtyard, serving the crispy, flavor-filled pies made with locally sourced mozzarella. And if you’re more interested in a fine-dining experience, Cello, run by couple Ryan and Kim Boya, takes a contemporary approach to comfort food. The 29-seat intimate restaurant has a thoughtfully curated menu of autochthonous plates like oyster mushroom pappardelle and a honey-nut squash served with goat cheese, pickled poblanos, granola, and a drizzle of hot honey.

Wildlife is ever-present in the Berkshires, from the calming transparently clear waters of Lenox Beach to the newly renovated aquarium wing of the Berkshire Museum in Pittsfield. Out the window of our car, driving from town to town, we saw peaceful creeks, grazing deer, and chirping birds. I soaked it all in, even stopping at the side of Glendale Road in Stockbridge to take in the beauty of the Housatonic River.

In recent years, because of the chaos of flying with toddlers, I’ve come to really appreciate the convenience of going to places just outside of New York City. I just want a soft life—or one as soft as humanly possible. With a good playlist on deck (we lean toward Afrojazz or Bad Bunny), we strap the kids into their car seats and roll. Open space and calm have to be on the itinerary.

There are many places we could go, but this experience in the Berkshires really stuck with me because it offered a quiet luxury that doesn’t position itself as high-maintenance or arrogant. There’s an ease and openness to this place, and if nothing else, an amazing peck of apples I’ll be looking forward to on my next visit.

#6695. Most Black Friday Sales Are Fake. These Ones Aren’t.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Internet is a deep, dark place this week, but we found where the deals are worth it.

How do you even define a good deal anymore? The whole point of Black Friday was for retailers to make us wake up at the crack of dawn the day after Thanksgiving, wait in line, and then fight someone over a TV that was 50 percent off at Sears or Best Buy. How did they do that? They would sell a few products (doorbusters) at a loss just to physically bring people into the store. Those deals were good, and everything else, which you would buy because you were already there, was where the money was made.

That is now a thing of the past. Lines and physical altercations aside, in the past 20 years there has been a real swing in the favor of big retailers. First, we created Cyber Monday (though most people do their Black Friday shopping online already). Then the deals started a week earlier. Now, they last all month. And instead of having door busters to get you in the door, retailers just have very aggressive online marketing programs. In fact, it turns out that most Black Friday deals aren’t even deals at all. In a recent study, it was found that one in three Amazon Black Friday deals provided no true savings at all—prices were marked up earlier in the year, only to be brought back down to a standard retail value. The big retailers like Amazon and Walmart are the most to blame for these tricky strategies, but you should still be judicious when looking at any so-called Black Friday sales.

The good news? Black Friday is still filled with great deals—you just have to know where to look and what to expect. A lot of big wins revolve around private sales at more niche, direct-to-consumer stores. For example, Filson, one of our favorite outerwear brands, is having a 25-percent-off sale for Black Friday. That’s not the 75-percent-off you might have wanted, and there are other times that Filson goes 25-percent off. But the best strategy these days is to think about holiday shopping: Just buy the things you already would, but do it while they’re on sale now.

You should be especially skeptical of tech products. The increase in all tech prices has made it easy for those brands to push a 10-percent-off sale when products were 10-percent-off the current price. This is the sort of stuff we’ve been watching as the sales roll around. Based on this and years of covering sales and knowing what’s good and what’s not, we determined these are the best deals of the weekend. These are the deals our editors think are actually worth shopping this Black Friday.

Mr. Porter has an absolute goldmine of a sale. Like sure, you can grab this Brunello Cucinelli Jacket for $6,500 at 50 percent off. But it’s also filled with basics from brands like On that rarely go on sale.

Fortela is our favorite luxury westernwear brand with Italian design sensibilities. If you pay full price, you get every dollars’ worth in quality and concept. But shopping its archive sale and saving 50 percent? That’s highway robbery.

A cashmere-blend overcoat for 30 percent off is a real steal. Use code BLACKFRIDAY before it sells out.

A few days ago, the Samsung Frame Pro was 50 percent off. That deal was real, and it sold out. Today the Hisense Art TV (which we’re fans of) is 50-percent off. These deals vary daily, but today, this is the one we’d buy.

Dyson deals are confusing as fuck. You need to be knowledgeable about the model you’re buying into, and here that number matters. You will be able to find models like the V8 and the V10 for super cheap this week—but that’s because those models are old and outdated. Right now, the best and newest Dyson cordless vacuum is the V16—with all the bells and whistles, it retails at $1,100, but it’s $200 off right now.

But as someone who has used every Dyson, I know the V12 is a damn good model. And while it isn’t the newest, it still has lots of the same features as newer models and will last you years. So the fact that it’s on sale for $400 this weekend makes it a truly good deal. Also, I can’t find this deal on Amazon. For the best deals from Dyson, I would always check its actual site.

As previously mentioned, we love that many of Filson’s jackets are 25 percent off this week. This is a good example of when a brand just wants to participate in a sale, so they’re willing to mark down popular items. This jacket is so good our commerce editor Luke Guillory endorsed it last week, calling it the best $250 jacket. Guess what? Now it’s only $187. We know for a fact that this deal won’t last past Black Friday.

It’s expensive, yes, but Begg x Co. is one of the best cashmere brands anywhere in the world. $500 off a new cashmere sweater is something that doesn’t happen until the end of the season. But if you buy it now, you still have six good months of wear to get in.

Water filtration has become very expensive, especially when it comes to reverse osmosis and a countertop version that looks sleek. We have been recommending this one for over a year at $500, so the fact that it’s $150 off for once makes it a pretty good deal.

Furniture on sale is a whole category we can’t ignore. Furniture markups are a real thing, and a lot of it has to do with quality and materials. Marble is overpriced for sure, but good quality marble is worth it. We love Soho Home’s marble, and this dining table has been one of its most popular pieces for a while. Getting it for almost $3,000 off when you’re not a member is a big deal.

If you’re on the home redo kick, then a brand like Le Maé, which is offering some of its best wood pieces at 50-percent off, is a great place to start. Made with high quality materials and oak, this is one of those furniture pieces you will own forever.

If you’re a camo guy or do any non-deal hunting, then you know that Tom Beckbe jackets never go on sale—but this is your lucky week.

#6691. The Scoop with Kolor’s Taro Horiuchi: On His Zara Collab

Welcome to the Scoop: a weekly email series in which I quiz fashion insiders on the stories of the week. This will be a way for the Vogue Business community to synthesize and reflect on the latest headlines and get a little inside scoop every Friday.

This week’s guest is Taro Horiuchi, who took over the creative direction of Japanese brand Kolor from its founder Junichi Abe, a couple of years ago. Before that, Taro was running his namesake womenswear label, which he launched after moving to France in 2010, as well as a menswear line called TH Product, founded in 2018 (the two merged when he took over Kolor in 2025).

Before all that, Taro studied photography at London’s Kingston University and fashion design at the Royal Academy of Fine Arts in Antwerp, alongside Demna and Glenn Martens, who were in the year above and below, respectively. The thread that seems to bind Taro’s work together is turning basic garments on their heads, which is also what he sought to do in his most recent collaboration with a little conglomerate called Zara. I called him up to discuss that and more.

On December 3, we will be launching a collaboration with Zara. We are hosting a pop-up in Tokyo on December 2, and the global launch will be the following day. It’s the first global collaboration we’ve announced since I took over as creative director in 2025, so I feel honored to be in this position. The collection will span womenswear, menswear, and childrenswear. It’s the first time Kolor is making kids’ clothing, so that was an interesting challenge for us.

Shots from the Kolor x Zara campaign. It’s the brand’s first global collaboration since Horiuchi took over as creative director from founder Junichi Abe in 2025.

What was it like designing children’s clothes then?

The core challenge for us in this project was expressing the brand without the high pricing and intricate details. We wanted to make good, interesting clothes regardless of whether it was for children or adults. However, there are constraints when it comes to making clothes for children, both in terms of the textiles you can use and because the accessories and the parts on the clothing are very small. That was definitely a learning experience for us, which we took on happily.

Can you tell me a little bit about the collection? What was the inspiration behind it?

So the theme for this project is “World Uniform”. It’s something we do at Kolor: take something basic and turn it into something a little different. Fans of Kolor are always on the cutting edge of fashion, and they are always seeking something new. But those who shop at Zara are looking for clothes for their daily lives. So our challenge was to find the perfect balance between familiar and inspirational.

Behind the scenes of the Kolor x Zara campaign shoot. The collection’s theme, “World Uniform”, reflects Kolor’s habit of taking basic garments and making them “a little different”.

For sure, there are those who go to Zara for basics, but I’d argue that’s changing because of all the luxury brand collaborations Zara orchestrates, including the one with Kolor.

One thing that really stood out to me through this experience was the very high quality of fabrics and operations that Zara offers. My family and friends wear a lot of Zara, though I personally haven’t, so I wasn’t aware of the way they work. But the whole process was very inspirational to me. Both the product quality and the speed of responding were quite remarkable.

I remember when Zara launched in Greece; I was about 8 or 9. My mother was delighted because she could buy cheap but nice clothes for us. I still have some of those sweaters from 30 years ago, and my son wears them now. Has it been fun to work with Zara’s manufacturing resources?

For sure. When it comes to any collaboration, it doesn’t make sense for me to make anything you can make alone. I am very happy to say we managed to realize something that Kolor couldn’t have made on its own, and I believe Zara feels the same way about us.

A look from Kolor x Zara, which launches globally on December 3 after a pop-up in Tokyo on December 2.

You’ve taken over Kolor from its founder. It feels like a tough gig to follow a founder who is still involved and alive. But if we look at people like Julian Klausner at Dries Van Noten and Haider Ackermann at Tom Ford, maybe that’s part of the success recipe? How do you work with Junichi Abe, and is there a lot of pressure to match him?

I’ll answer your second question about pressure first. Of course, I feel the pressure, and I also respect Abe-san incredibly. Whether it’s a recipe for success, I’m not sure yet. What I can say is that the core value and DNA of a fashion brand aren’t something you can build in one day. It takes time. All I can do is what Abe-san told me to do at the very beginning of my time at Kolor: “Don’t repeat what I could. Just make Kolor unique to you,” he said.

There are quite a few open positions at the big luxury houses at the moment, and we expect them to be filled any day now. Does the constant change-up in creative directors affect the way you and other designers work or view the industry?

Things change much faster than when I started in fashion around maybe 2005. As I was saying earlier, building a brand takes time because it needs to have a culture and a philosophy. The compatibility of the brand and the designer is very important too in all that. In that sense, speedy changes are not good because they don’t allow the two to get to know each other.

Kolor x Zara. Horiuchi says the collaboration let him make something Kolor “couldn’t have made on its own”, thanks to Zara’s fabrics and production speed.

Applications for the 2027 LVMH prize are now open. You won the Diesel Award at ITS 20 years ago. How necessary are those awards and prizes to young designers?

I graduated from the Royal Academy of Fine Arts in Antwerp in 2007, and I am still so thankful to Barbara (Franchin, founder and director of ITS) and Renzo (Rosso, founder of Diesel and founder and chairman of OTB) for the opportunity. Looking back now, this was a big turning point in my career. It wasn’t so much about winning the award, but more about the people I met. Demna was in the year above me at Antwerp, and together we organized an exhibition about Japan. At the Diesel award, I met Matthieu Blazy, who is now at Chanel. So, for me, those prizes and competitions are meaningful because of the communities they draw together. If you want to tell what the future of the industry will be in 20 years, you should look at who is entering those competitions now.

But I have a concern about the future of the industry. When I was a student, the tuition fees for Antwerp were around €1,000 per year. Now, for non-EEA students, it’s between €13,000 and €25,000. The people who can’t afford such high tuition will not be entering the fashion industry. Demna wouldn’t have been at Antwerp if the tuition was any more expensive. So maybe, one suggestion for the organizations that support those awards would be to focus more on scholarships and grants.

→ Baca artikel asli

The choreographer is taking on Spring Awakening’s Off-Broadway Revival

Look at you thinking ahead, you! Great question. To keep things short and sweet: It’s almost too late, but you can still beat the clock with a few methods. IMO, HydroJug tumblers are elite—they keep drinks super cold and get so many compliments wherever I go. This striped one is gorgeous!

Love an aromatherapy moment. And while bath bombs are great, these are perfect for the person who prefers showers (but still wants a spa vibe).

Calling all crafty girls—these DIY book nooks are so fun to put together and look adorable on a bookshelf. If your giftee has been wanting to lessen their screen time, this is the perfect pick.

Having your initials plastered on a bag might sound a little too schoolgirl at first, but on this iconic Longchamp? Suddenly, it’s the chicest personalized accessory. And I mean, you can pretty much peg it as one of a kind (wink!).

The girlies love jewelry, especially when there’s a little meaning behind it! A birthstone ring will add a personal touch to their everyday collection.
