#6685. The Best Japanese Restaurants in London, From Omakase Counters to Neighborhood Gems

Finding good Japanese food in London hasn’t always been the easiest task, given the city’s abundance of mediocre sushi takeaway spots and gaudy central London restaurants serving sub-par fish dressed up with too many bells and whistles. (No piece of sushi has ever needed gold leaf so liberally applied.) Historically, to eat well, you’ve had to splurge: it isn’t cheap, after all, to source top-quality fish in the British capital, and it’s still worth saving your pennies for a blowout meal at one of the city’s top omakase counters, such as Sushi Kanesaka or Sushi Tetsu.

But in truth, you don’t need to break the bank to eat Japanese food well in London: just take the well-priced lunch set menu at Kiku Mayfair, or the reliably fresh sushi at any Eat Tokyo outpost, or the excellent quality-to-price ratio at Bond Street institution Chisou. (Its former location next to the old Condé Nast offices on Hanover Square led it to be nicknamed the “Vogue canteen.”) And that’s without mentioning beloved neighborhood spots like Uchi Hackney or Jin Kichi in Hampstead.

From exclusive six-seat counters to beloved West End izakayas, here is our definitive guide to the Japanese restaurants in London that are truly worth the hype.

Located within the ritzy 45 Park Lane hotel, this Michelin-starred, omakase-only destination with 13 seats is as atmospheric a sushi experience as you’ll find anywhere in London. Step through the discreet noren curtain that marks the entrance, and you’ll feel as if you entered a portal straight to Kyoto, with the gently lit interiors—all hinoki wood, lime plaster walls, and intricate kumiko lattice work—serving as a delicate backdrop for the equally delicate flight of dishes that follows. Helmed by legendary chef Shinji Kanesaka, the 13-seat restaurant offers a masterclass in traditional Edomae-style omakase using an impressive array of locally sourced ingredients: a silky pot of chawanmushi topped with the briny sweetness of fresh Cornish crab; marbled, melt-in-your-mouth fatty tuna nigiri with perfectly seasoned rice sourced from Japan’s Yamagata Prefecture; a Scottish lobster presented on a board then whizzed up into shatteringly crisp morsels of tempura. With a price tag of £420 per person, it isn’t for the faint of heart—but for those seeking true perfection, it’s London’s ultimate bucket-list sushi destination. —Liam Hess

A true Mayfair stalwart, Kiku has been serving the neighborhood since 1978, making it one of the oldest Japanese restaurants in the city—and unlike some of its flashier neighboring sushi restaurants, Kiku’s vibe is one of calm, quiet elegance. It’s the place to go for a consistently excellent, no-frills sushi experience where the quality of the fish speaks for itself, whether it’s the salmon and tuna in a classic sashimi set, or rare delicacies like Hokkaido sea urchin and sweet prawn served on perfectly seasoned rice. The tempura here is world-class, with a light, lace-like batter, and the £40 lunchtime set menu—which consists of yakitori, tempura, sushi, miso soup, and a dessert—is one of the best deals in central London. The ultimate seal of approval? It’s a favorite spot among the staff at the nearby Japanese Embassy on Piccadilly. —L.H.

This low-key but stylish joint, which opened in 2024 in a bijou shopfront on Brewer Street, is the brainchild of chef Takamasa Mogi—also the founder of popular Mayfair spot Cubé—and serves up astonishingly good sushi to the 10 seats that surround its subdued but stylish hinoki wood counter. (Soho regulars will remember the building’s former life as a manga store and karaoke bar—it turns out there’s still a karaoke room downstairs if your omakase dinner ends up getting a little raucous.) I was especially impressed by the creative but not overdone nigiri, including smoky slivers of seared Korean turbot and Spanish tuna with Oscietra caviar, served atop pillowy beds of red vinegar-seasoned rice. Given the quality, it’s excellent value too: the lunchtime menu offers salad, seven pieces of omakase nigiri, and dessert for £51.80, while the dinner menu of two appetizers, nine pieces of omakase nigiri, a temaki roll, and dessert comes to £89. (They also do a well-priced pre-theater menu if you’re planning to catch a West End show.) —L.H.

Under former Noma sommelier Yuki Kaneko’s ownership, Yuki Bar has transcended East London’s small plates and orange wine scene. Within the low-lit, 20-cover spot soundtracked by disco J-pop, dishes riff on French and Nordic classics with Japanese inflections, like a sesame-studded take on oeufs mayonnaise, embracing home-style cooking and a choose-your-own-adventure menu of “snacks, littles, and larges.” A few portions of house-made potato crisps with sansho pepper and grilled onigiri kick the evening off, while rotating mains have included an aromatic chicken hot pot and blushing strips of beef tataki silken with soy sauce and mustard. Go with a group, be greedy, and get a few bottles recommended by Kaneko. —Anna Cafolla

Mayfair isn’t exactly short on high-end sushi counters, so it takes serious pedigree to make a splashy entrance here. One of the few people capable of that is Shogo Amamoto, the Fukuoka-born chef behind Taipei’s two-Michelin-starred Sushi Amamoto—a reservation so notoriously tough to secure that die-hard sushi-heads are known to plan trips to Taiwan around it. (Sushi mastery clearly runs in the family: his brother, Masamichi, helms Tokyo’s cult-favorite Higashiazabu Amamoto.) Chef Shogo’s London outpost forgoes the usual Mayfair glitz in favor of a serene, minimalist room centered around a pale hinoki wood counter and bespoke Japanese ceramics, and the focus lies firmly in classic Edomae culinary craft. Dishes feature top-tier seafood flown in from Tokyo’s Toyosu Market alongside standout European catches—translucent slices of French wild seabass sashimi with shiso flower and ponzu, a sweet seared Orkney scallop wrapped in crisp nori, or rich, smoky nodoguro whose skin crackles straight off the grill—and warm, perfectly seasoned rice. It’s purist without feeling oppressively solemn—and one of the freshest (in every sense) new additions to London’s luxury sushi roster. —L.H.

One of London’s sexiest counter seats (and that’s saying something), Ushio arrives from Fukuoka, on Japan’s Kyushu island. Its first European location, Ushio serves A5 wagyu omakase style (no sushi here!), specifically akami wagyu that has been imported from Japan. It’s leaner and higher in protein, with a taste that will unfurl across an incisive multi-course menu of wagyu every which way. You’ll get a card that tells you which prime cut of beef you’re eating with every course, and it’s certainly a journey. Some are barely kissed by the grill, others draped in a rich sukiyaki marinade or glazed in their signature tare sauce. A trio of tenderloin, tongue, and kurimi are delicately salted and presented gloriously as is. (My tip? Say yes to the extra curry course! If it wasn’t so sophisticated in there, I would be licking the bowl. Add the sake pairing and enjoy your time with the very charming sommelier too.) Finish with a (meatless, but still mighty) matcha choux puff. In a quiet, darkened corridor of Fitzrovia, the minimal interiors are stunning and sleek, just like the Fukuoka flagship, built around wood and stone with Ko-Imari porcelains and hand-built ceramics by Taeko Kajihara. And everyone who works there just seems cool! A spot that lingers long on the palate. —A.C.

Tucked behind a bright white storefront in Clerkenwell, this seven-person omakase experience is among the city’s toughest reservations to secure. Husband-and-wife duo Harumi and Toru Takahashi are behind the operation (the latter being the chef), both of whom are famously warm and welcoming, fostering a convivial atmosphere versus the buttoned-up formality of traditional omakase settings. The seasonal menu relies on what is strongest in the market that day, but expect sushi so fresh it melts in your mouth and impeccable sake pairings. The restaurant sits along Jerusalem Passage, an 18th-century path to the priory of the Order of St. John of Jerusalem, which later transformed into a pedestrian walkway linking St John’s Square to Aylesbury Street. —Nicole Kliest

If you’re craving sushi but aren’t in the mood for a formal omakase environment, Uchi Hackney achieves an exceptional balance. This ultra-cozy spot in a residential area of Hackney leans on traditional Japanese design motifs (linen, ceramics, wood), and even encourages guests to kick off their shoes and slip into a pair of provided slippers. The restaurant began as a delivery service in 2015 and now offers a high-quality menu in its brick-and-mortar location, serving both brunch and dinner. As for the dishes, expect a variety of hot favorites such as grilled pork belly skewers and lightly deep-fried tofu, and savor the selection of fresh sushi and sake to round out the experience. (An aside: Uchi Hackney’s intimate ambience makes for the perfect date night locale.) —N.K.

Set on the ninth-floor Liverpool Street branch of Los Mochis, arriving at Luna Omakase comes with a delightful sense of ceremony. As the evening begins, you’ll be guided with your group from the bar along a nondescript corridor to a set of sliding doors that are opened—with plenty of theatrical flourish—to reveal the 12-seater dining room, where a sculptural timber counter is lit with a lunar glow against a backdrop of glittering skyline views of the City. Over the course of 12 seasonal plates, the vibe is lively and conversational, as the sushi masters hand each pristine slice of aged fish—think melt-in-your-mouth otoro tartare with Oscietra caviar served with a cloud of smoke, or juicy king crab leg nigiri with feathery shavings of yuzu—and artfully balance traditional technique and off-the-cuff flourishes throughout the meal. (If you fancy it, you can also make the evening more spirited—quite literally—courtesy of the tightly curated list of rare sakes and Japanese whiskies that the team is more than happy to geek out over, and that will serve as an instant conversation starter with your fellow sushi obsessives.) It’s intimate and undeniably stylish, but free of the stuffiness that can make your typical high-end omakase experience feel a little intimidating. —L.H.

In operation for nearly 30 years, this beloved sushi institution now operates across eight outposts in London (plus six in Tokyo). For those who favor great value and generous portion sizes, this restaurant chain consistently gets top marks. Choose from a menu of sushi, sashimi, nigiri, chirashi, unagi, or opt for a bento box to get the full experience. Their latest creation worth dining out for? Wagyu with black tobiko (or a ‘sushi taco’, as it’s so nicknamed). —N.K.

This Japanese udon noodle bar opened its doors in Soho back in 2010 and has since expanded to three popular London locations, plus a shop offering provisions such as chilli oil and jars of Fukujinzuke. The original location is worth a visit for the freshly made noodles and dashi alone, but don’t sleep on the weekly blackboard specials either (previous highlights include grilled corn with miso butter and fried whitebait with shibazuke cucumber). Koya Soho is walk-ins only and is open from breakfast through to dinner. —N.K.

This is where you go when you want authentic Japanese food and a killer sake selection, all in a calm, relaxed setting. Chisou’s two central London locations—Mayfair and Knightsbridge—treat guests to seasonally changing dishes and a menu of wow-worthy raw fish, with the option to sit at a sushi bar or at an intimate table. And back to the sake for a moment: Chisou prides itself on having one of the largest menus around, with experts on hand to suggest pairings and special events like sake and British cheese pairing classes led by specialists. —N.K.

A charming, intimate spot tucked away on New Quebec Street in Marylebone, Roketsu recently relaunched as a rare example in London of kappo-style dining, where the chefs will whip up a customized set menu based on a conversation about your preferences at the beginning of the meal. The team of chefs here take a creative approach to classic Japanese cuisine, folding in a handful of French flourishes: most notably, perhaps, with one of the restaurant’s signature dishes, an opening bite of a crisp wafer filled with 10-day miso-marinated foie gras. (Other highlights included Cornish cod with a vibrant nori sauce, and flame-grilled wagyu beef with yuzu oroshi for a clean, citrus-forward finish.) With an open kitchen behind a hinoki wood counter, Roketsu is an elevated, classically Japanese dining experience—but it also comes with an element of choose-your-own-adventure fun that makes it feel genuinely unique. —L.H.

If you’re seeking a quirkier spin on omakase dining, head over to Notting Hill, where you’ll find the six-seat Juno hidden in a speakeasy-like space at the back of Los Mochis, the popular neighborhood Mexican-Japanese restaurant on Uxbridge St. The dining room’s delightfully funky, “Tokyo-meets-Tulum” decor—bamboo, seagrass, high-gloss wallpaper—should serve as an initial clue for what to expect here: the textures and flavors of Japanese cuisine are lent a fresh twist courtesy of a bold, Mexican-inspired toolkit of spices, acids, and smoke. (Think chutoro spiked with a sharp kick of fermented jalapeño, or red bream brushed with lime aioli and topped with a sprinkle of crushed-up Oaxacan flying ant for a surprisingly delicious note of nutty earthiness.) Bring your friends and make it a party. —L.H.

There are few more scenic spots for lunchtime sushi in London than chef Endo Kazutoshi’s rooftop restaurant, which sits proudly above the Raffles London hotel at the Old War Office—and trades the monastic restraint of Endo’s Michelin-starred White City flagship for sweeping views over Horse Guards Parade. Grab an outdoor table and browse a menu that bridges Japanese precision with Mediterranean flourishes, the latter a direct nod to the years Kazutoshi spent cooking in Spain. Most of the dishes are built for sharing, so order generously: try the tuna tartare on miso brioche, the yellowfin tuna “chorizo” (its smoky, paprika-laced punch is balanced by pickled tsukemono and warm pita), or the charred, melt-in-the-mouth octopus with miso, fava beans, and asakura sansho—it’s all rather decadent, but somehow never overly heavy. Come for the pristine, perfectly seasoned nigiri; stay for the golden-hour sunset over the Neoclassical rooftops of this stately corner of central London. —L.H.

Many of the best Japanese restaurants fall into two categories: no-frills neighborhood ramen and udon spots, or the kind of hushed, six-seater omakase counters where you might be frowned at for asking for extra soy sauce. Aki London, tucked inside a soaring, Grade II-listed former bank on Cavendish Square, couldn’t be more different. Designed by Francis Sultana, the dining room is all about pure, flamboyant glamour—think fluted marble columns, soaring coffered ceilings, and plush velvet banquette seating. This isn’t a case of style over substance, however: The kitchen leans into farm-to-table Japanese cooking rooted in Kyoto traditions, ranging from crisp, featherlight tempura rock shrimp to delicate steamed-and-crisped wagyu gyoza, to smoky, tare-brushed chicken thigh skewers straight off the robata grill. Whether you’re coming for a leisurely lunch or a late-night dinner, make sure to check out the subterranean cocktail bar first for a round of yuzu-infused martinis. (Aki is best enjoyed in the company of a lively group.) —L.H.

A neighborhood restaurant in the truest sense, Jin Kichi’s cult following makes booking a table a competitive sport. This tiny, two-floor restaurant is cozy, often crowded, and wonderfully authentic. While the yakitori is a major draw, the sushi and sashimi are of a quality that rivals the city’s most expensive counters. It’s the kind of place where the chefs know the regulars by name (and where said regulars might be the likes of Kate Moss and Harry Styles). —L.H.

#6682. The Best Black Friday Jewelry Deals 2026, Handpicked by Vogue Editors

We love a good Black Friday fashion deal as much as the next. A cashmere sweater half off? A pair of boots we’ve been eyeing all season, at a fraction of its original price? Yes, and yes. But while the clothing (and home and beauty!) discounts are certainly worth browsing, we find the best Black Friday sales in the jewelry department.

Black Friday is the perfect opportunity to invest in the pieces you’ve been dreaming about but haven’t quite been able to justify buying at full price. Maybe it’s a diamond tennis necklace you’ve had on your wishlist for months, a pair of gold hoops you imagine you’ll never take off, or a delicate bracelet that will look just as good stacked with existing favorites as it does on its own. And while there’s certainly moments indulge in an impulse, jewelry is one category where planning pays off. Fine jewelry, in particular, adds up—so when our favorite brands offer a discount, we pay attention.

With the holidays around the corner, we’re not exclusively shopping for ourselves. Jewelry is also one of the easiest places to start checking names off the gift lists. Unlike clothing, there’s no need to worry about sizing (at least not with necklaces, bracelets, and earrings), and we can’t think of many people who would object to adding another beautiful bauble to their jewelry box. Whether it's a personalized pendant for your best friend, a pair of sparkling earrings for your mom, or something special for your partner, a thoughtfully chosen piece of jewelry always feels personal.

Ahead, we’ve rounded up the best Black Friday jewelry deals from the brands we love and wear ourselves. You'll find a mix of everyday staples we reach for on repeat, fashion-forward designs from designer names, and fine and demi-fine classics worth investing in. #6678. Sarah Pidgeon Ticks Off Two of Winter’s Biggest Outerwear Trends in One Viral Coat

It’s been a banner year for Sarah Pidgeon’s outerwear wardrobe. The actor, who made her debut on Vogue’s Best Dressed list this week, has spent much of 2026 investing in a really good collection of coats. And while playing Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy in Love Story has furnished Pidgeon with an extensive knowledge of the late fashion publicist’s preferred ’90s silhouettes, her own tastes are often a little more irreverent and varied.

Taking in a Knicks and Cavaliers game at Madison Square Garden in New York this week, Pidgeon ticked off two of the season’s biggest outerwear trends—animal print and flourishes of faux fur, as well as the sophisticated standing collar shape—in one covetable coat. The actor wore the Dolly Maxi by Carelli, an elongated iteration of the label’s cult jacket, with cream-and-black spotted, pony hair-effect fabric, a jaw-grazing collar, and fluffy chocolate-brown cuffs. Pidgeon let the coat do most of the talking, adding straight-leg blue jeans, pointy black boots, and her trusty black Balenciaga Le City bag, which she clutched under her arm.

Carelli is one of the runaway fashion success stories of 2026. Founded by Anna Carels in early 2025, the brand has amassed a celebrity clientele that includes Dua Lipa, Olivia Dean, and Peggy Gou, as well as legions of fans on TikTok. As Carels told British Vogue earlier this month, the original Dolly sold out in under an hour. The designer’s penchant for old-school glamour chimes with a broader appetite for rather louche, vintage-inflected outerwear: See Conner Ives’s decadent fur-trimmed creations and Charlotte Simone’s always sell-out fuzzy numbers, as well as the proliferation of vintage silk opera coats on everyone from Kendall Jenner to Hailey Bieber and Jennifer Lawrence. After several seasons of austere and minimalist coats, we’re all in the mood for something a little more flamboyant. (Vogue’s predictions for the hottest coats of the coldest months says just as much.)

Pidgeon is no stranger to the timeless pleasures of a leopard-print coat. During her Love Story press tour, the actor stepped out in Adam Lippes’s leopard chenille Margaux coat in a subtle nod to Bessette-Kennedy’s own affinity for animal print. She’s also made a habit of punctuating her more pared-back outfits with leopard-print accessories.

Elsewhere in her enviable outerwear rotation? A collarless leather trench from Aflalo, a sculptural peplum jacket from Celine, and the supple Prada leathers she sported during fashion month.

Working with stylist Emma Jade Morrison, Pidgeon has struck an interesting balance between the restraint that characterized CBK’s wardrobe and her own more adventurous inclinations. She has an eye for younger labels, too, and a knack for assimilating their more idiosyncratic designs into an otherwise unfussy wardrobe of great denim, white tees, and ’90s-coded accessories. The Carelli coat feels especially at home among them. Consider Sarah Pidgeon’s latest outing some inspiration: swap out that sensible black winter coat in favor of something with a little more personality. Match point!

#6676. Queen Letizia Makes the Case for a Two-Shoe Kind of Day

If you’ve ever peeked inside the capacious tote bag of a fashion editor or discerning corporate queen, you’ll know the merits of carrying a second pair of shoes. (Especially at this time of year, when a day at the office might segue into several festive engagements.) It seems that Queen Letizia of Spain is also well versed in the art of the midday shoe swap, albeit with a slightly more sophisticated, regal approach.

On Wednesday, the Spanish monarch joined King Felipe VI and the Austrian president and first lady, Alexander Van der Bellen and Doris Schmidauer, to inaugurate the Mariana of Austria exhibition at Madrid’s Museo Nacional del Prado, before hosting a luncheon at Zarzuela Palace. Rather than changing outfits between engagements, Letizia switched out her shoes to produce quite a different effect.

The queen opted for a burgundy pantsuit from Spanish label Bleis, comprising a single-breasted blazer and straight-leg pants, worn over a pink tie-neck blouse. She brought out the pink further by layering on a punchy, fuchsia Carolina Herrera felt coat that has been a firm favorite in her wardrobe for years.

She accessorized with a burgundy JW Pei Noor top-handle bag, pointed-toe Magrit kitten heels in a matching shade of leather, and zigzagging diamond earrings by Bárbara Goenaga, neatly rounding out her royal ensemble.

Come lunchtime, Letizia had traded her pointed pumps for a pair of pale pink patent Sézane Paula Babies: a rounded toe, double strap, and sturdy block heel shoe. The French label’s retro-inflected Mary Janes have become something of a wardrobe staple for the royal, who owns them in no fewer than five colorways. Her morning’s footwear emphasized the sharpness of her tailoring, while the second pair brought a sweeter sensibility to suiting.

Burgundy and pink proved itself a popular color combo on the fall 2026 runways, particularly at Chanel under Matthieu Blazy, with wider shades of red and pink at Celine. Queen Letizia has long had a knack for making the season’s more adventurous color trends work within the parameters of her royal wardrobe, too. Earlier this month, on a visit to Campus Diversia, she teamed a pair of navy corduroy pants with chocolate-brown outerwear and sturdy boots. Over the summer, she embraced yellow in a gathered Adolfo Domínguez dress flecked with other pastel shades. And who could forget her coronation weekend wardrobe all the way back 2023, when she went from a zesty green Victoria Beckham dress to a head-to-toe Barbie pink ensemble?

What compounds the Spanish royal’s elegant sense of style is that so much of it is defined by pieces she’s had for years, reconfigured according to her schedule or the prevailing fashion mood. With a swift change of shoes or a refreshed color combo, you needn’t have the resources of a royal wardrobe to pull it off.

#6675. So, You Want to Tie Someone Up During Sex

Consider this your beginner’s guide to bondage, minus the complicated knots.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Being tied up during sex sounds pretty hot in theory, but the logistics of actually making it happen? Slightly less hot. What are you supposed to use? Where do your hands go? And how do you tell your partner you'd like to be temporarily relieved of all decision-making responsibilities in the bedroom without making it a whole thing?

Fortunately, experimenting with bondage doesn't have to involve a complicated rope setup or a trip to the hardware store (in fact, please skip the latter).

“You don’t need to rush to the most elaborate version of bondage to make it hot,” says Lisa Opel, the resident sex educator at MYLO.

Even the simplest forms of bondage, though, require a little more communication than your average hookup. Whether you're interested in doing the tying, being the one who's tied up, or taking turns, a little preparation goes a long way.

Below, sex experts break down how to tie someone up during sex, what to use (and what to leave in your junk drawer), and how to explore bondage safely without getting in over your head.

Bondage is a form of consensual play that involves restricting someone's movement. It's the “B” in BDSM, but you don't necessarily have to be into pain, punishment, or even dominance and submission to enjoy it.

“People enjoy rope play for all kinds of reasons: the feeling of surrender, the trust and power exchange, the anticipation, restriction, or simply because it feels meditative, pleasurable, or playful,” says Wildilisa Moronta, a relationship therapist and sex expert at Taimi.

For the person being restrained, part of the appeal might be relinquishing control or experiencing familiar sensations in a new way. For the person doing the restraining, it might be the excitement of taking charge or building anticipation for their partner. And for some people, the visual alone is a major turn-on.

Bondage can be as simple as wearing a pair of soft cuffs or as elaborate as practicing shibari, a form of rope bondage with Japanese roots. Some people even enjoy rope bondage as an artistic practice outside of sex, says Moronta.

Basically, there's no minimum level of kinkiness required to enjoy bondage. You can be perfectly content with a pair of quick-release cuffs and never feel the urge to pick up a rope.

You don't have to go from zero to a full-blown BDSM scene the first time you experiment with bondage. Both Moronta and Opel recommend keeping things simple and getting comfortable with the experience before introducing anything more complicated. Here's how to get started:

Before anyone breaks out the handcuffs, make sure you're working with the same definition of bondage. “One person might be imagining wrists in cuffs while kissing; the other might be picturing pain, control, or a much more intense scene,” says Opel.

Discuss who wants to be restrained, what kinds of touch you're comfortable with, and what your hard and soft limits are. You'll also want to talk about any injuries or joint issues that could make certain positions uncomfortable. Establish a safeword and discuss how you'd like to communicate throughout the experience.

You don't need anything particularly elaborate for your first experience with bondage. Opel recommends soft or padded cuffs with quick-release fastenings designed specifically for bondage, since they're easier to remove.

If you're specifically interested in rope bondage, both experts recommend taking a class with a qualified instructor before attempting any knots on a partner. Whatever restraints you choose, make sure you know how to remove them quickly before using them.

You can experiment with the feeling of bondage without actually tying anyone up. Moronta suggests loosely draping a scarf or rope over your partner's wrists while they're sitting or lying comfortably, without securing it or restricting their movement. It's a low-pressure way to figure out whether the sensation and dynamic appeal to you both.

If you're ready to experiment with actual restraints, Opel recommends starting with soft, quick-release cuffs around the wrists, keeping your partner's hands in front of their body rather than behind their back. There's no need to attempt complicated positions, especially ones that put unnecessary strain on the shoulders or joints. The experience should be about enjoying the sensation of restraint, not testing anyone's flexibility.

If you're the person doing the tying, you're responsible for more than setting the mood. You also need to pay attention to your partner's physical and emotional comfort and be prepared to release them immediately.

If you're the person being tied up, think about what appeals to you and what might make you uncomfortable. Maybe you love the idea of relinquishing control but get anxious when you can't move freely. Communicate those feelings to your partner, and speak up if something doesn't feel right.

Giving up control doesn't mean giving up your ability to make decisions. You're allowed to change your mind at any point, even if the experience was your idea in the first place.

Checking in doesn't have to take you out of the moment. “A quiet ‘How are you feeling?’ can help someone feel safe enough to relax and surrender,” says Moronta.

“The restrained person shouldn’t have to carry all the responsibility for saying when something is wrong,” adds Opel. Rather than relying on a safeword alone, ask specific questions about your partner's physical comfort and pay attention to changes in their behavior. If something seems off, pause and check in.

When you're finished, remove the restraints and spend some time checking in with each other. What that aftercare looks like will depend on what you both need.

“That might be a cuddle, water, a blanket, space, reassurance, or just a very normal conversation,” says Opel.

It's also worth talking about what you enjoyed, what you didn't, and whether you'd want to do anything differently next time. You might discover that you love bondage, that you prefer being restrained to doing the restraining (or vice versa), or that you'd rather leave it in the realm of fantasy.

As much fun as bondage can be, restricting someone's movement comes with very real physical risks. And just because a restraint looks comfortable doesn't necessarily mean it is.

“A common suggestion is to see whether you can slide a finger beneath a tie, but that doesn’t prove the tie is safe or rule out nerve pressure,” says Moronta.

Keep restraints away from the neck, avoid positions that restrict breathing, and be mindful of pressure on joints, nerves, and blood vessels. Both experts recommend leaving suspension, weight-bearing ties, and complicated rope work to people who've received proper training.

To keep things safe, both experts also recommend the traffic light system for safewords: Green means you're comfortable continuing, yellow means slow down or check in, and red means stop and release. If speaking might be difficult, establish a nonverbal signal beforehand that the restrained person can reliably make and their partner can easily recognize. If you can't establish a reliable way to communicate, don't restrict your partner's ability to speak or signal.

During play, Opel recommends asking specific questions, like whether anything feels too tight or whether your partner is experiencing any tingling or pain. Numbness, burning, weakness, dizziness, loss of movement, and difficulty breathing are all signs to stop and release the restraints immediately. Seek emergency medical help for difficulty breathing or other severe symptoms, and seek medical attention if symptoms persist after the restraints are removed.

You'll also want to stay sober, keep any necessary release tools (including safety shears if you're using rope) within reach, and never leave a restrained partner unattended, even for a minute.

If someone wants to stop for any reason, release them immediately. “Don’t persuade them to continue and don’t make them explain themselves while they’re still tied up,” says Opel.

Consent is ongoing, even after the cuffs go on, and agreeing to try bondage once doesn't mean either of you is obligated to do it again.

Not everything that can be used as a restraint should be. Ideally, you want something that's comfortable against the skin, won't tighten unexpectedly, and can be removed quickly.

“I’d choose purpose-made soft or padded cuffs, Velcro or other quick-release restraints, or bondage tape designed to stick to itself rather than skin,” says Opel. If you're shopping in person, take the opportunity to feel the materials, check the sizing, and familiarize yourself with how the restraints fasten and release.

If it's rope bondage you're interested in, Moronta notes that materials like jute, hemp, cotton, and nylon vary in texture and how they feel against the skin. Try handling a few varieties during a bondage workshop before investing in your own rope.

As for household items? Scarves, bathrobe belts, and neckties might look relatively harmless, but they can slip or tighten unexpectedly. The experts advise against using metal handcuffs, zip ties, thin cords, hardware-store rope, or anything else that's rigid or difficult to remove. Duct tape and electrical tape are also a no-go.

For more beginner-friendly options, check out our guide to the best bondage restraints. You don't need to invest in an entire collection of BDSM accessories to figure out whether bondage is your thing.

#6674. The Rise, Fall, and Future of Makeup Artist-Led Brands

When news broke in October that Isamaya Ffrench had severed ties with her namesake beauty brand, fans of the avant-garde makeup artist were saddened but not surprised. For months, there had been growing customer frustration about unfulfilled orders and unresponsive support. Buyers reported severe shipping delays, missing or damaged items, and unanswered refund requests across the brand’s Instagram and review sites like Trustpilot. By July, its social media accounts had stopped creating new posts, while inventory markdowns on its website were as high as 70%.

Ffrench responded to a customer complaint on Instagram in October by apologizing, adding that she was no longer associated with the brand. Shortly after, she thanked her followers on her personal Instagram during what she called “an incredibly difficult and emotional time”. The Isamaya Beauty brand did not respond to a request for comment.

Though Isamaya Beauty remains operational, speculation about its future has been rife. It follows the bankruptcy of fellow makeup artist Pat McGrath’s eponymous label earlier in January. Pat McGrath Labs famously hit a $1 billion valuation in 2018, a mere three years after launch, but struggled to scale and keep pace with modern beauty demands. It exited the Chapter 11 bankruptcy process in April under new majority owner GDA Luma, a Miami-based investment firm. McGrath herself stepped down as CEO and transferred her equity to the holding company while staying on as chief creative officer.

While Ffrench and McGrath remain highly respected creative forces (McGrath is the creative director of cosmetics for La Beauté Louis Vuitton, while Ffrench currently runs the interdisciplinary concept store and gallery Studio Iron), the hurdles faced by their namesake labels highlight the structural friction between avant-garde artistry and commercial scaling. Their trajectories also signal a broader reckoning for makeup artist (MUA) brands, which have become a dominant yet deeply volatile segment of the beauty market, characterized by rapid boom-and-bust cycles.

The late 2010s marked an unprecedented period of rapid industry growth. Social media, viral launches, and Sephora shelf space helped drive hype for makeup artist-led brands, several of which subsequently enjoyed massive funding and bloated valuations. Many, however, struggled with post-pandemic relevance and debt. Today, the market has become saturated, and shoppers are increasingly fatigued by frequent drops and buzzwords. Consumer preferences are also evolving, with Gen Z opting for customizable, skin-first routines while millennials desire substantive education beyond surface-level trends.

“On the back of the success of brands like Charlotte Tilbury, which grew and ultimately sold to a strategic buyer, more people became interested in putting in capital behind the next generation [of MUAs],” says Ilya Seglin, managing director of consumer, retail and e-commerce at Cascadia Capital, citing Patrick Ta, Makeup by Mario, Danessa Myricks Beauty and Westman Atelier. However, many of these brands have not been acquired. Their founders have spoken publicly about choosing to stay independent and fund long-term growth, but at the same time, beauty strategic investors have pulled back on makeup deals.

Charlotte Tilbury at the opening of her Covent Garden, London flagship in 2025.

As Seglin explains, “Strategics have no idea how to get comfortable with color [cosmetics]”, which is increasingly viewed by investors as a “fashion” category — in other words, a volatile, trend-driven sector that is high risk but high reward. Both fashion and MUA-founded labels share the challenges of maintaining quarterly growth while mitigating trend fatigue and inventory risk. Beauty trend cycles, in particular, have accelerated with the advent of online algorithms and short-form content that create rapid microtrends that can rise, peak, and fade within weeks or months, rather than years. “The perceived risk profile of color brands has increased, so strategics have become more cautious. They don’t know if this is like a fashion brand that’s going to be around for 5-10 years, or whether it’s going to be around for longer. Strategics want to acquire brands that are going to be around forever.”

Despite the risks, makeup artist brands remain attractive to customers, accounting for roughly 30% of the prestige beauty market in the US, according to Larissa Jensen, senior vice president and global beauty industry advisor at Circana. “Makeup artist brands are the largest brand type within the makeup category. Collectively, they’re growing about 7%, which is outpacing total makeup growth,” she explains. “The units are actually performing, up 5%, compared to makeup overall, which was flat. That’s an indicator of demand.”

Still, MUAs have come a long way since early pioneers Bobbi Brown (1991), François Nars (1994) and Laura Mercier (1996) disrupted traditional department-store beauty, bringing their backstage and editorial expertise to everyday consumers via wearable neutrals and face base products. The subsequent rise of Instagram and YouTube transformed makeup artists like Charlotte Tilbury into global personalities. By the late 2010s, A-list celebrities and influencers (think Kylie Cosmetics by Kylie Jenner, Fenty Beauty by Rihanna and Rare Beauty by Selena Gomez) flooded the market. Meanwhile, contract manufacturing enabled independent, founder-led brands (like Glossier and Morphe) and beauty vloggers with massive, highly trusting audiences (such as Huda Kattan and Jaclyn Hill) to launch quickly and market directly to consumers. Built-in audiences and excited VCs temporarily overshadowed traditional artist-founded lines.

Bobbi Brown applying makeup on model backstage at Tory Burch SS07.

As consumers grow weary of celebrity white-label lines, credibility is shifting back to working makeup artists. Mary Phillips (M.ph Beauty) and Mario Dedivanovic (Makeup by Mario), for instance, regularly share behind-the-scenes details, signature techniques and product breakdowns from their red carpet prep with the likes of the Kardashian-Jenners. Both artists have long been part of the family’s glam squad and turned their routines into global beauty trends and brands.

Meanwhile, some modern founders didn’t come from traditional channels like celebrity glam or print magazines, but can demonstrate why their formulas deserve permanent space in one’s bag. Jamie Genevieve, for example, built an online following after working on makeup counters in Scotland. Her beauty brand Vieve celebrates its sixth anniversary this month. Sales have grown from £6.5 million in 2024 to £9.3 million by late 2026. Meanwhile, Jordana Ticia’s eponymous label has become one of the UK’s biggest indie beauty success stories in recent years, marked by a 239% increase in TikTok Shop GMV in 2024 (two years after joining the platform) and a May 2026 launch into Space NK where first-week sales beat forecasts, according to the Manchester-based founder.

Many of these modern MUA brands are actively pushing back against traditional “haul” culture by highlighting durability, multi-season versatility and cost-per-wear. Rather than lean heavily on novelty collaborations or viral packaging, the new generation is more focused on approachable, everyday beauty staples that can become high-repeat categories.

Violette, founded in 2021 by the Parisian makeup artist Violette Serrat, has leaned into French attitudes to beauty: to age with ease and feel comfortable in one’s own skin, and to treat skincare and fragrance like food or wine — something to savor and enjoy. Instead of seeking to mask or fix perceived flaws, Serrat — who has amassed over a million followers collectively across her personal Instagram and YouTube accounts — encourages natural-looking, lived-in looks. Her routines often show her applying products with her fingers rather than complex tools.

Violette is inspired by makeup artist Violette Serrat's love for fine art and French beauty philosophy.

Violette’s go-to market strategy was not to launch in Sephora or an equivalent retailer. Instead, the brand launched in locations like Oh My Cream, a “clean beauty” concept store, and French pharmacies, which in her view offered a real “stamp of approval” given their penchant for products that are simple, consistent and credible. Violette’s top sellers include the Bisou Balm (a lipstick-balm hybrid that creates a soft, blurred look) and Boum Boum Milk (a 3-in-1 skincare spray combining toner, serum and moisturizer).

Serrat’s belief is that while brands don’t have to speak to everyone, their products need to be universally understood. “I think it’s ok to be a little bit more niche. But my mission as a makeup artist is to simplify my technique,” explains Serrat, whose makeup has appeared editorially in American and French Vogue, and on the red carpet via figures such as Léa Seydoux and Natalia Vodianova. “If you look at my work, it’s very complex makeup. But for my brand, I want to make sure my products are understood on an instinctual level. You just put your finger in and do it.”

The British makeup artist Katie Jane Hughes, who counts a total of over two million followers across TikTok and Instagram, also bridges playful artistry with accessible, skin-first education. Rather than pushing rigid beauty rules or complicated tutorials, Hughes offers practical breakdowns and techniques that let natural skin texture show through. After years of working with high-profile clients such as Emmy Rossum, Kerry Washington, and Dua Lipa, among others, Hughes — who is based in New York — founded her beauty brand KJH in 2023, selling a tight lineup of accessibly priced items including the Precision Sculpture Stylo (a sculpting liner for the lips or eyes) and Microscara (a mascara with a petite brush built for better control). Sales have grown 250% year on year.

Core to KJH is its ability to reach a wide demographic of people. A poll conducted independently by KJH found that only 10% of the brand’s customers are makeup artists or fellow creatives. “Most of the people are not professionals. So I wanted to create products that allow them to feel like they’re more of an expert with what’s in their makeup bag,” says Hughes. As of August, the brand has expanded from DTC into Sephora in the US and Canada. Asked whether it’s a pivot in strategy, Hughes explains that cosmetics are deeply sensory products that rely on in-person testing and trust. “I like playing with a product in person before buying it. I think that’s really important.”

British makeup artist Katie Jane Hughes has won over a loyal audience for her approachable tutorials, signature glowing aesthetic and rule-breaking attitude toward beauty.

Built on technical skill and real-world experience, makeup artist-founded brands continue to stand out from celebrity or creator cash-grab lines. More are also expected to come to market, with the next highly anticipated launch to potentially come from celebrity makeup artist Nina Park, if early trademark filings are any indication. However, as Circana’s Jensen warns, “how many of [those MUA labels] really blow up and become huge or mainstays remains to be seen.”

Indeed, Violette, which is not yet profitable, raised $5 million from three existing investors in May, extending the Series B round that originally closed in 2024. It’s significantly smaller than the earlier sky-high multiples some MUA labels have previously commanded. Nonetheless, the recent funding indicates investor optimism and wider appetite for Violette, which Serrat is confident can reach profitability in the short term. The brand has entered new doors, including Sephora in the US as of 2025 and the UK this November. According to Serrat, sales at Sephora have doubled year over year, and the brand has since been “fast-tracked” to roll out in 800 doors, including in Canada, up from 80.

Taking on investment means toeing the line between creativity and commerciality. “If you’re niche, you cannot have investors,” she says, matter-of-factly. “You can do a super niche and creative brand, but you have to be self-funded. For me, raising money made me anxious. I needed to make sure I was able to have a very healthy business. You can create a brand that is inspiring, but at the same time, you need to think of the bottom line. It’s my obsession. I want to be profitable as soon as I can.”

It’s for these reasons KJH is primarily self-funded and has operated without traditional outside venture capital or institutional funding rounds since its launch. Hughes stressed the need to work at her own pace, despite ongoing interest from incubators and VCs alike. “I didn’t want to be tied to anybody. I didn’t want to feel like I had this big company above me telling me what I should do next,” she explains. “Now, we have more of a steady flow of sales, allowing us to have more launches throughout the year. We’ll probably release two to three products a year versus just one. But it’s because I want to do it. I want to do what’s right for me and my community.”

Hughes's beauty brand KJH focuses on multi-use formulas and practical makeup education.

However, experts also say that more than ever, consumers are looking to credible industry leaders and specialized experts to cut through the noise and AI-driven information overload. “For makeup artists, it’s really about bringing forth their expertise. There’s an authenticity that comes with their brands,” says Jensen. “Within the hair category, salon brands are the largest. Within skincare, it’s clinical brands founded by doctors or cosmetic chemists. What that collectively says about the consumer is that they are looking for expertise in their categories.”

Seglin adds that investor sentiment — like consumer trends — is cyclical. And the current funding constraints may, in fact, be beneficial for both the market and MUAs. “It forces them to actually be good. They will probably be better businesses than the brands that were flooded with investor cash before.” Those eager for a payout need to “be realistic about market dynamics”, he says. “If your expectation is to raise $10 million to launch a brand and in three years you’re going to sell to L’Oréal, I would temper those aspirations.”

#6668. 21 Useful Travel Gifts for Frequent Fliers

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Pour one out for those who have to travel during the holidays. If I want to see all the family I care about in one holiday season (and I rarely have the time or extra money too), I have to travel to Pittsburgh, Hawaii, and California. Travel is expensive, time-consuming, and with all the FAA outages this year, it has become especially anxiety-inducing as of late. So if you have a loved one who has to take a few planes between now and the end of the year, you may as well splurge and get them an excellent gift. You know, one they can actually use.

Instead, go for the pieces they can style for everyday wear like a thermal layer or some comfy shoes. Or just a pair of shiny new headphones. And remember, he will never say no to a new suitcase.

#6664. How to Read the ‘Percy Jackson’ Books in Order of Release

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

With the new third season of Percy Jackson and the Olympians on Disney+, this is a great time to dive into Rick Riordan’s books, whether you’re reading them for the first time, rereading them, or picking up where you left off once upon a time. In true camp fashion, here’s your itinerary for reading all of the Percy Jackson books in order. (Or the Camp Half-Blood Chronicles, if you’d prefer to call them that.)

In the world that Riordan has created, demigods roam the Earth in modern times. For the most part, they’re indistinguishable from regular kids. Some of them, like Percy Jackson himself, don’t even know that their parents are literally figures from Greek and Roman mythology. Since there are so many of those centuries-old tales, Riordan has been able to turn them into a ton of young adult adventures.

In the first book, Percy Jackson learns that he’s a demigod and enrolls at Camp Half-Blood. He meets Annabeth Chase and Grover Underwood, and the three of them go on a quest to find Zeus’ lightning bolt.

This time, the teenage demigods go looking for the Golden Fleece. They’re joined by Tyson, Percy’s brother who happens to be a cyclops.

In the third book, which happens to be the basis for the current season of the Disney+ series, the group expands and they go on a rescue mission. For who exactly? You’re going to have to read to find out!

Camp Half-Blood is under attack (again), and our heroes enter the mythical Labyrinth to save it from invading forces.

In the (original) grand finale, the fight moves from Camp Half-Blood to Manhattan.

Fifteen years later, Riordan returned to his original hero with a duology about Percy’s last year in school. This means quests and college applications.

The final (unless there’s more) book is set at Halloween as he pet sits for Hecate. Should be easy!

But wait, there’s more! Riordan has dozens of other stories, long and short, in this universe. Here’s enough to get you started:

This is a series about a group of seven demigods who embark on a quest together.

This series is, like it says on the tin, about Apollo.

Nico also appears in Heroes of Olympus. This continues his story.

This series is set in the same universe, but all about Egyptian mythology.

These short stories are Percy Jackson and Kane sibling crossovers.

This trilogy is about Annabeth’s cousin and his dealings in Norse mythology.

The most recent book, co-written by Annabelle Oh, has a female protagonist: Katie Kim, daughter of Ares. It will get a sequel called The Rite of Aphrodite in March 2027.
